2. **(Complete) Kernel support:** the console as descriptors 0, 1 and 2; syscall errors mapped to `errno` values; `getpid`/`getppid`; a `Stat` syscall; and an environment block passed by `Spawn` (`environ`, `getenv`, `setenv`).
3. **(Complete) Memory:** `malloc`/`free`/`calloc`/`realloc` as a free-list allocator over `ExtendHeap`.
4. **(Complete) stdio:** the `printf` family built on one `vsnprintf`, and buffered `FILE*` streams (`stdin`/`stdout`/`stderr`, `fopen`, `fread`/`fwrite`, `fgets`/`fputs`, `fflush`, `fclose`). The console is line-buffered.
5. **(Complete) POSIX calls:**
   - the rest of `unistd.h` (`lseek`, `unlink`, `rmdir`, `chdir`, `getcwd`, `sleep`), `fcntl.h` (`open` flags), `mkdir` in `sys/stat.h`, and `dirent.h`
   - `time.h`: `time`, `gmtime`, `strftime`, `clock_gettime`, `nanosleep`
   - `spawn.h` (`posix_spawn`) with `sys/wait.h` (`waitpid`, `WNOHANG`, `WIFEXITED`)
   - `scanf`, `fscanf`, `sscanf` and their `v` forms: integers with the same length modifiers as `printf`, `%s`, `%c`, scansets (`%[...]`), `%n`, assignment suppression (`%*d`), and field widths, sharing one header-only engine with host tests against the host's `sscanf`
6. **(Complete) Floating point:** save FPU/SIMD state per task on both architectures and drop `-mgeneral-regs-only`. Then add the floating-point conversions to `printf` and `scanf`, `strtod`/`atof`, and a small `math.h`.
7. **(Complete) Adoption:**
   - Move every program in `src/user/` onto libc and retire `runtime.h`.
   - Add a `libctest` conformance program to the matrix.
   - Document the subset in `docs/posix.md`: each function, how it differs from POSIX, and what is left out on purpose.

**Step 7 summary:**
- **Programs:** every program in `src/user/` now starts at `main` and uses the C library, and `runtime.h` is gone. The one exception is the kernel self-test `test_program`, which reads the raw arguments block. `src/user/command_line.h` holds the shared `-h`/`--help` check, a strict number parser, and the shell's command-line splitter. A host test checks that a split line reaches the program as the same words after `posix_spawn` and the kernel.
- **Syscalls still used directly:** `filetest` and `hardening` call `syscall.h` where they test the kernel's exact errors. The `primes` workers do too, because they return 64-bit results through their exit status.
- **New in the C library**, so programs need no raw syscalls:
  - `posix_spawnattr_setflags`, where `POSIX_SPAWN_SETSID` makes a session leader and `POSIX_SPAWN_SETPGROUP` starts a background child; init uses both
  - `sbrk`, `sched_getcpu`, and `reboot`
  - `<featheros.h>`: `featheros_read_line`, a prompted line read that redraws the prompt after Ctrl+C, and `featheros_arguments_page`
- **Fixes:** AArch64 `setjmp`/`longjmp` now save `d8`–`d15`, which step 6 had left out. `libctest` (278 checks) catches its absence.
- **Docs:** [posix.md](posix.md) documents every header and function, how each differs from POSIX, and what is left out on purpose.
- **Image size:** programs are larger with the C library linked in, about 20 pages at `-O0`, so `memtest exhaust` reaches about 1000 heap pages instead of 1020.

**Step 6 summary:**
- **Kernel:** the x86-64 kernel is built with `-mgeneral-regs-only`, so the kernel never uses FP/SIMD registers on either architecture. The scheduler saves and loads them on each context switch: FXSAVE on x86-64, q0–q31 with FPCR/FPSR on AArch64. Each CPU enables them at boot, and x86-64 programs start with the ABI's stack alignment. User programs drop `-mgeneral-regs-only` and `+nofp+nosimd`. `fptest` fills every vector register in 8 workers and fails if a switch loses them; with saving disabled, it does fail.
- **Conversions:** `printf` gains `%f %e %g %a` (and `L`), exact via big integers and matching the host's `printf` digit for digit. `strtod`/`strtof`/`strtold`/`atof` are correctly rounded, and `scanf` reads floats.
- **`math.h`:** the exact functions match the host bit for bit. `exp`, `log`, `pow`, the trigonometric and hyperbolic functions, and `hypot` are within 1–3 ulps of the host's `libm`, and set `errno`.
- **Tests:** `libctest` (264 checks) checks all of this on both architectures, including x87 and 128-bit `long double` output.

**Step 5 summary:**
- **Files:** `open`/`creat` with Linux flag values, where `O_APPEND` puts every write at the end. Also `lseek` (the kernel's `Seek` now takes an origin and returns the new offset), `unlink`, `mkdir`, `rmdir`, `chdir`, `getcwd`, `access`, `isatty`, and `dirent.h`.
- **Time:** `time.h`, built on the shared `calendar.h`, with UTC conversions, `mktime` normalization, a C-locale `strftime`, `clock_gettime` and `nanosleep`, plus `sleep`/`usleep`.
- **Processes:** `posix_spawn`/`posix_spawnp` turn `argv` and `envp` into the kernel's command line and environment block. `waitpid`/`wait` use the Linux status encoding, and `-1` polls the spawned children.
- **Scanning:** `scanf`, `fscanf` and `sscanf` share one header-only engine.
- **Tests:** host tests compare `scanf` and `strftime` with the host's own and cover the spawn requests. `libctest` (240 checks) exercises all of it on both architectures, including `scanf(stdin)` and spawning `echo`, `env`, a failing child and a faulting one.

**Step 4 summary:** One header-only `printf` engine writes through a sink: a stream, a buffer, or a descriptor. It handles the integer, character, string and pointer conversions with every flag, width, precision and length modifier, and host tests compare it with the host's `snprintf`. `FILE*` streams have 512-byte buffers: stdout is line-buffered, stderr unbuffered, and a console read flushes stdout first. They cover `fopen` modes, `fread`/`fwrite`, `fgets`, `getline`, `ungetc`, `fseek`/`ftell`, append mode, and switching between reading and writing. `exit` flushes all streams last. `assert.h`, `perror` and `remove` are also added. `libctest` (187 checks) now prints through stdio, reads a line with `fgets(stdin)`, round-trips a disk file, and checks that a failed `assert` aborts. Not yet: floating-point conversions (step 6) and `scanf` (added in step 5).

**Step 3 summary:** `malloc`, `calloc`, `realloc` and `free` use a first-fit allocator with address-ordered free lists. Free neighbors merge on both sides, `realloc` grows in place when it can, and the heap grows in 64 KiB steps by only the shortfall. Invalid and double frees are caught. C++ `new`/`delete` and `strdup`/`strndup` build on it. `ExtendHeap` now checks the page limit before mapping, so a failed request no longer leaves pages mapped that later growth would trip over. Host tests cover the allocator over an in-memory arena; `libctest` (152 checks) covers allocation, `new`/`delete`, reuse, and growing after a failed request.

**Step 2 summary:** Descriptors 0–2 are the console in every process: writes print as one unit, and reads return a line at a time (foreground only), keeping what does not fit for the next read. Syscall errors now name their cause (bad descriptor or address, read-only, exists, not a directory, not empty, and others), with the error range widened to 4095 values as on Linux, and the C library maps each to `errno`. New syscalls are `Stat`, `StatDescriptor`, `ProcessId` and `ParentProcessId`. `Spawn` takes an environment block that the kernel validates and maps with the arguments. init sets variables from `env` lines in `init.conf`, and the shell passes its environment on. The C library adds `read`/`write`/`close`, `getpid`/`getppid`, `stat`/`fstat`, `environ`, `getenv`/`setenv`/`unsetenv`, and a new `env` program uses only the C library. Unit tests cover the error codes, `Stat`, environment validation, `env` lines, and the environment table; `libctest` grows to 104 checks, including a console read.

**Step 1 summary:** `src/libc/` builds as `libfeatheros_c.a` in CMake, which now also builds the user programs into `build/<arch>/programs`. A program with a `main` links the C library's `_start`, which copies `argv` to writable memory, runs constructors and calls `exit(main(...))`. Also included: `string.h`, `ctype.h`, `errno.h`, `setjmp.h` in assembly for each architecture, the integer parts of `stdlib.h`, and the C++ runtime hooks. Host unit tests cover the `strtol` parser and heapsort, and the shell test runs `libctest` (75 checks plus exit-handler order) on both architectures.

**Follow-up (complete): returning freed heap memory.** `ExtendHeap` takes a negative increment: it unmaps the whole pages above the new break, frees their frames, and lowers the page count, through a new `UnmapUserPage` on both architectures. `sbrk` passes negative increments through. `malloc` gives back all but a 64 KiB cushion, in 64 KiB steps, when more than 256 KiB is free at the end of the heap, and only while the break is still where its memory ends.
- **Tests:**
  - Host tests cover the release, its threshold and cushion, refusing when the end has moved, and a randomized run while shrinking.
  - `memtest exhaust` now grows to the page limit, shrinks back, and must grow as far again.
  - `hardening` checks that a page the heap gave back faults, and that the heap cannot shrink below its start (48 checks).
  - `libctest` (282 checks) checks that `free` returns memory and leaves a program's own `sbrk` memory alone.
- **Still open:** only the top of the heap can be returned. `mmap`/`munmap` (in [ideas.md](ideas.md) under Scalability), with large allocations in their own mappings, would return any freed block at once.

Out of scope for Part 3:
- `fork`/`exec`: `posix_spawn` covers process creation until copy-on-write exists.
- signals and pipes: covered by the later bullet.
- pthreads, sockets, users and permissions, `mmap`, `termios`, locales, and wide characters.

**Complete when:** init starts and supervises basic services, and users can run a documented set of utilities and programs through the shell on both targets, including programs installed on the disk.

### 12. Security And Hardening (Complete)

**Part 1 (complete):** The security invariants are documented in `docs/development.md`. The loader checks that segments lie in user space, AArch64 user pages are privileged-execute-never, and each process may have at most 16 live children. The shell test's `hardening` program runs 44 negative checks: bad syscall pointers and arguments, a kernel-half ELF, heap execution, code writes, kernel reads, an invalid syscall, and the child limit.

**Part 2 (complete):** AArch64 device registers (UART, RTC, GIC, virtio-mmio) are mapped in the kernel half through `Memory::MapDevice`, as is the x86-64 local APIC, and AArch64 user page tables no longer copy the kernel's low `TTBR0` mappings. The memory test maps a user page at the UART's identity address to prove the slot is free.

**Part 3 (complete):** Spawned processes get a randomized stack, arguments page, and heap start, using a cycle-counter-seeded generator. The shell test runs `aslr` twice and requires different addresses. AArch64 user space now ends at `2^47` to match its canonical-address check.

**Part 4 (complete):** Spawned programs load as static PIEs at a random image base (see M13). The kernel enables x86-64 SMEP/SMAP and AArch64 PAN when the CPU has them; the loader's copies into user memory open a short user-access window, and interrupt entry closes it again. `RDRAND`/`RNDR` are mixed into the random source when present. Matrix cases run the shell session on QEMU's `max` CPU and require these protections. Moved: the open-file limit to M9, with file syscalls.

- Audit syscall argument validation, ELF loading, address-space isolation, and resource cleanup.
- Enforce writable/executable memory policy and tighten privilege transitions.
- Add negative tests for invalid syscalls, malformed binaries, and attempts to access protected memory.
- Remove kernel low-address mappings (such as the UART identity map) from AArch64 user roots by moving them to the higher half.
- Add user stack guard pages, per-process resource limits (pages, tasks, open files), and address-space layout randomization. Randomizing the program image depends on PIE support in M13.

**Complete when:** security invariants are documented and automated tests demonstrate that malformed inputs and user programs cannot access or corrupt protected kernel state.

### 13. Memory Management And Process Scalability (Complete)

- Replace fixed limits with dynamic ones: a growable task table, heap-allocated kernel stacks, and a per-process page limit instead of the fixed ELF page cap.
- Give user programs a growable stack, a heap (`brk` or `mmap`-style syscall), and optional demand paging with copy-on-write.
- Extend the ELF loader beyond static executables: PIE/relocations (which also enables image ASLR for M12), `PT_TLS`, and a documented policy for dynamic linking.
- Add scheduler policy beyond round-robin, such as priorities; blocked/sleeping states, timed sleep, and an idle task already exist.

**Part 1 (complete):** Tasks and process records are heap-allocated. Kernel stacks come from a guard-paged pool, and a per-process page limit replaces the fixed ELF page cap. QEMU tests run 12 concurrent tasks for three rounds without leaking frames, check that a guard page faults, and have the shell's `fanout 6` run six children at once.

**Part 2 (complete):** User stacks grow on demand through a 64-page region whose lowest page is a guard, and the `ExtendHeap` syscall grows a program break above the ELF image. The shell test's `memtest` checks deep recursion and heap reads and writes, and `memtest overflow` is terminated at the guard page.

**Part 3 (complete):** Tasks have Low, Normal, or High priority, and the scheduler runs the highest-priority ready task, round-robin within a level. The `Sleep` syscall blocks a program without using a CPU. A scheduler test checks priority order, and the shell test runs `sleep 1100`.

**Part 4 (complete):** User programs are built as static PIEs. The ELF loader accepts `ET_DYN`, applies `R_X86_64_RELATIVE`/`R_AARCH64_RELATIVE` relocations before setting final permissions, and rejects symbol, PLT, and text relocations; unit tests cover each case. `memtest exhaust` checks that hitting the page limit fails cleanly and that pages are reclaimed. The dynamic-linking policy is documented in `docs/development.md`. Moved: `PT_TLS` and copy-on-write to M11.

**Complete when:** more than four processes can run with independently growing memory on both targets, sleeping tasks do not consume CPU, and QEMU tests cover allocation failure and resource reclamation under load.

### 14. USB Host And HID Input

- Add xHCI host-controller support and enumerate USB devices on both architectures.
- Implement USB HID keyboard reports and route key events through the shared input queue from M4.
- Add QEMU xHCI keyboard devices to both machine configurations for repeatable tests.

**Complete when:** both targets enumerate a QEMU USB keyboard and deliver key events through the shared input path.

### 15. Terminal Layout And Status Bar (Complete)

**Summary:** The framebuffer terminal has a status bar on its top row. It shows the foreground program on the left and, on the right, busy/online CPUs and the UTC time. Busy CPUs are counted from scheduler timer ticks. The prompt (`> `) and input are edited inline with a cursor. The `ReadUptime` syscall backs a new `uptime` command, and the once-a-second uptime log is gone. Unit tests cover the bar layout, inline erase across wraps, incremental-versus-full rendering, and the short date format. The SMP test checks busy-CPU accounting, and the shell test runs `uptime`. Follow-ups: `calc` evaluates full expressions with exact fractions, interactively at a `calc> ` prompt or from its arguments (the argument limit rose to 16). The `primes <workers>` demo splits work across processes and reports each worker's CPUs through the new `CurrentCpu` syscall. Spawned programs are now time-sliced on every timer tick on both architectures, so a CPU-bound program no longer keeps its core until it blocks. Ctrl+C interrupts the shell's foreground job and its descendants, and children are terminated and reaped when their parent exits.

- Add a one-row status bar at the top of the framebuffer terminal, above the scrolling text.
- Show the foreground program's name on the left of the bar, and on the right the busy CPU count over the online CPU count (for example `2/4`) followed by the current RTC date and time as `Mon Oct 19 9:36 PM`.
- Remove the dedicated input row at the bottom: the prompt and the line being typed appear inline in the scrolling text, like a normal terminal, with Backspace editing in place.
- Change the shell prompt to `> `.
- Remove the on-screen uptime and add an `uptime` command that prints it, backed by a new syscall that reports time since boot.
- Stop the once-a-second uptime log on the terminal and serial port; the smoke test checks the `uptime` command instead.

**Complete when:** on both targets the bar shows the right program, CPU count, and time as programs start and exit, input is edited inline, and QEMU tests cover the `uptime` command and the new prompt.

### 16. Physical Storage Drivers

- Add NVMe first, since its paired submission and completion queues are close to the virtio-blk driver, then AHCI for SATA disks (moved from M9).
- Develop and test each driver on QEMU's emulated controllers (`-device nvme`, `-device ahci`) with the same matrix tests as virtio-blk, before trying real hardware.
- Share one block-device interface so the filesystem does not depend on the controller.
- Later, SD/eMMC for ARM boards. USB mass storage is M19 Part 2.

**Complete when:** both targets mount and write the FAT test disk through NVMe and AHCI in QEMU, passing the same storage tests as virtio-blk.

### 17. Native Toolchain

A compiler toolchain written from scratch that runs on FeatherOS. Porting GCC or Clang is out of scope, both because the project uses no outside code and because of their size. Each tool is written in C++, developed and unit-tested on the host first, and then built as a FeatherOS program. Needs: the M11 C library, running programs from disk, per-process memory limits well above 4 MiB, and a way to edit source files.

- **Stage 1: Assembler and linker.**
  - Assemble x86-64 and AArch64 text assembly into object files.
  - Link them into static-PIE ELF programs that the existing loader accepts.
- **Stage 2: C compiler.**
  - Cover a C11 subset, with one code generator per architecture.
  - It should build the libc and simple tools on FeatherOS.
- **Stage 3: C++ subset.** Namespaces, classes, references, overloading, `constexpr`, and simple templates: the subset the FeatherOS code style uses. It should build the user programs in `src/user/`. Full C++ (general templates, exceptions, RTTI, newer standards) is not a goal.
- **Stretch:** compile the toolchain itself (self-hosting), then the kernel.

**Complete when:** on both targets, FeatherOS compiles a C++ user program from source on its own disk and runs the result, and the matrix tests this.

**Open decision: port TCC first.** Not decided yet. An alternative is to start with a port of TCC (Tiny C Compiler), an exception to the no-outside-code rule, and keep the stages above as a longer-term goal that TCC can be compared against.

- **Why TCC:** it is small (about 1 MB), has x86-64 and AArch64 backends, writes ELF, and needs little beyond stdio, `malloc` and files. GCC and Clang need `mmap`, hundreds of MB per process and much of POSIX, and there is no small C++ compiler.
- **Work:** build TCC against the FeatherOS libc and close the gaps it finds; make its output something the loader accepts (static-PIE, or teach the loader `ET_EXEC`); build its `libtcc1` runtime; and install the libc headers and objects on the disk.
- **Gives:** a C compiler on FeatherOS on both targets, but no C++. The from-scratch C++ subset would still be needed for Stage 3.
- **Complete when (for this part):** on both targets, TCC on FeatherOS compiles a C program from the disk and runs it, and the matrix tests this.

### 18. Scripting Languages

Interpreters written from scratch, like the M17 toolchain.

- **Part 1: Shell scripts.** Extend the shell into an interpreter for a documented subset of POSIX `sh`, not full Bash:
  - variables, quoting, `$?`
  - `if`/`for`/`while`/`case`, functions
  - `$(...)` command substitution, `#!` script files
  - Builds on the M11 pipes, signals and background jobs.
- **Part 2: Python subset.**
  - A bytecode interpreter for a documented core: integers, floats, strings, lists, dicts, functions, classes, exceptions, and a small built-in library (`print`, file I/O, `os`-style calls over the M11 C library).
  - Memory is managed by reference counting with a cycle collector, or by a tracing garbage collector.
  - Needs floating point from M11 Part 3.

**Complete when:** on both targets, the matrix runs a shell script and a Python script from the disk and checks their output.

### 19. Removable Drives And Foreign Filesystems

The goal is to plug in a USB drive formatted as FAT, exFAT, ext4, NTFS or APFS, and read and write it. The mount table, the filesystem driver interface and ext2/ext4 come from M22. The milestone is split in two because only the second half needs USB: the filesystems need just a block device, so they are built and tested on a second virtio-blk disk and then work unchanged on USB drives behind the same block-device interface.

- **Part 1: foreign filesystems** (after M22, no USB). Each driver lands in the shared filesystem core (`src/fs/core/`, M22) first, like ext2/ext4, with unit tests over image files there. QEMU gets a second virtio-blk disk holding one partition per filesystem, built on the host. Partitions on it are mounted with `mount` under `/media/<label>` and unmounted with `umount`, which flushes everything first.
- **Part 2: removable USB drives** (after M14). M14 plans only HID keyboards over interrupt transfers, so this part adds xHCI bulk transfers, then USB mass storage: the Bulk-Only Transport protocol carrying SCSI commands, with UAS later. Hot-plug detects the partitions on a new drive (the GPT/MBR code exists) and mounts each one at `/media/<label>`, and `eject` unmounts every partition of a drive so pulling it out afterwards is safe.

**Safety rules:** a filesystem bug here corrupts a real drive, so:
- Mount new filesystem drivers read-only until their write path passes the matrix.
- Mount read-only whenever the volume reports it was not cleanly unmounted:
  - an ext4 journal that needs recovery, until we replay the journal ourselves
  - NTFS left hibernated by Windows "Fast Startup", or flagged dirty
  - an APFS container whose latest checkpoint is not valid
- After the OS writes to a test image, check it on the host with that filesystem's own repair tool. This extends the `STORAGE_TEST` pattern, which already cross-checks FAT with mtools.

| Filesystem | Specification | Write difficulty | Host tools for test images and checks |
|---|---|---|---|
| FAT32 | Microsoft FAT specification | Low: extends the core's FAT driver (M22 Part 2) | `mkfs.fat`, `fsck.fat`, mtools |
| exFAT | Microsoft's published exFAT specification | Low to medium: allocation bitmap, checksummed directory entry sets, UTF-16 names. The usual format of large USB drives | `mkfs.exfat`, `fsck.exfat` |
| NTFS | Reverse-engineered documentation (Linux ntfs3 and NTFS-3G docs) | High: MFT records and attributes, data runs, B+ tree directory indexes, `$Bitmap`, update sequence arrays. Mark the volume dirty while mounted. Compressed and encrypted files stay read-only | `mkntfs`, `ntfsfix -n` (NTFS-3G) |
| APFS | Apple's "Apple File System Reference" | Highest: copy-on-write B-trees, object map, space manager, checkpoints and transaction IDs, Fletcher-64 checksums. FileVault-encrypted volumes also need AES-XTS and the keybag | `hdiutil create -fs APFS`, `fsck_apfs -n` on macOS |

ext4 drives work through the M22 driver once Part 2 is in place.

**Order:** FAT32 → exFAT → NTFS → APFS. Each filesystem is read-only first, then gets writes.

**Complete when:**
- Part 1: on both targets, each filesystem on the second virtio-blk disk is mounted under `/media`, written and unmounted, and the host repair tool finds no errors.
- Part 2: on both targets, a QEMU USB drive (`-device usb-storage` on the xHCI controller) is detected when it is attached, its partitions are mounted under `/media`, written and cleanly ejected, and the host repair tool finds no errors.

### 20. C++23 And Sanitizers (Complete)

**Summary:** The kernel, libc, programs and host tests all build as `gnu++23`. The host tests run under AddressSanitizer and UndefinedBehaviorSanitizer, which found one font bug. Every syscall handler table uses designated initializers, and the global locks and wait queues are `constinit`. Along the way, the AArch64 shell smoke test's intermittent failure turned out not to be a guest hang. The scripted session had grown to about 90 s on AArch64, which was its whole time limit. The limit is now 180 s, and a failure that stopped at the limit says so.

Scheduled right after M11, before the next feature milestone, so later work starts on a stricter base. Each part lands on its own, with the full matrix on both targets.

**Part 1 (complete):** The kernel, libc, programs and host tests all build as `gnu++23` (CMake 3.20 or newer). Besides the six warnings from the trial build, four more `++` on a `volatile` sat in `kernel_test.cpp` code that only builds with a test toggle on. Each became `x = x + 1`. The standard brought no new clang-tidy findings.

**Part 2 (complete):** `./build.sh unit-test` builds the Catch2 tests, and Catch2 itself, with `-fsanitize=address,undefined -fno-sanitize-recover=all`, and `SANITIZE=OFF` turns that off. On macOS it uses Homebrew LLVM, because Apple clang 17's AddressSanitizer runtime deadlocks before `main` on macOS 26. The sanitizers found one bug: `indexForCharacter` scanned 96 bytes of a 95-byte map. It returned a glyph past the end of the font for a NUL cell, and drew the apostrophe with the backtick's glyph. It now computes the index from the character.

**Part 3 (complete):** `ProcessSyscallHandlers`, the serial tables in both `arch/*/exceptions.cpp` and the unit tests' tables name each slot with a designated initializer. Every `Syscall::Handlers` member defaults to `nullptr`, so a table lists only the syscalls it serves, and a new syscall no longer touches the architecture files. The kernel's 22 spinlocks, its file-system mutex and its 5 wait queues defined at namespace scope are `constinit`, and so are libc's three locks (`LibC::Lock` gained a `= 0` default so clang accepts it).

- **Part 1: one language standard.** The kernel builds as `gnu++11`, libc and the programs as `gnu++17`, and the host tests with the compiler's default. Move all of them to `gnu++23`, which GCC 14 and Apple clang 17 both support. A trial build as C++23 found no errors and six warnings, all `++` on a `volatile` (deprecated since C++20), in `arch/aarch64/keyboard.cpp`, `scheduler.cpp`, `time/timer.cpp`, `libctest.cpp` and `test_program.cpp`. C++23 rather than C++20, because C++23 allows `|=` and `&=` on `volatile` again, which the device-register code relies on. Also clear whatever new clang-tidy findings the standard brings.
- **Part 2: sanitizers for the host tests.** Build and run the Catch2 tests with AddressSanitizer and UndefinedBehaviorSanitizer (`-fsanitize=address,undefined`), as a `build.sh` option or the default for `unit-test`, and fix what they find. Leak checking is not available on macOS arm64, so it covers out-of-bounds access, use after free and undefined behaviour.
- **Part 3: safer tables and globals.** Fill the syscall handler tables (`process.cpp`, both `arch/*/exceptions.cpp`, and the tests' tables) with C++20 designated initializers, so each entry names its slot and a new syscall no longer edits the architecture files. Mark the global spinlocks, mutexes and wait queues `constinit`, so the compiler checks that they need no constructor, which early boot code relies on.

**Complete when:** every target builds as `gnu++23` without warnings, the host tests pass under both sanitizers, and the syscall tables use designated initializers.

### 21. Embedded Swift For User Programs (Complete)

**Summary:** User programs can be written in Embedded Swift with the swift.org toolchain. They import the C library as `FeatherLibC` and the shared `FeatherSupport` module, and link into the same static-PIE programs as the C++ ones. `hello`, `rev`, `json` and `life` run on both targets, and the shell smoke test checks them. The parsers of `json` and `life` are host-tested in `unit-test`. `swiftbench` against `cppbench` puts optimized Swift at 1.6 to 2 times C++'s time, about 4 times on `life`, mostly from bounds and overflow checks, with code 1.7 to 1.9 times as large. Measuring it showed that every C++ program had been built at `-O0`, so `build.sh` gained `BUILD_TYPE=Debug|Release`. What Embedded Swift leaves out is listed in `docs/development.md`. The C library gained `fma` for `Double(String)`. `tasks` runs `async`/`await`, tasks and actors on both targets, on Swift's concurrency runtime built from the 6.4.2 sources with pthreads, over libc++'s headers. A global executor of FeatherOS's own runs the jobs on a thread for every CPU, and `taskprimes` shows a task group spread over all four of QEMU's CPUs.

User programs written in Embedded Swift, built on the host with the swift.org toolchain (Swift 6 or newer; Xcode's toolchain does not reliably target ELF) and linked against the FeatherOS libc like the C++ programs. The kernel stays C++.

- **Part 1: toolchain and build (done).** `build.sh` asks swiftly for the toolchain `.swift-version` pins (or takes `SWIFTC`) and passes it to CMake as `FEATHEROS_SWIFTC`. CMake test-compiles a probe and otherwise skips the Swift programs with a message; the shell smoke test then skips their checks. Each program in `FEATHEROS_SWIFT_PROGRAMS` is compiled by a custom command (`-enable-experimental-feature Embedded -wmo -Osize`, target `x86_64-unknown-none-elf` or `aarch64-none-none-elf`) into one object, linked with the C library's startup into the same static-PIE programs. Swift's ELF output is already position-independent. The runtime needs `posix_memalign`, `free`, `memset` and `putchar`; `posix_memalign` was added to the C library (`HeapAllocator::AllocateAligned`). `hello` prints through `print`, and the shell smoke test checks it on both targets.
- **Part 2: C interop (done).** `src/libc/swift/module.modulemap` makes every C library header but `assert.h` one module, `FeatherLibC`, which Clang reads with `-ffreestanding`. New in the C library: `arc4random_buf` (buffered reads of `/dev/random`, for hashing and `random`), and `featheros_argument_count`/`featheros_argument_values` in `<featheros.h>`, since Swift's top-level code does not see `main`'s parameters and Embedded Swift has no `CommandLine`. Swift programs link the toolchain's `libswiftUnicodeDataTables.a`, which `String` hashing and `Character`s need even for ASCII text; the linker takes only the parts used. `hello` prints its arguments and uses a protocol with generics, a class with reference counting, a `Set` of strings, a `Character` count, a random number and C calls; libctest checks the new C functions.
- **Part 3: shared Swift code (done).** `FeatherSupport`, a Swift module in `src/user/swift_support/` built once per architecture and imported by every Swift program: `Arguments` (`all`, `operands`, `programName`, `helpRequested`), `InputFile` (a path or standard input, `readAll` and `readLine` as bytes over `fread` and `getline`), `Output.standard`/`Output.error` (a `TextOutputStream` over the C streams, so its text keeps its order with `print`), `SystemError` with `strerror` text and an `errno` property (the C macro is not imported), and `ExitCode` with `exit`, `warn` and `fail`, whose messages start with the program's name. Errors use typed throws (`throws(SystemError)`), so callers get a `SystemError` without a cast. Embedded Swift serializes every function body into the `.swiftmodule`, so each program compiles in the parts it uses and the library archive is only a fallback. `rev` (Swift; reverses each line by `Character`) reads files or standard input; `hello` reads its arguments through `Arguments` and compares `readAll` with `readLine` on `/etc/hosts`; the shell smoke test checks both, including a missing file and `rev` in a pipeline.
- **Part 4: `json` (done).** `json [-c] [-r] [path [file...]]`, a jq-like tool and the first Swift program of several files (`src/user/json/`, top-level code in `main.swift`; a `FEATHEROS_SWIFT_PROGRAMS` source may be a directory). An RFC 8259 parser over bytes into an `indirect enum JSONValue` (strings as UTF-8 bytes with escapes, surrogate pairs and raw UTF-8 checked, numbers kept as text, repeated keys as in jq, at most 256 levels), with errors that give the line, the column in characters and what was found; a whitespace-separated sequence of values, as jq reads. Pretty (two spaces) and compact printers implement the `JSONPrinter` protocol, whose extension walks the value, and the program is generic over them. Paths `.`, `.name`, `."name"`, `[n]` with negative indexes, `["name"]` and `[]`, with jq's null rules; `-r` prints strings raw. Working on bytes keeps the Unicode data tables out of the binary. `unit-test` builds the sources without `main.swift` with `tests/unit/swift/json_tests.swift` for the host and runs it as the CTest case `swift.json`; the shell smoke test reads `/system/sample.json` and piped input, and `NETWORK_TEST` pipes `fetch` into it over both IP versions.
- **Part 5: `life` (done).** `life [-p glider|blinker|random] [-n generations] [-s columnsxrows]` (`src/user/life/`), Conway's Game of Life on a grid whose edges wrap, starting from a shape in the middle or a quarter of the cells at random (`SystemRandomNumberGenerator`, over `arc4random_buf`). Without `-n` it animates on the alternate screen in raw mode, with plain `tcsetattr` and escape codes: a read with `VMIN` 0 and `VTIME` 1 paces the generations and takes `q` or Ctrl+C, and only the rows that changed are drawn again, below a status line with the generation and the population. With `-n` it prints the grid after that many generations, `#` and `.`, so tests need no terminal. The grid is the terminal's size above its last row, or 80×23 without a terminal. Swift does not import variadic C functions such as `ioctl`, so the C library gained POSIX 2024's `tcgetwinsize`/`tcsetwinsize`, with `struct winsize` moved to `termios.h`; `ioctl` calls them. `grid.swift` and `pattern.swift` import nothing, so `unit-test` builds them for the host with `tests/unit/swift/life_tests.swift` (CTest `swift.life`; the Swift test cases are a loop over program directories). The shell smoke test prints a glider and a blinker, the size without a terminal, errors, and animates a glider until `q`. An editor, speed control, resizing and Ctrl+Z redraws are in `docs/ideas.md`.
- **Part 6: benchmark against C++ (done).** `swiftbench` (`src/user/swiftbench/`) and `cppbench` (`src/user/cppbench.cpp`) run five workloads written step for step alike:
  - `life`: 64x64 cells for 20 generations, on `life`'s own `Grid`, compiled in through the new `FEATHEROS_SWIFT_SHARED_SOURCES_<name>`.
  - `sieve`: the primes below 1 000 000.
  - `quicksort`: the same hand-written quicksort over 20 000 numbers.
  - `sort`: the same numbers, by Swift's `sort()` against `qsort`.
  - `json`: an 87 KB document, parsed by `json`'s parser and by a line-by-line C++ port with its own `Vector`, as the toolchain has no C++ standard library.

  Both programs draw numbers from SplitMix64 with the same seeds, and each workload prints a check value that must match between them. `-t` times a workload for some seconds with `CLOCK_MONOTONIC`, and `-n` runs it a set number of times. The shell smoke test checks the check values of both programs.

  The benchmark showed that every C++ program and the C library were built at `-O0`, because `CMAKE_BUILD_TYPE` was never set. So `build.sh` gained `BUILD_TYPE`:
  - Debug, the default: `-O0 -g`, and Swift `-Onone`.
  - Release: `-O2`, and Swift `-O`, for the kernel, the C library and the programs, keeping `assert`.

  The initramfs gets copies of the programs with the debug information stripped. Release needed `#pragma GCC optimize("no-tree-loop-distribute-patterns")` in the `memset`/`memcpy` files, libgcc's `__lshrti3` in `src/libc/compiler_runtime.cpp` for AArch64, and `hardening`'s `RunViolation` kept out of line. The matrix runs the shell session in Release on both targets as well (38 cases).

  `./build.sh --arch <arch> benchmark` (`tools/benchmark.py`) builds in Release and boots QEMU. It runs three interleaved rounds, prints each workload's fastest time with Swift's ratio to C++, and the loaded code and data bytes, and fails on a check mismatch.

  Results:
  - Swift at `-O` takes 1.6 to 2.0 times as long as C++ at `-O2` on `sieve`, `quicksort` and `json`.
  - It takes about 4 times as long on `life`, mostly because of the bounds and overflow checks: at `-Ounchecked`, `life`, `sieve` and `quicksort` fell to 1.9, 1.2 and 1.05 times.
  - `json` stays at 1.8 times without the checks.
  - Swift's library sort takes 0.6 to 0.8 times as long as `qsort`.
  - Swift's code is 1.7 to 1.9 times as large.

  The table is in `docs/development.md`, "Swift against C++".
- **Part 7: what Embedded Swift leaves out (done).** `docs/development.md`, "What Embedded Swift leaves out", lists what the pinned 6.4.2 toolchain leaves out, each item checked by compiling small programs for both targets:
  - reflection (`Mirror`, `String(describing:)`)
  - Foundation, `Codable` and `Regex`
  - concurrency
  - `weak` and `unowned`
  - non-final generic methods of classes
  - casts to protocol types
  - key paths as values

  Existentials and untyped `throws` turned out to work, so the earlier note that Embedded Swift has no `any Error` was corrected. One gap is FeatherOS's own: on x86-64, `Double(String)` calls `fma`, which the C library lacks.
- **Part 8: `fma` (done).** `fma` and `fmaf` in the C library (`Math::FusedMultiplyAdd` in `src/libc/math_functions.cpp`). They form the exact product of the mantissas in 128 bits and align the addend under a sticky bit, then round once to the target format, so `fmaf` never rounds through `double`. They set `EDOM` for an infinity times zero or opposite infinities, and `ERANGE` on overflow. Host tests compare them bit for bit with the host's over special values, random exponents, near-cancellations, subnormals and overflow, and libctest checks them on both targets. `hello` parses a `Double` and a `Float`, which failed to link on x86-64 before.
- **Part 9: concurrency on x86-64, one thread (done).** `async`/`await`, `Task` and actors with the toolchain's `libswift_Concurrency.a` and its cooperative executor, which runs every job on the program's thread in queue order. Programs in `FEATHEROS_SWIFT_CONCURRENCY_PROGRAMS` are built only where that archive exists (x86-64), with `-parse-as-library` and an `@main` type, because top-level `await` crashes swiftc 6.4.2. `src/user/swift_concurrency_platform.cpp` provides the 15 symbols the link left:
  - the Darwin names the archive was built against, `__error`, `__stderrp`, `_fputs` and `_nanosleep`, forwarded to the C library, and `memset_s`
  - libc++'s `std::chrono::steady_clock::now()` over `CLOCK_MONOTONIC`; `clock_getres` went into the C library
  - `swift_getObjectType` (the object's first word), `swift_reportError` and `swift_getMainExecutor` (the generic executor, as the cooperative executor has it)
  - the `_task_serialExecutor_*`/`_task_taskExecutor_*` hooks and `swift::getResilientMetadataBounds`, which abort: they are generic Swift functions Embedded Swift never puts in the archive, reached only by custom executors and distributed actors (upstream's macOS tests dead-strip their callers)

  `tasks` (`src/user/tasks.swift`) checks a task's value, two tasks taking turns, an actor incremented by 100 child tasks, a task group, `async let`, a checked continuation, an `AsyncStream`, cancellation, a thrown error and a `@TaskLocal`; the shell smoke test checks it on x86-64 in Debug and Release. Embedded Swift has no clocks, so no `Task.sleep`, and no `Task.checkCancellation`.
- **Part 10: the concurrency runtime from source (done).** The toolchain has no `_Concurrency` module or runtime for `aarch64-none-none-elf`, and its x86-64 runtime was built against Darwin's headers without threads. `src/user/swift_concurrency/` now builds both halves for both targets, as upstream builds them for Embedded Swift but with Swift's pthreads threading package:
  - **Sources:** `build.sh` fetches shallow, sparse clones of `swift-6.4.2-RELEASE` into `deps/swift` and of `llvmorg-21.1.8`'s `libcxx/include` into `deps/llvm-project`. This works on Linux hosts too.
  - **The patch:** `swift.patch` adds `__featheros__` cases to the runtime's clocks, its sleep and its thread stack bounds, and drops an unused `<sys/syscall.h>`.
  - **C++ half:** compiled by the toolchain's `clang++` over libc++'s headers and the FeatherOS C headers, with a FeatherOS `__config_site` (pthreads; no locales, wide characters or files).
  - **Swift half:** only the `_Concurrency` module, found by the programs through `-I`.
  - **Platform file:** `platform.cpp` replaces Part 9's Darwin names. It keeps the stand-ins for runtime entry points Embedded Swift leaves out, and adds `steady_clock::now` and `this_thread::sleep_for` from libc++'s compiled library.

  The C library gained what libc++'s headers need: `pthread_key_*` (128 keys, destructors when a thread ends, linked only by programs that create a key), `uchar.h` (`mbstate_t` and UTF-8 conversions to UTF-16 and UTF-32, host-tested) and `inttypes.h`. Clang reads the C headers, too. `tasks` passes every check on AArch64 as on x86-64. The executor is still the cooperative one, on the program's main thread.
- **Part 11: an executor on every CPU (done).** `src/user/swift_concurrency/global_executor.cpp` replaces upstream's `CooperativeGlobalExecutor.cpp`:
  - **Queue:** one queue of jobs, by priority and then arrival, behind a pthread mutex and a condition variable on `CLOCK_MONOTONIC`. Delayed jobs wait in a list sorted by deadline.
  - **Threads:** the first job starts a worker for every online CPU but one. The main thread joins them in `swift_task_asyncMainDrainQueue`, so a program on QEMU's 4 CPUs has 4 threads running jobs.
  - **Main executor:** Embedded Swift has no `MainActor` outside WASI, so the main executor stays the generic one.

  `platform.cpp` lost `steady_clock::now` and `sleep_for`, which only the cooperative executor used.

  Running tasks at the same time showed a bug that one thread had hidden. The runtime's `_Deque` takes its capacity from `ManagedBuffer.capacity`, which on a bare-metal target reads a `_swift_stdlib_malloc_size` shim that returns 0. So the capacity was negative, and an `AsyncStream` holding a second buffered value failed an assertion. `swift.patch` makes the deque use the capacity it asked for under Embedded Swift, as upstream does on OpenBSD.

  Tests:
  - `tasks` checks that each of two turn-taking tasks reaches the shared actor in order, instead of a fixed interleaving. It also checks a stream whose 100 values are all buffered before the first is read.
  - `taskprimes [tasks] [limit]` (`src/user/taskprimes.swift`) counts primes in a task group and prints the threads and CPUs the tasks ran on.
  - The shell smoke test runs `taskprimes 16 100000` and checks the count and at least two threads and CPUs, on both targets in Debug and Release.

**Complete when:** on both targets, Swift programs build from `src/user/`, run from the shell, and the matrix checks their output, and Swift tasks run on every CPU.

### 22. Mount Table, FAT And ext2/ext3/ext4 (Complete)

**Summary:** Filesystems are mounted through a mount table (`mount`, `umount`, `/proc/mounts`) and come from a shared, self-contained core in `src/fs/core/` (namespace `FeatherDisk`, MIT OR Apache-2.0) that the kernel, the host tests and the host tool `fscli` all use. The core reads MBR and GPT disks, reads and writes FAT16, and reads and writes ext2, ext3 and ext4: checksums, extent trees, hash-indexed directories, and jbd2 journals in ordered mode with replay, commits, checkpoints and orphan handling, so a crash at any write leaves a volume that our mount or `e2fsck -fy` recovers. FeatherOS gained the POSIX file ABI with symbolic and hard links, a kernel writeback task that commits every 5 s, and an ext4 root on the test disk's `vda1`, chosen by the lowest-numbered filesystem partition or by `root=vdaN`. FAT16, ext2 and ext3 partitions are mounted beside it, written, unmounted, and checked after reboots and power cuts with `e2fsck`, `debugfs` and mtools on both targets.

The mount table and Linux filesystems, split out of M19 because they need no USB: they are built and tested on the existing virtio-blk disk. This moves beyond the FAT16 limits (ASCII names of at most 63 characters, no permissions or links).

**Shared filesystem core:** the filesystem drivers are written once and used by three hosts: the FeatherOS kernel, Feather Disk (a Mac app by the same author, dual MIT OR Apache-2.0, later also on Windows) and a command-line tool, `fscli`. The core is developed here in `src/fs/core/` and kept ready to leave: when the Mac app starts, `git subtree split --prefix=src/fs/core` gives the Feather Disk repo its first commits, and FeatherOS then takes updates with `git subtree pull --squash`. Until then these rules keep the folder self-contained:

- It includes nothing outside `src/fs/core/`, which a `tools/quality.sh` check enforces. It reaches the outside only through `block_device.h` (read and write sectors) and `host.h` (allocation, locks, logging, clock).
- It has its own `CMakeLists.txt` and builds on its own: on the host for its tests and `fscli`, and as a static library with the kernel's flags.
- It must compile with our standard library, Apple's libc++ and Microsoft's STL, so it uses only headers that never allocate: `<cstdint>`, `<cstddef>`, `<array>`, `<span>`, `<string_view>`, `<optional>`, `<expected>`, `<bit>`, `<type_traits>`, `<utility>`. No `vector`, `string` or `new`; memory comes from `host.h`. No exceptions or RTTI.
- Every file starts with `// SPDX-License-Identifier: MIT OR Apache-2.0`.
- The kernel's adapter (core interface and `host.h` to the VFS, heap, locks, `KLogger`, clock and block devices) lives in `src/kernel/filesystem/` and stays behind at the split.
- The Mac and Windows layers bind to a thin C API over the core, added when the Mac app starts, not in this milestone.

The core's driver interface covers probe, mount/unmount/sync, lookup, directory reads, attributes, read/write/truncate, create/remove/rename/mkdir/link/symlink and capabilities, with opaque 64-bit node IDs and plain error codes. Each part below lands in the core first, with host tests over image files, so it reaches the Mac app as soon as it lands.

```
src/fs/core/
  CMakeLists.txt
  include/fs/   block_device.h, host.h, filesystem.h
  common/       block cache, bitmaps, CRC32C, UTF-8/16, little-endian helpers
  partition/    MBR + GPT
  fat/ ext/     one directory per filesystem
  probe.cpp     filesystem detection from superblocks
  tests/        host tests over images (mkfs.fat, mke2fs -d), image scripts
  tools/fscli/  ls, cat, cp in and out, info on an image or disk
```

After the split, the Feather Disk repo adds `mac/` (a SwiftUI app that browses and copies, then mounting through an FSKit extension) and later `windows/` (the same, mounting through WinFsp or Dokany; check their licenses first).

- **Part 1: mount table (done).** A filesystem driver interface and a mount table in the VFS replace the hard-coded `/` (FAT16) and `/system` (initramfs), with `mount`, `umount` and `/proc/mounts`.
  - **Driver interface:** `Filesystem::Driver` (`src/kernel/filesystem/filesystem.h`) has path operations relative to the mount point (open, stat, create, remove, `mkdir`/`rmdir`, rename within the filesystem, is-directory, read directory, read-only) and handle operations on a driver-owned `Filesystem::Node` (a 64-bit ID plus a 64-bit payload: FAT16 keeps the entry's directory cluster and index as the ID and the first cluster as the payload), plus `Sync`, which unmounting calls last. Unsupported operations fail by default (`Sync` succeeds), and `Filesystem::Properties` says whether a driver is read-only, tracks its open files or holds devices. `drivers.cpp` wraps FAT16 (whose `Sync` is `BlockDevice::Flush`, since it writes every sector through), the initramfs's `system` directory, `/dev`, `/proc` and the two tmpfs roots. `VFS::FileHandle` lost `FileKind` and the FAT16 fields; `Files` asks `VFS::IsWritable`, `IsDevice`, `DeviceNode` and `KeyOf` (driver and node, the open-file key), and `FAT16::DirectoryEntry` became `Filesystem::DirectoryEntry`.
  - **Mount table and types:** `VFS::MountTable` (`mount_table.cpp`, 16 entries, no allocation) does the longest-prefix match on whole components, lists the mount points inside a directory in mount order, and holds a driver instance at one point at most. `VFS::TypeRegistry` (`type_registry.cpp`) names each instance by type and source: `initramfs` `initramfs` (`/system`), `tmpfs` `etc` (`/etc`), `devfs` `devfs` (`/dev`), `procfs` `procfs` (`/proc`), `tmpfs` `tmp` (`/tmp`), and `fat16` `vda` (`/`; `vda` is Linux's name for the first virtio disk, as `/dev` has no disk node yet; `vda1` since Part 3). There is no multi-instance support: mounting a type mounts its one instance, which keeps its contents while unmounted. `VFS::MountBuiltIn` (kernel startup, before `LoadEtc`) registers and mounts the five, and `VFS::MountBlockDevice` registers and mounts FAT16; ext2 (Part 3) registers the same way. Both tables are under one spinlock (`GUARDED_BY(mount_lock)`) that is never held across a driver call.
  - **Unmounting:** `VFS::Unmount` is busy while the mount has users or another mount point lies inside it. Every VFS call counts as a user of its mount from lookup until the driver returns (`MountTable::Acquire`/`Release`, through `Use` in `vfs.cpp`), and an open handle until `Close`. `BeginUnmount` marks a mount with no users, after which lookups fail instead of using it or falling through to its parent, so the driver's `Sync` runs without the spinlock and `FinishUnmount` then removes it; if `Sync` fails, the mount stays (`MountResult::Failed`, `EIO`). `Files::Unmount` also refuses while any process's working directory is inside the mount, asking `process.cpp` under the filesystem lock, which `chdir` (`Files::ChangeDirectory`) now holds while it stores the directory. Terminal descriptors opened through `/dev` hold no VFS handle and do not keep it busy.
  - **Interfaces:** syscalls `Mount` (64, source, target, type) and `Unmount` (65), the new `SyscallErrorUnknownFilesystemType` (`ENODEV`, added to `errno.h`), and libc's `mount` (Linux's signature, flags 0 and data null) and `umount` in `<sys/mount.h>`. A target need not exist, but its parent must. `/proc/mounts` lists `<source> <point> <type> <ro|rw> 0 0` in mount order (`VFS::DescribeMount` through `Procfs::Source::mount_at`). Programs `mount` (no arguments prints `/proc/mounts`; `mount -t <type> <source> <target>`) and `umount <target>`.
  - **Tests:** host tests in `mount_table_tests.cpp` (longest prefix, busy rules, unmounting blocks lookups, the type registry), `virtual_files_tests.cpp` (`/proc/mounts`, `Files::Mount`/`Unmount` errors, `/tmp` keeping its files across a remount, nested mounts) and `filesystem_tests.cpp` (the disk is flushed on unmount and keeps its files), and the syscall dispatch test. The shell test (`tools/smoke/cases/shell/mounts.py`) lists the mounts, unmounts `/tmp` (gone from `mount`, `ls /tmp` fails), mounts it again with a file written before still there, and checks that `umount /`, `umount /nonexistent` and an unknown type fail. The `STORAGE` kernel test unmounts the built-in filesystems and the disk (busy while they are mounted and while `PERSIST.TXT` is open), mounts the disk again and checks the file. A disk directory named like a mount point is still listed beside it, as in step 1.
- **Part 2: the shared core, with FAT (done).** `src/fs/core/` exists under the rules above, with its own CMake, the include check and host tests; the FAT16 driver and the MBR/GPT parsing moved into it, the kernel's FAT code became an adapter, and `fscli` reads FAT16 images and disks on the host. `STORAGE_TEST` with its mtools checks passes unchanged. FAT32 stays in M19 Part 1, as an extension of this driver.
  - **Skeleton and partitions:** The core's namespace is `FeatherDisk`, its public headers are included as `fs/...`, and its internal ones (`common/...`) by their path from `src/fs/core/`. `include/fs/error.h` has the plain `Error` enum and `Result<T>` = `std::expected<T, Error>` (`Status` for `void`). `block_device.h`'s abstract `BlockDevice` gives the sector size (a power of two from 512) and count, multi-sector `Read`/`Write` over `std::span` and `Flush`. `host.h` declares `FeatherDisk::Host::Allocate`/`Free`, an opaque `Lock` that each host defines (`CreateLock`, `Acquire`, `Release`, `DestroyLock`; held only briefly and never across a device call), `Log(LogLevel, std::string_view)` and `WallClockNanoseconds()`; hosts bind at link time. `filesystem.h` splits the driver into `FilesystemType` (`Name`, `Probe`, `Mount` returning a `Filesystem*`, `Unmount`, which syncs and frees and leaves the volume mounted if syncing fails) and the mounted `Filesystem`: `GetCapabilities`, `Root`, `Statistics`, `Lookup` of one name in a directory, `ReadDirectory` by cookie without `.` and `..`, `GetAttributes`/`SetAttributes` (mode, owner, group, times), `Read`, `Write`, `Truncate`, `Create`, `MakeDirectory`, `Remove`, `RemoveDirectory`, `Rename`, `Link`, `SymbolicLink`, `ReadLink` and `Sync`, all on opaque 64-bit `NodeId`s. The write side defaults to `Error::Unsupported` (`filesystem.cpp`) and `Sync` to success. Both interfaces have protected non-virtual destructors, so the kernel needs no `operator delete` for them. `common/little_endian.h` has `LoadLittleEndian<T>`/`StoreLittleEndian<T>` and `common/host_buffer.cpp` a `HostBuffer` from `Host::Allocate`. `partition/partition.cpp` is the kernel's MBR/GPT parser moved over `BlockDevice` as `FeatherDisk::Partition::Find(device, std::span<Range>)`, now for any power-of-two sector size, with one sector buffer from the host instead of the stack; `Partition::Identify` reports the scheme sector 0 announces (`Scheme::None`, `Mbr`, `Gpt`; a protective entry means GPT even when the GPT is damaged), for `fscli info`. `src/fs/core/CMakeLists.txt` builds `featherdisk_core` with the kernel's flags against `src/libcxx` when `src/CMakeLists.txt` sets `FEATHERDISK_FREESTANDING`; otherwise it builds the library (with `-fno-exceptions`; RTTI stays on, since the Catch2 tests subclass its interfaces, and the kernel build rejects both), `featherdisk_standard_host` (`host/standard_host.cpp`, over `malloc`, `std::mutex`, `stderr` and `std::chrono`) and `featherdisk_core_tests` from `tests/`, with the Catch2 target the parent passes in `FEATHERDISK_CATCH2_TARGET` or, configured on its own, a fetched Catch2 v3.7.1. The kernel side is `src/kernel/storage/core_block_device.cpp` (`BlockDevice::CoreDevice()`) and `src/kernel/filesystem/core_host.cpp` (heap, `SpinLock` with saved interrupts, a `KLogger` on the serial port, `Time::ReadWallClock`); `FAT16::Mount` finds its partitions through the core, and `src/kernel/storage/partition.*` is gone. `tools/quality.sh` has a new `fs-core` stage (and pre-commit hook) for the SPDX line, includes inside the folder and the allowed standard headers; `tests/`, `host/` and `tools/` may use any standard header. Configured on its own, the core builds the fetched Catch2 as C++23, without which Catch2 cannot print a `std::string_view` and the tests do not link. Tests: `fs.core:` CTest cases in `./build.sh unit-test` over in-memory MBR and GPT images (FAT16 and other types, entries off the disk, the capacity cap, a protective MBR, the CRC test moved from `filesystem_tests.cpp`, impossible header fields with valid CRCs, 256-byte entries, 4096-byte sectors, read errors and bad sector sizes), the little-endian helpers, `HostBuffer`, the standard host and the interface defaults; `STORAGE` and `SHELL` smoke tests on both targets.
  - **FAT16 in the core, the kernel's FAT code an adapter:** `src/fs/core/fat/fat16.cpp` is the kernel's FAT16 driver ported over `BlockDevice` and the core interface, as `FeatherDisk::Fat::Fat16Type()` (`include/fs/fat.h`, type name `fat16`), with `fat/names.cpp` (8.3 names, VFAT long-name entries, `LongNameReader`; FAT-specific, so not in `common/`). The logic is the old one with globals turned into members and `bool`s into `Status`/`Result` (`Io`, `Corrupt`, `NoSpace`, `Exists`, `NotEmpty`, `NameTooLong`, ...), so the disk sees the same boot-sector checks, short names and `BASIS~N` aliases, case bits, first-free zeroed clusters, every FAT copy updated, update order and zero timestamps on new entries. It still needs 512-byte sectors. Node IDs: a file is its entry's location, the directory's first cluster (0 for the root) in the upper half and the entry index in the lower, as before, so the root's first slot is node 0; a directory is its first cluster with bit 63 set, which is all a `..` entry records, so it keeps its node across a rename; `Root()` is bit 63 alone. `Lookup` takes `.` and `..` (`..` at the root stays there). Where the interface asks for more than the old driver did: `Write` past the end and a growing `Truncate` fill with zeros, `Rename` replaces a file or an empty directory of the same kind, a read-only mount refuses changes (`ReadOnly`), `Statistics` counts clusters, and `GetAttributes` reports modes 0666/0777 (without write bits read-only) and zero times (FAT times are not read yet). Two small changes: `MakeDirectory` frees its new cluster when the entry cannot be written (the old code leaked it), and the move-into-itself check is bounded by the cluster count instead of the 64-component path depth. The filesystem keeps nothing in memory that changes, so it takes no lock; the host runs changing operations one at a time (the kernel's `file_system_lock`). `Mount` builds it in `Host::Allocate` memory through a class-specific placement `operator new`, since `<new>` is not an allowed header. `FeatherDisk::Partition::Volume` (`include/fs/partition.h`, `partition/volume.cpp`) is one partition of a device as a `BlockDevice`. Kernel side: `Filesystem::CoreDriver` (`src/kernel/filesystem/core_driver.{h,cpp}`) implements `Filesystem::Driver` over any core `Filesystem`, for ext2 too: it walks paths with `Lookup`, turns every error into `false`, answers `Stat`/`ReadDirectory` sizes with `GetAttributes`, counts cookies for an index-based `ReadDirectory`, keeps the VFS rules (truncate only shrinks, rename only onto a missing name or the same node), and `Sync` is the core's `Sync` (`BlockDevice::Flush`). `CoreDriver::Mount(type, disk)` unmounts what it held and mounts the first partition from `Partition::Find` that the type probes; `Drivers::Disk()` is now a `CoreDriver` and `Drivers::MountDisk()` mounts FAT16 on `BlockDevice::CoreDevice()` for `VFS::MountBlockDevice`. `src/kernel/filesystem/fat16.*` and `fat_names.*` are gone, and the `STORAGE` kernel test counts free clusters with `Disk().Statistics`. Tests: the driver's own tests moved to `src/fs/core/tests/` (`fat_tests.cpp` over in-memory volumes from `fat_image.cpp`, `fat_names_tests.cpp`) and gained probing, read-only mounts, replacing renames, growth with zeros, two FAT copies, cookies, statistics, sync and unmount, and mounting through a `Partition::Volume`. `fat_mtools_tests.cpp` runs over an image that `tests/make_fat16_image.sh` makes with mtools at build time (a bare 8 MiB FAT16 volume with two FATs, a long name, nested directories and a 40-entry directory): the core reads it, writes to it, and `mtype`/`mdir` read the changes back; without mtools CMake leaves the image out and both tests `SKIP`. `tests/unit/kernel/filesystem/filesystem_tests.cpp` keeps the adapter and VFS tests. The images come from mtools rather than `mkfs.fat`, which macOS lacks.
  - **`fscli`:** `src/fs/core/tools/fscli/` is a host program (`fscli` target, built by the core's CMake when it is not freestanding, so also in `./build.sh unit-test`, never for the kernel) over `featherdisk_core` and `featherdisk_standard_host`. `file_device.cpp`'s `Fscli::FileDevice` is a `BlockDevice` over an image file (512-byte sectors, a partial last sector left out) or a raw disk (sector size and count from `DKIOCGETBLOCKSIZE`/`DKIOCGETBLOCKCOUNT` on macOS, `BLKSSZGET`/`BLKGETSIZE64` on Linux) through `pread`/`pwrite`; it opens read-only unless a command writes, and none does yet (`Write` fails with `ReadOnly`). `disk.cpp` detects filesystems through `Fscli::FilesystemTypes()`, a list of `FilesystemType`s probed in order (FAT16 alone; ext2 is added there), reads the layout (a filesystem on the whole device first, else `Partition::Identify` and `Partition::Find`), and `MountedVolume` mounts one volume read-only, walks `/`-separated paths with `Lookup` and unmounts when it goes away. Commands (`main.cpp`): `info <image>` (disk size, table `none`/`mbr`/`gpt`, each partition's start, size and filesystem, and the volume statistics of what mounts), `ls <image>[:N] [path]` (`-`/`d` marker, size, name; a file lists itself), `cat <image>[:N] <path>`, `cp <image>[:N] <path> <host destination>` (into a directory under the same name; nothing is left behind on failure) and `--help`. Partition numbers follow `info`; without one, the whole disk if it holds a filesystem, else the first partition that does. An existing file whose name ends in `:N` is taken whole. Exit status 1 for a failed operation and 2 for a usage error, with `fscli: <image>: <path>: <reason>` on standard error. `cp` in comes with ext2 writes in Part 4.
  - **Tests for `fscli`:** `tests/make_fat16_image.sh` takes `mbr` to put the same contents in the only partition of an MBR disk, from sector 2048; both images are made at build time when mtools is present. `tools/fscli/tests/fscli_tests.sh` runs the CTest cases `fs.core:fscli help`, `info`, `ls`, `cat`, `cp`, `partition` (the MBR image, `image:1`, a missing partition) and `errors` (missing images and paths, directories, a partition number on a bare volume, usage errors, a disk without a filesystem); without the images it exits 77, which CTest reports as skipped. `fs.core:` also checks `Partition::Identify`. By hand, `fscli` read the `build/<arch>/virtio-disk.img` test disk (MBR) and a GPT disk from `tools/create_test_disk.py --layout gpt`. Part 2 passed `./build.sh unit-test`, the `STORAGE` and `SHELL` smoke tests on both targets and the full matrix.
- **Part 3: ext2 read (done).** The core reads ext2 (`FeatherDisk::Ext::Ext2Type()`, `src/fs/core/ext/`), `fscli` reads it on the host, and the test disk has an ext2 partition after the FAT16 one that FeatherOS mounts read-only with `mount -t ext2 vda2 <point>`. Partition sources are named as Linux names them (`vda1`, `vda2`), so the FAT16 root is now `vda1`.
  - **The driver (step 1):** `src/fs/core/ext/` reads ext2 as `FeatherDisk::Ext::Ext2Type()` (`include/fs/ext2.h`, type name `ext2`). `superblock.cpp` decodes and checks the superblock: revisions 0 and 1, 1, 2 or 4 KiB blocks (larger ones `Unsupported`), power-of-two inodes from 128 bytes up to the block size, the first data block, per-group counts within one bitmap block, an inode count of exactly groups times inodes per group, and the group descriptor table and block count inside the volume. `inode.cpp` decodes inodes: 32-bit owners and groups with the Linux high halves, a regular file's 64-bit size, times with the large-inode nanoseconds and epoch bits when `i_extra_isize` covers them, and `i_blocks` with huge_file. `directory.cpp` parses one linked-list record, with or without the filetype byte. `ext2.cpp` is the filesystem. Node IDs are inode numbers, with the root at 2. Group descriptors locate the inode tables, and `MapBlock` walks direct and single, double and triple indirect blocks, with 0 as a hole that reads as zeros. `Lookup` matches bytes and takes `.` and `..` from the directory's own records. `ReadDirectory` leaves them out, takes types from the filetype byte or the inode, and uses the byte offset of the next record as its cookie; each block is walked from its start, so any cookie finds the next record. `ReadLink` reads fast links (under 60 bytes, in `i_block`) and slow ones (one block), and `GetAttributes` gives mode, owner, group, links, size and times; `Statistics` reports the superblock's counts.
  - **Read-only and refusals:** every mount is read-only, whatever `MountOptions` ask (`WritesSupported` is false until Part 4; unknown read-only compatible features would force it too). `GetCapabilities().writable` is false, every change fails with `ReadOnly`, and `Sync` has nothing to write. An incompatible feature other than filetype refuses the mount with `Unsupported` and a host log line with the feature bits: extents, 64bit, flex_bg, meta_bg, a journal that needs recovery, and so ext4. Inodes flagged for extents or inline data are `Unsupported` too. An ext3 volume with a clean journal reads as ext2.
  - **Validation:** every record must be at least 12 bytes, 4-byte aligned and inside its block, with room for its name. Names are at most 255 bytes, and a used record needs a name. Inode numbers are checked against the inode count, and block numbers must lie above the superblock's block and below the block count. Inode tables must fit in the volume, a directory's size must be whole blocks within the bytes it holds, a slow link must fit in its block, and a size past what the block map reaches is refused. The root must be a directory. Each failure is `Corrupt`, and the indirection depth is fixed, so nothing loops.
  - **Memory:** `common/block_cache.cpp` keeps 32 KiB of metadata blocks per mount, least recently used out. It copies blocks in and out under a host lock that is never held across a device call, so operations may run at once. File data bypasses the cache, with consecutive blocks in one device read. Each operation takes one or two block buffers from `Host::Allocate`. `common/bytes.cpp` has `CopyBytes`/`ZeroBytes`, which the core needs without `<cstring>`.
  - **Partitions:** `Partition::Find` takes MBR type 0x83 as well as the FAT16 types. GPT already took every used entry, the Linux filesystem data GUID included. The kernel's FAT16 mount still finds the FAT partition behind a Linux one, since `CoreDriver::Mount` probes each partition; `filesystem_tests.cpp` checks it. Step 2 registered `ext2` in the kernel (below).
  - **`fscli`:** probes ext2 after FAT16. `ls` now prints `ls -l`-style modes, so FAT on its read-only mount shows `-r--r--r--`, and shows a link as `name -> target`. `cat` and `cp` refuse links (not followed) and devices, FIFOs and sockets.
  - **Core tests (step 1):** `tests/make_ext2_image.sh` makes seven 8 MiB images at build time with `mke2fs -d`, and fixes owners, times and link modes with `debugfs`:
    - 1 KiB blocks with 128-byte inodes, 4 KiB blocks with 256-byte ones, and 2 KiB blocks without filetype;
    - revision 0, the 1 KiB volume as an MBR disk's Linux partition, ext3 and ext4.
    - Each holds a 448000-byte file past the single indirect blocks and a sparse file whose tail at 70 MiB needs triple indirect blocks with 1 KiB blocks. It also has nested directories, fast and slow links, a hard link, a FIFO, a set-user-ID file and a 300-entry directory over several blocks.
    - CMake looks for `mke2fs` in Homebrew's keg-only `e2fsprogs` first (`/opt/homebrew/opt/e2fsprogs/sbin`, `/usr/local/opt/...`), then on `PATH`, and `debugfs` beside it; without either, the image tests skip.
    - `ext2_image_tests.cpp` reads every image. It also mounts ext2 and FAT16 partitions side by side in MBR and GPT disks, reads 4096-byte sectors, and refuses ext4 and patched incompatible features. It damages records, block pointers, sizes, counts, group descriptors and the root, and flips random metadata bytes in 300 rounds that must neither crash nor hang. `ext2_format_tests.cpp` and `block_cache_tests.cpp` cover the parts, and `fs.core:fscli ext2-info`, `ext2-ls`, `ext2-cat`, `ext2-partition` and `ext2-errors` run `fscli` over the images. The partition helpers moved to `tests/partition_image.cpp` and the generic ones to `tests/filesystem_helpers.cpp`. Step 1 passed `./build.sh unit-test` (ASan and UBSan), the standalone core build and the `STORAGE` and `SHELL` smoke tests on both targets.
  - **The test disk (step 2):** `tools/create_test_disk.py` makes a 48 MiB disk: FAT16 from sector 2048 to 32 MiB, the MBR layout's old size (same files, so the mtools checks are unchanged), then ext2 from sector 65536 to the end (16 MiB, or 33 sectors less on GPT for the backup table), as MBR type 0x83 or a GPT entry with the Linux filesystem GUID (`0FC63DAF-...`). The volume is built in a temporary file with `mke2fs -q -F -t ext2 -b 1024 -I 128 -d <tree>` and copied in; `find_mke2fs` looks in Homebrew's keg-only `e2fsprogs` (`/opt/homebrew/opt/e2fsprogs/sbin`, `/usr/local/opt/...`), then `PATH`, then `/usr/sbin` and `/sbin` (Linux), and exits with an install hint if there is none. A fixed volume UUID, hash seed, `E2FSPROGS_FAKE_TIME` and file times keep the image byte for byte reproducible. The tree (`EXT2_FILES`, `EXT2_LINKS`): `hello.txt`, `big.txt` (5000 lines of `ext2 line NNNNN`, 80000 bytes, past the direct blocks), `docs/notes/deep.txt`, `fast-link` -> `hello.txt`, `slow-link` (a 64-byte target through `../notes/` five times) and a 73-character name FAT16 cannot hold; mke2fs adds `lost+found`.
  - **Kernel (step 2):** `Filesystem::Drivers::Ext2()` is a second `CoreDriver` (`drivers.cpp`, read-only properties, so not tracked), and `CoreDriver::PartitionNumber()` records which entry of `Partition::Find`'s list it mounted, from 1. `Drivers::PartitionSource(n)` names it: `vda` for 0 (the whole disk, unused so far), `vda1`..`vda16`, null past them, the numbering `fscli` uses. `VFS::MountBlockDevice` registers FAT16 as `fat16` and its partition's name, and `RegisterExt2` (`vfs.cpp`) mounts the first ext2 partition in the core and registers it as `ext2 vda2` without adding it to the tree (M19 plans `/media/<label>`); unless it is mounted, a later call mounts it again, and a disk without one drops the registration (`TypeRegistry::Unregister`). Registering a driver again under its type now renames its source, since a disk's partitions can change. `/proc/mounts` shows `vda1 / fat16 rw 0 0` and, once mounted, `vda2 /mnt ext2 ro 0 0`. `core_host.cpp` needed no change: every `CreateLock` allocates a lock and each mount has its own `BlockCache`. The VFS's read-only rules come from the driver's properties, so creating, writing, `mkdir`, `rm` and `rename` fail with `SyscallErrorReadOnly` (`EROFS`, already in libc), and `write`, `mkdir` and `rm` now say why (`Read-only file system`).
  - **Links and long names:** `CoreDriver`'s path walk does not follow symbolic links, so `cat` on a link fails with `ENOENT` (`cat: cannot open ...`), and `ls` lists a link as a file of size 0 (its `stat` fails). Names are cut to 63 bytes by the 64-byte `DirectoryEntry` and `PATH_MAX` of 64, so the long name lists shortened and cannot be opened. (Part 4 step 1 lifted the name limits, and step 2 follows the links.)
  - **Tests (step 2):** the `STORAGE` kernel test (`CheckExt2` in `kernel_test.cpp`, on both layouts through matrix cases `storage_persistence` and `storage_on_gpt_disk`) remounts FAT16 as `fat16 vda1`, checks that `ext2 vda1` is `NotFound`, mounts `ext2 vda2` at `/mnt`, reads `hello.txt`, `docs/notes/deep.txt` and every line of `big.txt`, checks that creating, `mkdir`, removing and opening `fast-link` fail, and unmounts; its message is now `sector and FAT16 file writes and ext2 reads verified`. The shell test's `ext2` part (`tools/smoke/cases/shell/ext2.py`, after `mounts`) mounts `vda2` at `/mnt`, greps `/proc/mounts`, lists it (the shortened long name and `2 directories, 5 files, 80031 bytes`), `cat`s two files, checks `wc -c` of `big.txt`, that `write` and `rm` report `EROFS` and `cat` of a link fails, then unmounts. Host tests: `disk sources are named as Linux names ...` (`PartitionSource`, FAT16 as `vda1`, or `vda2` behind a Linux partition), `an ext2 partition after the FAT16 one mounts read-only by its partition name` (the core's `ext-images/1k.img` as a second partition of the fixture disk, read through the VFS; skipped without e2fsprogs) and the registry's renaming in `mount_table_tests.cpp`.
- **Part 4: ext2 write (done).** Block and inode bitmaps, create, write, truncate, delete, rename, `mkdir`/`rmdir`, symlinks and hard links. Permissions and owners are stored and shown by `ls`, though not yet enforced (FeatherOS has no users). After the OS writes, the host checks the image with `e2fsck -fn`, extending the `STORAGE_TEST` pattern. `fscli` gains `cp` in. Steps (user decision 2026-11-14: full POSIX shape, symlinks resolved in the VFS): 1. ABI widening: `NAME_MAX` 255, `PATH_MAX` 1024, `d_type` in `dirent`, a fuller `struct stat` (mode, uid/gid, nlink, times) through the kernel `DirectoryEntry`/`FileInfo`, the syscalls, libc, the shell and the Swift modulemap, with FAT and the virtual filesystems filling what they can; 2. symlinks: resolution in the VFS path walk across mounts (absolute and relative targets, a 40-hop limit), `lstat`, `readlink`, `symlink` and `link` syscalls and libc calls, `ls -l` showing modes, owners, link counts and `name -> target`; 3. ext2 write in the core: block and inode bitmaps, create, write, truncate, delete; 4. ext2 `mkdir`/`rmdir`, rename, symlinks and hard links; 5. ext2 mounted read-write in FeatherOS, `e2fsck -fn` on the test disk after the OS writes (extending `STORAGE_TEST`), `fscli cp` in, full matrix; `CoreDriver` maps core errors to errno instead of a plain failure, with Linux's codes for `.` and `..` (user decision 2026-11-16: `rmdir .` gives `EINVAL`, `rmdir ..` `ENOTEMPTY`, renaming `.` or `..` `EBUSY`). A crash in the middle of an ext2 rename can leave both names (accepted for ext2; journalled ext3/ext4 in Part 6 make it atomic).
  - **Step 1 done: the file ABI in the POSIX shape.**
    - **Limits:** `User::MaximumPathLength` (1024, `PATH_MAX`, terminator included) and `User::MaximumNameLength` (255, `NAME_MAX`) in `src/user/syscall_abi.h`, which libc's `limits.h` matches (`static_assert` in `dirent.cpp`) and the kernel's `Path::MaximumLength` takes. `Path::Resolve` also refuses a component over `NAME_MAX`, so a path too long as passed, once resolved or in one component fails with `SyscallErrorNameTooLong` (`ENAMETOOLONG`, already in `errno.h`). The 64-byte names and paths are gone from the kernel (`Filesystem::DirectoryEntry`, `MountTable` points, `Procfs::MountStatus`, tmpfs names, whose parent walk no longer copies the path, `Initramfs::ReadDirectory`, `LoadEtc`), libc (`d_name`, `DIR`'s path, `posix_spawn` file actions) and programs (shell, `vi`, `cat`, `libctest`). `User::MaximumCommandLineLength` is now `MaximumPathLength`, so a command line, the shell's line editor (`MaximumEditedLineLength`, with room for a prompt of a whole working directory) and a terminal's canonical line hold 1023 characters, and a full path fits as an argument; 16 words and the 1 KiB environment are unchanged, and `ProgramArguments` (3240 bytes) still fits its page.
    - **Kernel stacks:** nothing path-sized stays on a 16 KiB kernel stack. `Memory::HeapObject<T>` (`heap.h`) is a zero-filled heap object for one scope. Each path syscall copies its argument into a heap `UserPath` (the text and the resolved path), `Rename` takes two, `Mount` a `MountText` for source and type; `ReadLine`'s prompt and line, `Spawn`'s command line and environment, and inside `Spawn` the `ProgramArguments` block with the working directory and program path (`SpawnBuffers`, about 5 KiB at the new sizes) are on the heap too, as are `files.cpp`'s parent path and directory entries, the `ReadDirectory` syscall's entry, `/proc/mounts`' `MountStatus` and the text `/proc`'s `Stat` generates (1 KiB on the stack before). Below the syscall the walk passes pointers into the one heap path: `CoreDriver` walks `std::string_view` components, tmpfs walks a length-limited prefix, so exec, rename and the path walk add no path copies.
    - **Directory entries and stat:** `Filesystem::DirectoryEntry` has a 256-byte name, the mode's type bits and the node; `Filesystem::FileInfo` has node, size, mode (type and permission bits), link count, owner, group and three `Timestamp`s, and the VFS adds a device number per mount (`MountTable::DeviceOf`, numbered as mounts are added). `Driver::Stat` now answers for directories, links and a filesystem's root, and `Driver::StatNode` for an open file (`VFS::StatOpen`, which `fstat` uses). The syscall structs `User::DirectoryEntry` (280 bytes: node, size, `d_type` value, name) and `User::FileStatus` (96 bytes: device, node, mode, link count, owner, group, size, three `FileTime`s, the kind of open file and `read_only`) have their layouts asserted in `syscall_abi.h`; libc fills `struct stat` (now with `st_atim`/`st_mtim`/`st_ctim` as `timespec` and the `st_atime` macros, `S_IFLNK`, `S_IFBLK`, `S_ISLNK`, `S_ISBLK` and the permission constants) and `struct dirent` (`d_ino` from the node, `DT_FIFO` … `DT_SOCK`) from them, and `access(W_OK)` now asks `read_only`. `CoreDriver` maps the core's `Attributes` and `NodeType` (an ext2 special node has no type bits, since the core does not say which kind it is). FAT16 in the core reports `0644`/`0755` instead of `0666`/`0777` (`0444`/`0555` read-only) and still no times: reading FAT timestamps is left for later. tmpfs reports `0644`/`0755` with its node index plus one, `/system` and `/proc` `0444`/`0555`, `/dev` `S_IFCHR` `0666`, all with one link and zero times; pipes, sockets and terminals get device 0, their object's address as node and `0600`, `0777` and `0620`. A mount point listed in its parent carries its filesystem's root node (`MountTable::AcquireChild`).
    - **Programs:** `ls -l` prints the mode string, link count, owner, group, size and UTC modification time; plain `ls` takes directories from `d_type` and now `stat`s links as themselves. FAT16 long names stay at most 63 ASCII characters: widening them is a constant and test change in the core, left for M19's FAT work. The test disk's ext2 files now have fixed owners and modes whoever builds it (`create_test_disk.py` runs `debugfs` after `mke2fs`: user and group 1000, `hello.txt` `0640` in group 100, links `0777`), and the core's test images (`make_ext2_image.sh`) gained a 200-byte name four directories deep under `a/`, a 1006-byte path.
