A crash leaves the last committed state, with new blocks unreachable; overwrites inside a file may show new bytes before their commit (ordered mode, not atomic), a split write keeps its old size until the last piece and a split truncation or removal is finished by the next writable mount or by `e2fsck -fy`. `e2fsck -fn` on a copy that was recovered by either way exits 0.
    - **Tests (4d):** `tests/ext_transaction_crash_tests.cpp` stops runs after writes and recovers each copy both ways, ours (a writable mount: journal replay, then orphan processing) and `e2fsck -fy` (`ReplayBothWays` in `tests/ext2_image.cpp`): both must leave a volume `e2fsck -fn` passes, with the same tree (`TreeOf`: names, types, sizes, links, contents) and the same free ranges per group, and the tree must be that of the last committed state or the one after (`RequireCommittedState`, overwritten blocks may be old or new, no 0xA5 byte of a freed file anywhere). `a journalled volume stopped after any write` runs a 26-change scenario (directories, files with block maps and extents, links, renames onto files, a directory growing past a block, blocks freed and taken again) from mount to unmount on `ext3`, `ext4`, `ext4-blockmap` with a v2 journal, `ext4-journal-gdtcsum` and `ext4-64bit`, cut at every write of the first two (339 and 302 writes) and at every third write of the others, which differ only in the log's tag size and checksums (278 to 335 writes). Each cut is also recovered as a drive that lost a random part of its unflushed writes would leave it (`MemoryDevice::TrackUnflushed`, `CopyKeeping`), which is what makes the flush before the commit block observable. `a volume stopped around the log's wrap and its checkpoints` cuts every write around the first wrap and forced checkpoint of a 150-change run on `ext3` and `ext4`, and the unmount. `a split change stopped after any write` cuts extending writes, truncations, removals and renames onto a file with `LimitJournal` set so that they split, on `ext3-groups` (list) and `ext4` (orphan file, a fragmented file): every write within three of a flush plus an even sample. `an interrupted replay or orphan processing` stops the next mount at every write on five crash images with two or more transactions in the log and a pending entry. `random changes ... stopped at a random write` runs 8 seeds of 300 changes on `ext4` and `ext3` (half with limits, `{8, 24, 32}` or `{3, 8, 32}` so that changes split). `fscli ext-journal` writes ext3 and ext4 images with `cp-in`, `mkdir`, `mv`, `ln -s`, `ln` and `rm` and requires `e2fsck -fn` to exit 0, an empty journal and the right contents. The crash and model tests of volumes without a journal are unchanged and pass with the same cut counts (4d changed nothing under `ext/`, only the tests and `fscli`'s usage text).
    - **Departures from the plan:** The layouts that differ only in the log format are cut at every third write, not every one (the full run took 5 minutes under ASan). `TreeOf` leaves out the contents of files over 8 MiB (the images' own sparse files), which made the reads slow. The truncations of the split test fit one transaction on the volumes it uses, so the truncation split is covered by `tests/ext_orphan_tests.cpp` (4c) and not cut at every write; the extend and the removal splits are. Limits in the split test are 5 and 6 blocks (hard) and in the random test `{3, 8, 32}` for half of the limited seeds, because `{8, 24, 32}` splits nothing.
  - **Step 5 done: FeatherOS mounts ext3 and ext4 read-write, with a 5 s writeback** (design `docs/design/M22-P6-s5.md`). Kernel and harness only; `src/fs/core/` did not change. The full matrix passed at the end of the part.
    - **Mounts:** `ext3_driver` and `ext4_driver` (`filesystem/drivers.cpp`) are `Writable`, so `mount -t ext3 vda3` and `mount -t ext4 vda4` come up `rw` when the core takes writes, and `ro` with the core's log line when it refuses (errors recorded, unsupported journal features, a malformed orphan list, a replay that finds damage: that mounts read-only and returns 0). At boot they are still mounted read-only in the core; only a writable VFS mount replays the journal, a `-r` one never does. They are never the root (Part 6b).
    - **`Driver::Detach` and `SyncsInBackground`** (`filesystem/filesystem.{h,cpp}`; defaults `Sync()` and false). `CoreOptions {periodic_sync, close_on_detach}` (`core_driver.h`): `CoreDriver::Detach` of an ext3 or ext4 mount unmounts the core (`ExtFilesystemType::Unmount`: commit, checkpoint, clear `needs_recovery`, flush) and reopens it read-only, so the partition is clean after `umount` and at shutdown; a failed close leaves the mount in place (`EIO`), and after a journal abort `Detach` succeeds and writes nothing. For ext2 and FAT16 it is `Sync`. `SyncsInBackground` is true for ext2, ext3 and ext4 while mounted, writable in the core and not refusing writes. `VFS::Unmount` calls `Detach`; `VFS::DetachAll` (shutdown, `Files::Quiesce`, which now returns a `bool` that `power.cpp` logs as `KERN/W: Shutdown: a filesystem did not close cleanly`) and `VFS::SyncPeriodic` share a loop with `SyncAll`.
    - **Writeback (`src/kernel/writeback.{h,cpp}`):** a High-priority kernel task started in `RunConsole` after the network, sleeping to a tick deadline every `IntervalSeconds` (5, fixed, no `commit=` option) and calling `Files::SyncPeriodic` under `file_system_lock`; one `Writeback:` warning per failure streak. ext3/ext4 pass: a commit of the running transaction (nothing running writes nothing); ext2 pass: the core's `Sync`, which marks the volume clean only once its metadata is complete (invariant I12 in the design), so an idle pass writes no block. FAT16 and the virtual filesystems are not synced (its `Sync` is only a device flush).
    - **Tests:** host tests in `tests/unit/kernel/filesystem/filesystem_tests.cpp` (read-write mounts and unmounts clearing `needs_recovery`, a refused volume, an idle pass, a pass that commits what a stop would lose, `DetachAll`, a failed and then an aborted unmount, the ext2 root clean after a pass with the superblock written first by the next change, FAT16 untouched by a pass). `STORAGE` kernel test: `CheckExt3AndExt4(boot)` mounts both read-write and runs `WriteVolumeFirstBoot`/`ChangeVolumeSecondBoot` (the ext2 ones, now with a root path) on each; the first boot's shell session then mounts both, writes `/periodic.txt` on the root and on both, sleeps 8 s and is stopped without `sync` or `umount`, so the harness (`tools/smoke/cases/storage.py`, on copies) finds the root clean, the two files only in the logs (`needs_recovery` set, `e2fsck -fy` brings them), and boot 2 replays both journals (two `ext2: journal recovered` lines), mounts the root read-write and finds the root's file. Shell part `ext4.py` writes both partitions (`VerifyJournalledClean` after the session); `power.py` writes `/mnt/power.txt` on ext4 and `/power.txt` on the root before `shutdown`/`reboot` and checks the closed journal, the root and `e2fsck -fn`.
    - **Departures from the design:** after a reboot the power case cannot require the root's clean bit (the second boot's read-write mount clears it until the next writeback pass), so it checks `e2fsck -fn` and the file there and the bit only after a shutdown; `CoreDriver::Detach` logs `ext: filesystem could not be reopened read-only` without the type name; the host test for a refused volume sets the error state on the ext3 image, since changing the ext4 superblock without its checksum fails the mount outright. The matrix has not run yet.
    - **Later fix: virtio-blk polling.** The STORAGE test once failed on AArch64 with `journal aborted, no more writes: a flush failed`: both drivers polled a request at most 10^7 times (about 50 ms under TCG), and a flush, which waits for QEMU to sync the image file, can take longer under host load (measured up to 17 ms with host fsync load, 0.5 ms idle). A request now waits until the device answers, up to 30 s by the generic timer's counter on AArch64 and 2^31 `pause` polls on x86-64, after which the device is taken as failed for good, since the request it still holds would complete in place of a later one.
- **Part 6b: ext4 as root (done).** Switch the root partition to ext4. Steps:
  1. `root=vdaN` on the kernel command line names the root partition (FAT16, ext2, ext3 or ext4, whichever the partition holds); without it the root is the lowest-numbered partition of any of the four types. ext3 and ext4 can be the root, mounted read-write at boot (a journal needing recovery is replayed then). A `root=` that names no usable partition logs a warning and falls back to the default rule. `build.sh` passes `KERNEL_ROOT=vdaN` as `root=`. The test disk is unchanged in this step; a smoke check boots with `root=vda4` and writes the ext4 root.
     **(done)** `src/kernel/boot_options.{h,cpp}` (always built; `kmain` asks Limine for the kernel file's command line in every kernel, not only with `KERNEL_TESTS`, and calls `BootOptions::SelectRoot(ParseRoot(...))` before the heap exists; `NextWord`/`MatchesWord` are shared with `KernelTest::ParseCommandLine`): only `root=vda` plus a decimal number from 1 to 9999 is a partition, the last `root=` wins, any other `root=` is `Malformed` and logs `KERN/W: VFS: root= is not a partition such as vda1, using the default root`. `VFS::MountBlockDevice(requested = 0, bool* honored = nullptr)` considers FAT16, ext2, ext3 and ext4 (`VFS::ChooseRoot` picks the requested partition's volume, else the lowest-numbered one); if the requested one is missing or its `PrepareMount` fails, `*honored` is false, that volume is skipped and the default rule applies, and `MountAndVerifyDisk` logs `KERN/W: VFS: root=vda9 is not a usable partition, using the default root`. The read-only fallback is the same for every type (`PrepareMount(ReadWrite)` mounts read-only when the core refuses writes, so a refusing ext3/ext4 root logs the existing `read-only: the volume refused writes` warning); an ext3 or ext4 root is replayed by that read-write mount, is a `SyncsInBackground` mount, and `VFS::DetachAll` closes its journal at shutdown (nothing special-cased "/"). `build.sh`: `KERNEL_ROOT=vdaN` appends `root=vdaN` to the image's `cmdline:` line beside `test=`; `ROOT_TEST=ON` (default `vda4`) is the smoke toggle, `tools/matrix.py` cases 39-42 (`ext4_root` with `KERNEL_ROOT=vda4`, `unusable_root` with `KERNEL_ROOT=vda9`, both architectures). The kernel panics without `/BLOCK.TXT` on the root, which the test disk's ext3 and ext4 partitions lack, so `JournalledRoot.Start` (`tools/smoke/cases/root.py`) adds `BLOCK.TXT`, `/home` and `/bin` to the disk copy with `debugfs -w` before the boot (the shell and every program come from the initramfs, so nothing else is needed); the guest writes `/rootwrite.txt`, runs `shutdown`, and the harness checks the closed journal, `e2fsck -fn` and `debugfs cat` on the partition. `UnusableRoot` (a `NormalBoot`) checks the warning and the default `vda1 ext2` boot. Host tests: `tests/unit/kernel/boot_options_tests.cpp` (parser, `ChooseRoot`) and `filesystem_tests.cpp` (an ext4 root read-write, written, synced by `SyncPeriodic` and closed by `DetachAll`, a jbd2 replay of an ext3 root, the lowest-numbered of the four, fallbacks, a refusing root mounted read-only). Step 2 must move the `ROOT_VOLUMES` table of `root.py` along with the disk swap, and its `BLOCK.TXT`/`/home`/`/bin` patching is no longer needed for a partition that already holds the root's staging tree.

  2. The test disk swaps vda1 and vda4: vda1 is ext4 (the root's staging tree plus the ext4 test files, the depth-2 extent file included) and vda4 is ext2 (the old ext2 test files); the `STORAGE` kernel test, the shell parts (`mounts.py`, `ext2.py`, `ext4.py`), `power.py`, `boot.py` and the harness's `e2fsck`/`debugfs` checks follow, with the root's journal checked after the first `STORAGE` boot. The `root=` smoke check moves to another partition (an ext2 or ext3 root).
     **(done)** Harness, kernel test and `tools/create_test_disk.py` only; the kernel and `src/fs/core/` did not change. The full matrix (44 cases) passed at the end of the part, which completes M22; every item of **Complete when** below holds.
     - **Disk:** `vda1` (31 MiB) is `mke2fs -t ext4 -b 1024 -I 256` with the default features (extents, 64bit, flex_bg, metadata_csum, orphan_file, a 1 MiB journal; label `FEATHEROS`), made from the staging tree (`BLOCK.TXT`, `home`, `bin`) and `samples/` with the old ext4 files (`hello.txt`, the 2 MiB `extents.bin`, `docs/nested/deep.txt`, `link`), all owned by 0; the core wrote it without turning a feature off. `vda4` (8 MiB, the old ext4's range) is `-t ext2 -b 1024 -I 128` with the old root's `samples` files at its top level (`hello.txt`, `big.txt`, `docs/notes/deep.txt`, `fast-link`, `slow-link`, the long name), owned by 1000 and `hello.txt` 0640 in group 100. `vda2` FAT16 and `vda3` ext3 and every size are unchanged, so the disk is still 64 MiB; the constants are `ROOT_START`, `FAT16_START`, `EXT3_START`, `EXT2_START`, `EXT2_END`. MBR and GPT as before.
     - **`STORAGE`:** `CheckRoot` reads `/samples`, writes `/os` (`WriteVolumeFirstBoot`/`ChangeVolumeSecondBoot` on `""`) and syncs; `CheckExt3AndExt2` mounts ext2 `vda4` read-only (refuses changes, no link open) and then it and ext3 read-write at `/ext2` and `/ext3` (the wrong-type mounts, `ext2 vda1` and `ext3 vda1` among them, are `NotFound`), writes both and unmounts; `CheckRemount` (now last, with the data) unmounts the root and the mounts inside it, mounts it read-only (`/samples` and the boot's `/os` file read back, changes `EROFS`) and read-write again. Boot 1's shell session writes `/periodic.txt` on the root, ext3 and ext2, stops after 8 s; boot 2's root mount replays the root's journal (the harness still sees two `journal recovered` lines: the root's and ext3's), the kernel test finds ext2's file, replays ext3 and renames the root's and ext3's file. The harness (`storage.py`) checks ext2 clean plus `e2fsck -fn` and `debugfs` (`VerifyExt2`), and copies of the root and ext3 (`VerifyJournalled`: boot 1 `needs_recovery`, `/periodic.txt` only in the log, `ReplayOnCopy` then `-fn`; boot 2 ext3 clean as it is, the root, mounted again, replayed on the copy first).
     - **Shell and the rest:** `ext2.py` mounts `vda4` at `/mnt` (as ext4 it fails, `-r` is `ro` and refuses `write`, then `rw`) and runs the old `/samples` checks there (`2 directories, 5 files` because of `lost+found`), then unmounts; `ext4.py` runs the old ext4 checks on the root's `/samples` (`mount -t ext2 vda1` fails) and then ext3 at `/mnt`; the shell case replays a copy of the root after the session (`VerifyJournalledClean`, the interrupt part's `sync` commits it) and checks ext3 and ext2 clean. `power.py` writes `/power.txt` on the root and `/mnt/power.txt` on ext2: after a shutdown the root's journal is closed and clean, after a reboot (the root is mounted again, `needs_recovery` set) its copy is replayed first; ext2 is clean in both. `ROOT_VOLUMES` in `root.py` is now ext3 `vda3` and ext2 `vda4`, and both lack the root files, which `NamedRoot.Start` still adds with `debugfs`; the matrix cases are `ext3_root` (39-40, `KERNEL_ROOT=vda3`, also `ROOT_TEST`'s default), `unusable_root` (41-42) and a new `ext2_root` (43-44, `KERNEL_ROOT=vda4`), so an ext2 root, the old default, is still booted.
     - **Checks that no longer exist:** the one-instance-per-filesystem rule means no second ext4 can be mounted, so `mount -r -t ext4` and the kernel test's read-only mount of ext4 before its replay are gone (the ext3 one stays; the root's replay happens at boot, where its log line is the evidence), and the root cannot be mounted read-only from the shell. The `ext4 vda1` wrong-type check became `ext2 vda1`/`ext3 vda1`. The `mounts.py` listing now says `vda1 / ext4 rw 0 0`, and `ext4.py` does not print `/proc/mounts` for the root, which would match `mounts.py`'s listing regex.
- **Safety:** each driver mounts read-only until its write path passes the matrix, as in M19.

**Complete when:** on both targets, the root filesystem is ext4 from the shared core, FAT16 also comes from the core, ext2, ext3 and ext4 partitions on the virtio-blk disk are mounted, written and unmounted, the data survives a reboot, and `e2fsck -fn` finds no errors; on the host, `fscli` reads and writes the same images and the include check keeps `src/fs/core/` self-contained.

### 23. Pseudo-Terminals And Virtual Consoles (Complete)

**Summary:** The terminal is an object (`Terminals::Terminal`), with the line discipline, size, settings and foreground group that used to be global, and every process has a session and a controlling terminal behind `setsid`, `tcsetpgrp` and `/dev/tty`. Up to 16 pseudo-terminal pairs come from `/dev/ptmx` and `/dev/pts/N`, with window sizes and `SIGWINCH`, and hang-up with `SIGHUP` and end of file. `script` relays a terminal to a pty and logs the session, and `stty` shows and sets a terminal's size. Four virtual consoles, `/dev/tty1` to `/dev/tty4`, switch with Alt+F1 to Alt+F4 and each run their own shell from an init `console` service. Line editing, `vi`, `top`, Ctrl+C and jobs work the same on a console and on a pty. The shell test drives a nested `script` session, and the keyboard test runs a job on every console, checked through a kernel report of each console's foreground program.

The kernel has exactly one terminal today, and all of its state is global: the mode (`Console::Settings`), the size (`Console::Size`), the foreground process group (`foreground_group` in `process.cpp`), and Ctrl+C (`InterruptForegroundJob` in `console.cpp`). This milestone turns the terminal into an object, adds pseudo-terminals (ptys), and runs several consoles with a shell each. Later, every terminal window in a GUI holds a pty, and a remote shell (`docs/ideas.md`) is a relay between a socket and a pty.

**Terms:** a terminal has two sides. Programs (the shell, `vi`) read and write the *slave* side and see an ordinary terminal. The *master* side belongs to whatever plays the screen and keyboard: it reads what programs print and writes what the user types. Between the two, the kernel's *line discipline* applies the terminal's mode: echo, line editing in canonical mode, and Ctrl+C becoming `SIGINT` for the foreground group. For the console, the kernel itself is the master, drawing with `term.cpp` and reading the keyboard. For a pty, a user program is.

**End state, as on macOS:** terminal emulation leaves the kernel. macOS has no virtual consoles. Its kernel provides ptys and the line discipline, and Terminal.app, an ordinary program, parses escape sequences, draws the text and turns key presses into bytes. FeatherOS goes the same way with the GUI, where each terminal window is a program holding a pty master. It is safer: the VT100 parser reads bytes that any program can print (`cat` of a crafted file is enough), so a bug in it crashes one window instead of the kernel, and less code runs with full privileges. The kernel keeps a minimal text console for boot messages and panics, which cannot depend on user programs. M23 stops short of that move. Its virtual consoles still draw with `term.cpp` in the kernel, because drawing from user space needs a framebuffer and raw-input interface for programs, which only the GUI needs.

- **Part 1: the terminal object** (no change in behavior). The mode, size, foreground group, input queue and line discipline move out of `console.cpp` and `process.cpp` into a `Terminal` in `src/kernel/terminal/`, with an output sink and an input feed. The console becomes the first terminal, fed by the keyboard and serial and drawing through `term.cpp`. Each process gets a session and a controlling terminal, both inherited on spawn: `setsid` starts a session without a terminal, and the first terminal its leader opens becomes the controlling one. `TerminalMode`, `TerminalSize` and `ForegroundGroup` take a descriptor and act on the terminal behind it, so `tcsetattr`, `ioctl(TIOCGWINSZ)`, `tcsetpgrp` and `isatty` work on any terminal and fail on anything else. `/dev/tty` opens the caller's controlling terminal. Host tests cover the line discipline with scripted input and the session and foreground rules, and the matrix passes unchanged.
  1. **(Complete) The object:** `Terminals::Terminal` in `src/kernel/terminal/terminal.cpp` holds the input queue, the canonical line a read did not take (formerly per process), the settings and their owner, the size and the foreground group, and does the line discipline: canonical and raw reads, echo, output filtering and Ctrl+C, reaching processes through `Terminals::ProcessHooks`. It draws through a `Terminals::Device`; the console's is serial plus `term.cpp`, in `console.cpp`, which keeps the status bar. `Input` only feeds the console's terminal from the keyboards and serial. Host tests in `tests/unit/kernel/terminal/` drive the line discipline over a recording device.
  2. **(Complete) Sessions and descriptors:** each process has a session and a controlling terminal; the terminal keeps its session (`Terminal::Acquire`/`Release`) and applies the foreground rules (`AccessGroup`, `ForegroundGroupFor`, `ChangeForegroundGroup`). The kernel starts init in a session of its own without a terminal. Since no program runs between fork and exec, a `SpawnSessionLeader` child takes the terminal at its descriptor 0 if no session has it, which is how the shell gets the console; a session leader without a terminal also takes the first one it opens unless it passes `O_NOCTTY` (`OpenNoControllingTerminal`), which libc's `posix_spawn` file actions always pass. The terminal is released when its session's leader exits. `TerminalMode`, `TerminalSize` and `ForegroundGroup` take a descriptor (`SyscallErrorNotTerminal`, `ENOTTY`, on anything else). The foreground group's checks apply only on the caller's controlling terminal; `tcsetpgrp` there is for the session leader or the foreground group, to a group of the session. New `CreateSession` and `SessionId` syscalls behind `setsid` and `getsid`; `/dev/tty` opens the controlling terminal (`ENXIO` without one). Host tests cover the session rules and `/dev/tty`, and `libctest` checks sessions from the shell, a `setsid` child and a `POSIX_SPAWN_SETSID` child. Settings an exiting process left behind are still restored only on the console, which is the only terminal until Part 2.
- **Part 2: pseudo-terminals.** Opening `/dev/ptmx` creates a pair and returns its master, and the slave is `/dev/pts/N` (16 pairs at most). libc adds `posix_openpt`, `grantpt`, `unlockpt` and `ptsname`. Bytes written to the master are the slave's input, through the same line discipline as the keyboard, and what programs write to the slave is read from the master. `ioctl(TIOCSWINSZ)` on the master sets the size and sends a new `SIGWINCH` to the foreground group; the line editor, `vi` and `top` redraw on it. Closing the master sends `SIGHUP` to the session and ends reads on the slave, and once every slave descriptor is closed, master reads return end of file. Both sides work with `poll`. Host tests cover the data flow both ways, size changes and hangup, and `libctest` checks a pair from both ends.
  1. **(Complete) Pairs and data flow:** `src/kernel/terminal/pseudo_terminal.cpp` holds 16 pairs, each a `Terminals::Terminal` whose device writes into a 4 KiB pipe that the master reads. `/dev/ptmx` claims a pair (`Files::Kind::PseudoTerminalMaster`), and `/dev/pts/N` (listed while the pair is in use) opens its slave once unlocked, `SyscallErrorInputOutput` (`EIO`) before that or after the master closed. Master writes go through the line discipline as typed input (`Terminal::ReceiveAvailable`, waiting with `WaitForRoom` while the queue is full); slave output waits for room in the pipe (`Device::write_output`, so `Terminal::Write` now returns a count), while echo that does not fit is dropped. A pair is reused once its master and slave descriptors, `/dev/tty` included, are closed and no session holds its terminal. New `TerminalControl` syscall (pair number, lock), libc `posix_openpt`, `grantpt`, `unlockpt`, `ptsname`, `ptsname_r`; the master works with `O_NONBLOCK` and `poll`. Host tests in `tests/unit/kernel/terminal/pseudo_terminal_tests.cpp`, `libctest` checks a pair from both ends. A pair's device is set when it is claimed, since GCC 14 leaves a `constinit` array of self-referencing objects zero-filled.
  2. **(Complete) Window size:** `ioctl(TIOCSWINSZ)` on the master or the slave (or any terminal, by anyone, as on Linux) sets the size through the new `TerminalControlSetSize` request, and `TIOCGWINSZ` works on the master too. A change, and only a change, sends `SIGWINCH` (28, ignored by default) to the foreground group through the new `ProcessHooks::signal_group`. `src/user/window_size.h` catches it for the shell's line editor (`LineEditor::Resize` redraws from the prompt's row), `vi` (a full redraw) and `top` (the last table again, without waiting out the interval). Host tests cover the signal rules and the line editor's redraw; `libctest` sets the size from both ends and checks that a child leading a session on the slave gets `SIGWINCH`. The redraws themselves are driven in QEMU once `script` exists (Part 3).
  3. **(Complete) Hang-up and end of file:** closing the master calls the new `Terminal::HangUp` before the pair counts as closed: queued input is dropped, no session or group has the terminal any more, and the new `ProcessHooks::hang_up_session` clears the controlling terminal of every process in the session and sends each `SIGHUP`. Afterwards slave reads return 0, slave writes (including one waiting for room in the pipe) and `ReadLine` fail with `EIO`, `Acquire` fails, a reset of the settings draws nothing, and `poll` reports the slave readable, writable and `POLLHUP`. Each slave descriptor holds a write end of the output pipe (`Pipes::AddWriter`), the pair holding one only until its slave first opens, so master reads return end of file, and `poll` `POLLHUP`, once every slave descriptor is closed, and wait again after a reopen. As the master's close hangs up the session, a pair is freed as soon as its last descriptor closes. `PseudoTerminals::ReleaseSettings` restores what an exiting process left on any pty, as the console already did. Host tests cover the hang-up on the terminal and through a pair, a waiting slave writer, and master end of file; `libctest` checks end of file and a session-leading child that sees `SIGHUP`, end of file, `EIO` and `ENXIO` for `/dev/tty`.
- **Part 3: `script`.** `script [-c command] [file]` runs the shell, or a command, on a new pty. It puts its own terminal in raw mode, relays it to the master and saves the session to a file. Its relay loop is the same as a GUI terminal window's or a remote shell's, minus the drawing. The shell test runs `script` on both targets and drives line editing, `vi`, Ctrl+C, `&`/`fg` and a size change inside it, then checks that the console's own jobs stayed separate and that the file holds the session.
  1. **(Complete) `script` and `stty`:** `src/user/script.cpp` relays its own terminal, in raw mode, to a pty master with `poll`, keeping the master non-blocking so a session busy writing cannot stall its input, logs the output between `Script started on` and `Script done on` lines (`-a` appends, `-q` hides the messages), copies its size to the pty at the start and on `SIGWINCH`, and ends at the master's end of file or once `waitpid` finds the shell gone, as no `SIGCHLD` handler wakes it; it then closes the master and exits with the shell's status. `-c` runs `shell -c`, a new shell option that runs one command line and returns its status. `stty` (`src/user/stty.cpp`) shows a terminal's size and modes and sets the size, with `-F` for another terminal. The shell test runs `script` with line editing, a job brought back with `fg`, Ctrl+C, `vi`, `stty` and a nested `script` that follows the outer pty's size, set through `stty -F /dev/pts/0`, then checks that the console shell kept its own job and that the file holds the session.
- **Part 4: virtual consoles.** Four kernel terminals, each with its own `term.cpp` screen (text, cursor, alternate screen) and its own input. Alt+F1 to Alt+F4 switch which one is shown and gets the keys; a hidden console updates its buffer without drawing. Serial stays with the first console. init's `console` services name a terminal (`/dev/tty1` to `/dev/tty4`) and start a shell on each, each in its own session. The status bar shows the shown console's number and foreground program. The QEMU test switches consoles with the monitor's `sendkey`, runs a job on each, and checks through a kernel test hook which console is shown and that each one's foreground program is its own.
  1. **(Complete) Consoles and switching:** `src/kernel/console.cpp` holds four `Terminals::Terminal`s, each drawing on its own `Term` (one per console in `kernel_runtime.cpp`, sharing the framebuffer under one display lock), with serial and kernel messages on the first. `Term::SetShown` keeps a hidden screen's text without drawing and repaints it whole when shown. The keyboard tracks both Alt keys and turns Alt+F1 to Alt+F4 into a console request (`Keyboard::TakeConsoleSwitch`) instead of bytes; both keyboard drivers go through the new `Input::SubmitKey`, which calls `Console::Show`, while serial input always goes to the first console (`Input::SetKeyboardTerminal` moves only the keys). `/dev/tty1` to `/dev/tty4` open the consoles' terminals (`Devices::Device::Console`, registered by `Devices::SetConsoles`). The status bar names the shown console and its foreground program (`tty2  vi`), and settings an exiting process left are restored on every console. Host tests cover Alt+Fn, the input routing, hidden screens and `/dev/ttyN`; the input test types two keys after Alt+F2 that must not reach the first console's shell, and the shell test lists `tty4`.
  2. **(Complete) Shells on every console:** a `console` line in `init.conf` names its terminal before its command (`console /dev/tty2 shell`), at most one per terminal. init opens it on the child's descriptors 0 to 2 with `posix_spawn` file actions, and `POSIX_SPAWN_SETSID` makes the child take it as its controlling terminal, so the default configuration runs a shell in its own session on each of `/dev/tty1` to `/dev/tty4`. `INPUT_TEST` now also builds the kernel with `FEATHEROS_INPUT_TEST`, whose `KernelTest::StartConsoleReports` task writes the shown console and each console's foreground program to serial whenever they change (`KERN/I: Consoles: tty2 shown; shell, sleep, shell, shell`). After `keys`, the keyboard test starts `sleep 60` on the second console, `top` on the third and `vi` on the fourth with `sendkey`, interrupts the second one's `sleep` with Ctrl+C, and returns to the first, waiting for each report. Nothing typed on the others may reach serial. Host tests cover the new `console` lines.

**After M23, with the GUI:** `term.cpp`'s VT100 code moves into a user-space terminal library, each terminal window holds a pty master, and the kernel's virtual consoles shrink to the boot and panic console. Ctrl+Z and the stop signals moved to M24.

**Complete when:** on both targets, four consoles each run their own shell and jobs, a program on a pty behaves as on the console (line editing, `vi`, Ctrl+C, the window size), and the matrix covers both.

### 24. Stopping Jobs With Ctrl+Z (Complete)

**Summary:** Processes can stop: `SIGSTOP`, `SIGTSTP`, `SIGTTIN` and `SIGTTOU` stop every thread of a process until `SIGCONT`, the parent reads both changes with `WUNTRACED` and `WCONTINUED`, an orphaned group ignores the terminal's stop signals, and a syscall that only a stop interrupted starts again. Ctrl+Z sends `SIGTSTP` to the foreground group, and the shell keeps stopped jobs with their terminal settings, listed by `jobs` and continued by `fg` and `bg`. A background group that reads its controlling terminal stops with `SIGTTIN`, and one that changes its mode or foreground group with `SIGTTOU`, while one that ignores or blocks the signal gets `EIO` for a read and goes ahead with a change. `vi`, `top` and the shell's line editor see Ctrl+Z as a key in raw mode, or catch `SIGTSTP`, and stop through `RawTerminal::Suspend`, which puts the terminal back first and raw mode again once continued, so each redraws as it was. libctest, the shell test (on the console and inside `script`) and the keyboard test (on a second console) cover all of it on both targets.

M11 left out the stop signals because a process could not be stopped: Ctrl+C is the only way to get the prompt back from a foreground program, and a background job that reads the terminal just waits. This milestone adds stopped processes and the job control built on them, as on other Unix systems: Ctrl+Z stops the foreground job, `jobs` lists it as stopped, and `fg` or `bg` continues it in the foreground or the background.

**Terms:** the stop signals are `SIGSTOP` (19), which cannot be caught, blocked or ignored, `SIGTSTP` (20, Ctrl+Z), and `SIGTTIN` (21) and `SIGTTOU` (22), which the terminal sends to a background job that reads it or changes its settings. Their default action stops every thread of the process; `SIGCONT` (18) continues it, whatever its action. The parent learns of both through `waitpid` with `WUNTRACED` and `WCONTINUED`. A group whose members have no parent in another group of the same session is *orphaned*: nobody there could continue it, so the three terminal stop signals do not stop it.

- **Part 1: stopped processes.** Signals 19-22 become valid, with a new `Stop` decision. A thread that takes one stops the process and parks on its way back to user mode, as does every other thread of the process the next time it gets there; `SIGCONT` releases them, `SIGKILL` ends them while stopped. Sending a stop signal drops a pending `SIGCONT` and the reverse; the terminal stop signals are dropped for an orphaned group. The parent gets `SIGCHLD` for each change and reads it once with new `Wait` flags. `/proc` and `ps` show a stopped process as `T`. libc gains the four signals, `WUNTRACED`, `WCONTINUED`, `WIFSTOPPED`, `WSTOPSIG` and `WIFCONTINUED`, and `kill` the names. Host tests cover the decisions; `libctest` stops, continues and kills a child, and checks the reports and the orphan rule. Ctrl+Z waits for Part 2, since a shell that cannot see its job stop would hang.
  1. **(Complete)** `Signals::DecisionKind::Stop` for `SIGSTOP` (unblockable, like `SIGKILL`) and the default action of `SIGTSTP`, `SIGTTIN` and `SIGTTOU`; `Signals::Send` drops a pending `SIGCONT` for a stop signal and the reverse. Each architecture's `DeliverSignals` loops over `Process::HoldWhileStopped` (a thread whose context is `stopped` waits on the process's `resumed` queue until `SIGCONT` clears it or `SIGKILL` is pending) and `Process::Stop`, which marks the process and all thread contexts, unless a terminal stop signal reached an orphaned group (`GroupOrphanedLocked`). `SIGCONT` continues the process in `SendSignalLocked` whatever its action. Both report to the parent (`ProcessRecord::report`, `SIGCHLD`, every parent thread woken), and `Wait` takes `WaitStopped`/`WaitContinued` and returns `WaitResultStopped`/`WaitResultContinued`; `Scheduler::Wait`'s callback got a context for that. A syscall that only a stop interrupted starts again: the return address moves back over the syscall instruction and the number and first argument come back, with `Sleep` storing the time left in `UserExecutionContext::restart_argument`. `/proc` shows `T (stopped)`. Host tests cover the decisions and `SIGSTOP`'s rules; `libctest` checks `SIGTSTP` with its report and `/proc` state, a single report, `SIGCONT` with `WCONTINUED`, a sleep that survives a stop, `waitpid(-1, WUNTRACED)`, a signal held until `SIGCONT`, `SIGKILL` while stopped, and an orphaned session leader that keeps running.
- **Part 2: Ctrl+Z and the shell's job control.** The line discipline turns Ctrl+Z into `SIGTSTP` for the foreground group, as Ctrl+C becomes `SIGINT`, echoing `^Z`. The shell waits for its foreground job with `WUNTRACED`, and when the job stops takes the terminal back, saves the job's terminal settings and prints `[1] Stopped  vi`. `jobs` shows each job as running or stopped, `fg` gives the terminal and the saved settings back and sends `SIGCONT`, and a new `bg` continues a stopped job in the background. Host tests cover Ctrl+Z in the line discipline; the shell test stops `sleep` with Ctrl+Z, lists it, continues it with `bg` and `fg`, and stops and continues a pipeline.
  1. **(Complete)** `Terminals::SuspendByte` (Ctrl+Z, `0x1A`) goes through the same path as Ctrl+C (`Terminal::DeliverJobSignal`), with `ProcessHooks::interrupt_group` and `Process::InterruptGroup` taking the signal, from the keyboard, serial and pty masters alike; with `ISIG` off it is queued as a byte, and the kernel's line editing ignores it. libc reports it as `c_cc[VSUSP]`. `Process::Stop` became `Process::TakeSignal`, which takes the next signal and stops the process under the process lock, so a `SIGCONT` sent in between is no longer lost. The shell keeps stopped jobs in its job table (`stopped`, saved `termios`, a use counter for `fg` and `bg` without a number): `ReapJob` waits with `WUNTRACED | WCONTINUED`, `WaitInForeground` takes the terminal and the shell's own settings back when a job stops and prints `[n] Stopped  <command>`, `fg` restores the job's settings, gives it the terminal and sends `SIGCONT`, `bg` sends `SIGCONT` and prints `[n] <command> &`, `jobs` shows `Running` or `Stopped`, `wait` skips stopped jobs, and `exit` sends stopped jobs `SIGHUP` and `SIGCONT`. The shell does not ignore the stop signals, which its children would inherit: as a session leader it never gets Ctrl+Z, and its group is orphaned. `RawTerminal` now reads one byte at a time, as readline does, so a line typed right after another is not cut short. Host tests cover Ctrl+Z from `Receive` and `ReceiveAvailable`, with signals off, and in line editing. The shell test stops `sleep 4`, lists it, continues it with `bg`, waits for it with `fg`, and stops a pipeline that `fg 1` brings back, and inside `script` it stops a `sleep` on the pty. The harness also stopped counting a prompt inside `grep`'s output of the `script` file, which had made it type every later line one prompt early, and checks `date` against the whole session's time span.
- **Part 3: background access to the terminal.** A background group that reads its controlling terminal gets `SIGTTIN`, failing with `EIO` instead when it ignores or blocks the signal; one that changes the settings or the foreground group gets `SIGTTOU`, unless it ignores or blocks it, when the change goes ahead (so the shell can call `tcsetpgrp` from the background). Output from the background stays allowed, as `stty -tostop` does elsewhere. An orphaned group's reads and changes get `EIO` without the signal. The shell test starts a reading job with `&` and finds it stopped.
  1. **(Complete)** `Terminals::DecideBackgroundAccess` (host-tested) picks among going ahead, the signal and `EIO`, and `CheckBackgroundAccess` in `process.cpp` applies it for `Read` and `ReadLine` on the controlling terminal (`SIGTTIN`), `TerminalMode` changes and `ForegroundGroup` moves (`SIGTTOU`): it sends the signal to the caller's group and fails with `SyscallErrorInterrupted`, which the stop turns into a restart, so the call runs again once the job continues. `ChangeForegroundGroup` no longer checks the foreground group itself, and `Terminals::Caller` lost `session_leader`. The shell blocks `SIGTTOU` while it takes the terminal back from a stopped job, as its group is orphaned. `kill` follows `SIGTERM` and `SIGHUP` to a group with `SIGCONT`, as shells do for `kill %n`, so a stopped job ends. libctest stops a background `upper` with `SIGTTIN`, fails a read that ignores `SIGTTIN` with `EIO`, stops a background `tcsetattr` with `SIGTTOU` and lets it finish once the job has the terminal, and lets one that blocks `SIGTTOU` go ahead. The shell test stops `upper &`, which reads once `fg` gives it the console, and ends a stopped `upper &` with `kill %1`.
- **Part 4: programs that draw.** The shell's line editor, `vi` and `top` catch `SIGTSTP`: they put the terminal back as they found it, leave the alternate screen, stop themselves, and on `SIGCONT` set their mode again and redraw. The shell test stops and continues `vi` and `top` on the console and inside `script`, and the keyboard test stops a job on a second console.
  1. **(Complete)** Raw mode turns `ISIG` off, so Ctrl+Z reaches these programs as a key rather than as `SIGTSTP`; `RawTerminal` also catches `SIGTSTP` while active and hands it over as the same key. `RawTerminal::Suspend` restores the settings it found, sets `SIGTSTP` back to its default and sends it with `kill(0, SIGTSTP)`, which returns once the group continues (at once for an orphaned group), then puts raw mode back; a job continued with `bg` stops again with `SIGTTOU` there. `vi` leaves the alternate screen around it and redraws through `ViEditor::Resize`, `top` also shows the cursor and redraws its last table, and the shell's `LineEditor` gets a `Suspended` status that prints `^Z` and keeps the line for `Redraw`; a shell that leads its session ignores the key. The shell test stops `vi` (then saves with `:wq`), `top` and a nested shell on the console, and `vi` and `top` inside `script`; the keyboard test stops `sleep` on the second console with Ctrl+Z, continues it with `fg` and ends it with Ctrl+C.

**Complete when:** on both targets, Ctrl+Z stops a foreground job on a console and on a pty, `jobs`, `fg` and `bg` manage it, `vi` and `top` come back as they were, a background reader stops instead of waiting, and the matrix covers all of it.

### 25. Swift Beyond The Embedded Defaults (Complete)

**Summary:** Embedded Swift on FeatherOS now has everything a library can add back. The concurrency runtime gained the clocks and `Task.sleep`, `MainActor` with top-level `await`, and custom serial and task executors. `FeatherSupport` calls the C library's variadic functions through fixed parameters and `String(format:)`. C++ headers import into Swift programs, with the `Cxx` overlay built from source. The standard library itself is built from source, which made room for `print(_:to:)`, `AnyHashable`, `withVaList` and a `Codable` of FeatherOS's own over `EncoderBase`/`DecoderBase`, with `JSONEncoder` and `JSONDecoder`. Swift's `Regex` turned out to need run-time type metadata, so regular expressions came as the C library's POSIX `regex.h` instead, with `grep -E`/`-F`/`-o` and `FeatherSupport`'s `RegularExpression`. What is still missing needs the compiler's type metadata and is listed in `docs/development.md` ("What Embedded Swift leaves out"); M27 brings it back by leaving Embedded Swift for the full language.

M21 left a list of what Embedded Swift 6.4.2 does without ("What Embedded Swift leaves out" in `docs/development.md`). This milestone adds back everything on it that a library can provide. We already build `_Concurrency` from source, so a patch can add back what upstream only marks unavailable under Embedded Swift. Since Part 6 the standard library is built from source too, so the same holds for the parts that live there.

**Out of scope:** the compiler itself rejects these, or they need type metadata that Embedded Swift never emits (checked with swiftc 6.4.2 against both targets). M27 brings back all but Foundation and compiling Swift on FeatherOS by leaving Embedded Swift:
- `weak`/`unowned` ("attribute 'weak' cannot be used in Embedded Swift").
- Key paths passed as values ("cannot use key path in embedded Swift").
- Casts to protocol types (the compiler warns and the cast never succeeds).
- `Mirror` and other reflection.
- Generic class methods that are not `final`.
- Generic methods called through an existential, and parameter packs, which Embedded Swift cannot specialize.
- Foundation and the `Distributed` module, which are built on reflection.
- Compiling Swift on FeatherOS.

**Probe results that shape the plan:**
- Top-level `await` crashed swiftc only because top-level code runs on `MainActor`, which Embedded Swift leaves out outside WASI. With `MainActor.swift` built into our `_Concurrency`, the same file compiles (Part 2 confirmed it).
- `Duration` is already in the Embedded standard library.
- Swift can already import a C++ header with `-cxx-interoperability-mode=default`.
- `withVaList` and `CVarArg` are missing, as are `print(_:to:)`, `String(describing:)`, `AnyHashable` and `Codable`.

- **Part 1: clocks and sleeping (done).** `swift.patch` drops `@_unavailableInEmbedded` from `ContinuousClock`, `SuspendingClock`, `Task.sleep(nanoseconds:)`, `Task.sleep(for:)`, `Task.sleep(until:)` and `Task.checkCancellation()`. The fallback that turned a generic clock's instant into a timestamp cast the clock to `ContinuousClock` or `SuspendingClock`, which Embedded Swift cannot compile even in a generic body. The patch gives those two clocks an internal `_TimestampingClock` protocol that converts their own instants, and `Task._sleep` takes only such clocks. `Clock.cpp` already read both through `clock_gettime(CLOCK_MONOTONIC)`. A sleep becomes a delayed job in `global_executor.cpp`, and a cancelled one resumes at once with `CancellationError`, leaving the wake-up job to find the sleep already over. `tasks` sleeps 20, 30 and 50 ms through all three `Task.sleep` forms and checks against `ContinuousClock` that at least 100 ms passed. It also cancels a 60 s sleep, which must end in under a second with `CancellationError`, and catches `CancellationError` from `Task.checkCancellation()`. The shell smoke test checks these lines on both targets.
- **Part 2: `MainActor` and top-level `await` (done).** `swift.patch` compiles `MainActor.swift` when `_Concurrency` is built with `-D FEATHEROS`, as it is for WASI, and its `swift_task_enqueue` sends the main executor's jobs to `swift_task_enqueueMainExecutor` instead of reaching the custom-executor trap. `global_executor.cpp` gives the main executor a queue of its own, which only the main thread runs in `swift_task_asyncMainDrainQueue`, and an identity of its own, so `isMainExecutor` no longer treats the generic executor as the main one. Its `checkIsolated` and `isIsolatingCurrentContext` ask whether the caller is on the main thread. Since the main thread now runs only `MainActor`'s jobs, there is a worker for every CPU instead of every CPU but one. Concurrency programs are built without `-parse-as-library`, and `tasks` and `taskprimes` are top-level code. `tasks` checks, against the main thread's `pthread_self`, that top-level code and `MainActor.assumeIsolated` run on the main thread, and that a nonisolated async function runs on a worker, from which a `@MainActor` function, `MainActor.run` (calling `assumeIsolated`) and a `@MainActor` task reach the main thread, while a detached task does not. The shell smoke test checks these lines on both targets.
- **Part 3: custom executors (done).** The runtime reaches an executor written in Swift through functions that upstream writes as generics over the executor's type, which Embedded Swift emits only into programs that call them, so `platform.cpp` had stubs that aborted. `src/user/swift_concurrency/executors.swift`, compiled once into the runtime's archive, replaces them: it rebuilds an `any SerialExecutor` or `any TaskExecutor` from an executor reference's object and witness table, then enqueues a job, asks `isIsolatingCurrentContext` or `checkIsolated`, or returns the executor's own reference. `swift.patch` sends `swift_task_enqueue`'s jobs for custom serial executors and preferred task executors there instead of `swift_unreachable`, accepts tasks created with an owned task executor, and makes `swift_task_isCurrentExecutor` ask the expected executor's `isIsolatingCurrentContext`, then its `checkIsolated`, with Swift 6's crashing checks. It makes `withTaskExecutorPreference`, `addTask(executorPreference:)`, `globalConcurrentExecutor` (the runtime's own global executor), running a job on a task executor and `UnsafeCurrentTask.unownedTaskExecutor` available, as well as `preconditionIsolated`, `assertIsolated` and `assumeIsolated` for actors and serial executors, whose failure messages under Embedded Swift leave out the executor they cannot describe. Complex equality stays out: `isSameExclusiveExecutionContext(other: Self)` needs the executor's type at run time, so such executors are checked through `isIsolatingCurrentContext` and `checkIsolated` alone. `executors` runs an actor on a thread of its own (100 calls from a task group, and a resume after `Task.sleep`), passes `assumeIsolated` on that thread outside any job through `isIsolatingCurrentContext` and, for an executor that cannot tell, `checkIsolated`, and runs 16 tasks from `addTask(executorPreference:)` on a pool of two threads. Under `withTaskExecutorPreference` a nonisolated function, `async let` and a default actor run on the pool, while the actor with its own executor, an unstructured `Task` and a child task under `globalConcurrentExecutor` (no preference) do not, and `Task(executorPreference:)` runs on the pool. The shell smoke test checks every line on both targets.
- **Part 4: the C library from Swift (done).** `FeatherSupport` has `CommandLine` (`argc`, `unsafeArgv`, `arguments`) over `featheros_argument_count`/`featheros_argument_values`, and `Arguments` builds on it; its `errno` was already settable. It defines `open`, `openat`, `fcntl` and `ioctl` with the fixed parameters of Glibc's Swift overlay (`src/user/swift_support/variadic_functions.swift`), calling new libc entry points `featheros_open`, `featheros_openat`, `featheros_fcntl` and `featheros_ioctl`, which the variadic functions now call too; overload resolution prefers them to the unavailable C declarations. libc gains `openat` with `AT_FDCWD`: since directories cannot be opened, a relative path with any other descriptor fails with `ENOTDIR` (or `EBADF`). `String(format:_:)` (`format.swift`) turns each `FormatArgument` (integers, floating point, strings, pointers) into a tagged `struct featheros_format_argument` (`<bits/featheros_format.h>`, included by `featheros.h`) for `featheros_format`, an `snprintf` over an array. The engine in `format.h` now reads its arguments through a source: `VariadicArguments` for `printf`'s `va_list`, `TaggedArguments` for the array, where integers keep 64 bits unless `hh`/`h` asks for fewer and a conversion without a suitable argument is written as it appears. `hello` checks `CommandLine`, `String(format:)` and the four calls, `rev -n` numbers lines with `String(format:)`, `libctest` checks the new C functions (512 checks), and host tests cover the tagged path.
- **Part 5: C++ interoperability (done).** Programs listed in `FEATHEROS_SWIFT_CXX_PROGRAMS` are compiled with `-cxx-interoperability-mode=default` (C++23, no exceptions or RTTI) and import the C++ modules of `src/user/swift_cxx/module.modulemap`; GCC builds the C++ they call, listed per program in `FEATHEROS_SWIFT_CXX_SOURCES_<name>` or shared in `featheros_user_support`, and the two meet through the Itanium ABI. Clang reads libc++'s headers from `deps/llvm-project` ahead of the C library's. Swift's `Cxx` overlay, which upstream builds only outside Embedded Swift, is built from `deps/swift/stdlib/public/Cxx` (`src/user/swift_cxx/CMakeLists.txt`); `swift.patch` adds the `import Builtin` it needs. libc gains aligned `operator new`/`delete` and `std::__libcpp_verbose_abort`, which libc++'s containers call. `cxxinterop` checks namespaces, constants, `enum class`, structs, operators, a class template, constructors and destructors, a move-only class, a virtual method, a shared reference released by Swift, `std::optional`, `std::vector`, `std::span` and `std::string_view`. `http` (`url`, `request`, `response`) resolves URLs and parses request heads and responses with `http_message.cpp`. Out: `std::string` and the rest of libc++'s compiled library (its members are extern templates), the `CxxStdlib` overlay (it needs libc++'s generated module map), passing a derived class as its base, and retain or release functions outside the global scope. The shell smoke test checks both programs on both targets; "Swift and C++" in `docs/development.md` has the details.
- **Part 6: the standard library from source (done).** `src/user/swift_stdlib/` builds the Embedded `Swift` module from `deps/swift/stdlib/public/core` (now in the sparse clone, with gyb's `SwiftIntTypes.py` and `SwiftFloatingPointTypes.py`), from upstream's own list of `EMBEDDED` files and with upstream's flags, and every Swift module and program finds it first through `-I`; the availability macros moved there from the concurrency build. Unpatched, it gives the same machine code as the toolchain's module, and with `swift.patch` `swiftbench` is still the same code on both targets (benchmark run in `docs/development.md`). Results per item ("Swift's standard library" in `docs/development.md`):
  - `print(_:separator:terminator:to:)`: go, over `any CustomStringConvertible...`, since upstream's `Any...` needs protocol casts. Several items to standard output went into `FeatherSupport` instead: as a non-generic function in the standard library it changed the library's inlining into every program (3 KB more in `swiftbench`) and made the module build warn.
  - `String(describing:)`: already available for `CustomStringConvertible` and `TextOutputStreamable` values; the unconstrained one and the descriptions of generic types (`Array`, `Optional`) need protocol casts, so they stay out.
  - `AnyHashable`: go, with a box whose equality casts to the other side's concrete box instead of upstream's generic `_unbox`; no custom representations or upcasts to a `Hashable` superclass, and `base as? T` instead of `as? T`.
  - `withVaList`/`CVarArg`: go, `VarArgs.swift` added to the Embedded build, with a `_cVarArgPassedAsDouble` requirement in place of a protocol cast.
  - `Codable`: go, but not upstream's protocols, since the synthesized `encode(to:)` calls `container(keyedBy:)`, a generic requirement, through `any Encoder`. A prototype passed: `Encoder`/`Decoder` bound to `EncoderBase`/`DecoderBase` classes with non-generic virtual methods per primitive and for nested encoders, final generic `container(keyedBy:)`, struct containers, and a non-generic `_encodePrimitive` requirement for the fast path; synthesized structs, enums with payloads, raw-value enums, optionals and arrays round-tripped at `-Onone` and `-O`.
  - `hello` checks `print(_:to:)`, `AnyHashable` and `withVaList` with more arguments than registers, on both targets.
- **Part 7: `Codable` (done).** `src/user/swift_stdlib/codable.swift` is compiled into the `Swift` module, and `swift.patch` drops `EmbeddedStubs.swift`'s unavailable stubs, lists the file in `GroupInfo.json`, and makes upstream's range, `Duration`, `Never` and `CollectionDifference` conformances available (a range's error leaves out its bounds). `Encoder` and `Decoder` are protocols bound to the classes `EncoderBase` and `DecoderBase`, so the compiler still synthesizes conformances. A format subclasses them and overrides methods over concrete types, each given a `CodingSlot` (`.value`, `.key`, `.element`); `container(keyedBy:)` and the others are final, and the keyed, unkeyed and single-value containers are structs with upstream's members, nested containers, `superEncoder`/`superDecoder`, `userInfo` and coding paths. On decoding, `DecoderBase` moves past an element only once it decoded, so a failed one can be retried, as with Foundation. Every conformance upstream has is there, with `SIMD2` to `SIMD64` conforming one by one; `Dictionary` finds `CodingKeyRepresentable` keys through `Encodable`/`Decodable` requirements with defaults instead of protocol casts. Errors have upstream's cases and descriptions without the type or value. The warning on each synthesized conformance cannot be avoided: the compiler calls `container(keyedBy:)` by its full name through `any Encoder`, opening the existential, and swiftc 6.4.2 has no flag that ignores a warning group; hand-written code silences it with `@diagnose(EmbeddedRestrictions, as: ignored)`. `json`'s value, parser and printers moved into `FeatherSupport` (`swiftbench` uses them from there, 2 KB smaller; the benchmark ratios moved within noise), with `JSONEncoder` (bytes, `.prettyPrinted`, `.sortedKeys`) and `JSONDecoder` (bytes or a `JSONValue`) over them. `json -t type` decodes each result into the Swift types a type expression names (`uint8`, `[string]`, `{double?}`) and encodes it again. The host tests build the `Swift` module for the host through `featheros_add_swift_stdlib` (`src/user/swift_stdlib/swift_stdlib.cmake`) and run `tests/unit/swift/codable_tests.swift` in Embedded Swift at `-Onone` and `-O` (CTest `swift.codable.Onone`/`.O`); `hello` round-trips a synthesized struct on both targets, and the smoke test checks it and `json -t`. Programs at `-Onone` outgrew the 1 MiB program limit, so the initramfs copies are now stripped of symbols too (`--strip-all`), and the shell smoke test's limit went from 180 s to 300 s, since the session took about 3 minutes.
- **Part 8: regular expressions (done).** Swift's `Regex` is a no-go under Embedded Swift: upstream's `_StringProcessing` (swift-experimental-string-processing at `swift-6.4.2-RELEASE`) builds a match's output type while the program runs (`TypeConstruction.tuple(of:)` over `swift_getTupleTypeMetadata`) from captures held as `Any` with their `Any.Type`, casts it to `Output`, and reads captures through key paths. Regex literals themselves compile against a `_StringProcessing` module of our own (swiftc parses the literal and calls `Regex<Output>(_regexString:version:)` with `Output` already a tuple of `Substring` and `Substring?`), but they are left for M27's full Swift rather than a `Regex` type of our own. Instead, the C library gained POSIX `regex.h` (`regcomp`, `regexec`, `regerror`, `regfree`; basic and extended syntax, `REG_ICASE`, `REG_NEWLINE`, `REG_NOSUB`, `REG_NOTBOL`, `REG_NOTEOL`, back-references, glibc's values) over `src/libc/regex_engine.cpp`. A pattern compiles to a program of instructions with relative offsets, so intervals copy their operand and alternation inserts its splits in place. A Thompson simulation finds the leftmost-longest match in time proportional to the text times the program, then a depth-first search that remembers the states it tried finds the subexpressions within that match, in left-to-right order rather than POSIX's longest-each (documented in `docs/posix.md`); loops check that an iteration moved, and a group clears the subexpressions nested in it. Patterns with back-references are searched by backtracking from each start, with a step budget that ends in `REG_ESPACE`. `grep` now takes basic expressions by default, as POSIX's does, with `-E` for extended ones, `-F` for plain text (escaped into a basic expression) and `-o` for the matched parts; `ParseFlags` takes uppercase letters. `FeatherSupport` gained `RegularExpression` (`matches`, `firstMatch`, `allMatches`, a `Match` with `String.Index` ranges and optional groups), and the Swift program `regex` prints matches with their subexpressions. Host tests cover the engine (13 cases under ASan and UBSan: leftmost-longest, subexpressions, nested and empty repetitions, linear time on `(a*)*b`, brackets, basic syntax's plain characters, back-references, case, newlines, every error code) and `grep -F`'s escaping; the shell smoke test runs `grep` with basic, extended and plain patterns and `regex` with groups that took no part, on both targets.

**Complete when:**
- Every item above is available on both targets, or documented as no-go with the reason.
- "What Embedded Swift leaves out" lists only the out-of-scope items.
- The matrix covers the new programs.

### 26. A C++ Standard Library Of Our Own (Complete)

**Summary:** FeatherOS has its own C++ standard library: a C++23 subset in `src/libcxx/include/`, read by the kernel (the parts that need no allocation or C library), the C library, every C++ program, Swift's C++ programs (as Clang module `std`, under upstream's unchanged `CxxStdlib` overlay) and both Swift runtimes, with what is not a template in the static library `featheros_cxx`. It has the language-support headers, the vocabulary types, the containers and algorithms (`vector`, `string`, `deque`, the tree and hash containers, the adaptors, `bitset`), `<charconv>`, `<chrono>` with the calendar, atomics with waiting, threads, mutexes and the other synchronization types over pthreads, `<format>` and `<print>` checked at compile time, and `<cmath>`. Where the standard throws, an abort hook ends the program or panics the kernel. With the Swift runtimes over these headers, libc++ is no longer fetched; the only outside sources are Limine and Swift's. `docs/development.md` ("C++ Standard Library") lists what it has and leaves out.

FeatherOS has its own C library but no C++ standard library. GCC-built programs and the kernel have no `std` at all, so `cppbench` carries its own `Vector`. Swift's C++ programs (M25 Part 5) and the concurrency runtime read libc++'s headers from `deps/llvm-project`, which leaves out everything in libc++'s compiled library, `std::string` first. This milestone writes a subset of the C++23 standard library in `src/libcxx/`, as the C library was written: the parts programs need, growing with them.

**Gains:**
- One library for GCC and Clang, so headers shared between C++ and Swift code can use `std::string_view`, `std::optional` and the rest, which they cannot while each side has a different library (or none).
- `std::string` from Swift: its members are compiled by us, so there are no extern templates without a library behind them.
- The kernel can use the parts that need no allocation or system calls (`<type_traits>`, `<utility>`, `<array>`, `<span>`, `<optional>`); GCC here has no `<new>` today.
- `std::format` and `std::print` over the C library's `format.h` engine.

**Design:**
- Headers declare and define templates; whatever is not a template (`std::string`'s non-inline members, `std::to_chars`, the `<atomic>` wait functions, thread start-up) goes into `featheros_cxx`, a static library linked after the program and before `featheros_c`.
- No exceptions or RTTI, as for every FeatherOS program: what would throw calls an abort hook that prints and ends the program, as `operator new` does today. No allocator-aware extras beyond `std::allocator`.
- Some headers must match what the compilers expect: `<initializer_list>`'s layout, `<compare>`'s ordering types that `<=>` returns, `<coroutine>`, `<source_location>`, and `<type_traits>` built on the builtins GCC and Clang share. These are checked with both compilers from the first part.
- Everything lives in `std` with no inline namespace, so the Itanium mangling of GCC and Clang agree, including the `St`/`Ss`/`Sa` abbreviations.
- Host tests cannot use our types as `std`, since Catch2 needs the host's library: `unit-test` builds the headers in a namespace of their own (`FEATHEROS_STD_NAMESPACE`), and QEMU programs check them as `std`.

**Out of scope:** iostreams, locales, `<regex>`, `<filesystem>`, time zones, `<random>`'s distributions beyond a few, most of `<ranges>`, and `<stacktrace>`.

- **Part 1: language support (done).** The headers are in `src/libcxx/include/`, extensionless like any standard library's, with `__featheros_config` first in each: it sets `FEATHEROS_STD_NAMESPACE` (`std` unless a host test defines it), `_FSTD` for qualified calls, and `std::size_t`, `ptrdiff_t` and `nullptr_t` from `decltype`, so no C header is needed for them. Helpers live in `std::__detail`.
  - *C wrappers:* `<cstddef>` (with `std::byte`), `<cstdint>`, `<climits>`, `<cfloat>`, `<cstdarg>`, `<cassert>` (no include guard), `<cerrno>`, `<cctype>`, `<cstring>`, `<cstdlib>`, `<cstdio>`, `<ctime>`, `<csignal>`, `<csetjmp>`, `<cinttypes>`, `<cuchar>`. They bring in with `using` only what the C library has and ISO C names. `<cstring>`'s search functions (`memchr`, `strchr`, `strrchr`, `strpbrk`, `strstr`) are const-correct pairs, and `<cstdlib>`'s `abs` and `div` overloads are defined rather than brought in, since a host's `<stdlib.h>` may already add some to the global namespace. `<cmath>` waits for a later part: the C library lacks many `float` and `long double` variants, and the classification macros would have to become functions.
  - *Language support:* `<new>` (placement forms, `align_val_t`, `nothrow`, `destroying_delete`, `launder`, the interference sizes; declarations of every replaceable form, of which `cxx_runtime.cpp` now also defines the `nothrow` ones; no `bad_alloc` or `new_handler`), `<initializer_list>` (a pointer and a count, which both compilers accept; GCC calls its private constructor), `<type_traits>` (all of C++23's traits but the ones below; `is_layout_compatible`, `is_pointer_interconvertible_base_of` and `reference_c*_from_temporary` only where `__has_builtin` finds them; INVOKE in `__detail::Invoke`, which Part 2's `std::invoke` will call; `move`, `forward`, `declval` and `swap` live here because the traits need them), `<utility>` (`forward_like`, `exchange`, `cmp_*`, `in_range`, `to_underlying`, `unreachable`, integer sequences from `__make_integer_seq` or GCC's `__integer_pack`, the `in_place` tags, `tuple_size`/`tuple_element`, and `pair` with C++23's `common_type` and `basic_common_reference`), `<limits>`, `<compare>` (the ordering types, `common_comparison_category`, `three_way_comparable(_with)`, `compare_three_way`, and `__detail::synth_three_way` for `pair`), `<concepts>` (all of them, with `ranges::swap`), `<bit>`.
  - *Left out for now:* `pair`'s piecewise constructor (needs `<tuple>`; added in Part 2 step 2), its constructors from pair-like types and its const assignment for proxies; `strong_order`, `weak_order`, `partial_order` and the `compare_*_fallback` objects; `is_pointer_interconvertible_with_class` and `is_corresponding_member`; the deprecated `aligned_storage`, `aligned_union` and `is_pod`.
  - *Surprises:* GCC turns `__builtin_popcountll` into a call to libgcc's `__popcountdi2` on x86-64 without POPCNT and on AArch64 without SIMD (the kernel), so `std::popcount` counts bits by hand there, and with the builtin under Clang or where the instruction exists. Clang refuses any `long double` function on x86-64 without floating-point registers, which clang-tidy sees in kernel files, so `numeric_limits<long double>` is left out where `__SSE__` is undefined on x86-64. GCC rejected `declval<T&>().~T()` for reference types as a hard error even behind `||`, so `is_destructible` is a `consteval` function with `if constexpr`.
  - *Build:* `src/libcxx/CMakeLists.txt` makes the interface library `featheros_cxx_headers`, a SYSTEM include directory linked by the kernel and, publicly, by `featheros_c` and `featheros_crt0`, so every C++ program has it. Being system headers, their warnings are hidden where they are used; `quality.sh`, the pre-commit hooks and the format hook format them as C++.
  - *Users:* the kernel's `memory/heap.h` includes `<new>` instead of declaring the operators, `signals.cpp` uses `std::countr_zero`, the C library's `cxx_runtime.cpp` takes `align_val_t` from `<new>`, and `math_functions.cpp`, `wide_division.cpp` and `arch/x86_64/long_double.cpp` use `std::bit_width` and `std::countl_zero` instead of `__builtin_clzll`. `cppbench` uses `std::move` instead of its own `Move`.
  - *Tests:* `tests/unit/libcxx/` (`type_traits_tests.cpp`, `utility_tests.cpp`, `limits_bit_tests.cpp`) builds the headers as `featheros_std` with `-Wall -Wextra -Wshadow -Werror`. There, the types the compiler names (`initializer_list`, the ordering types, `align_val_t`) are aliases of the host's, so their own code is checked only by `cxxtest`. `src/user/cxxtest.cpp` checks the headers as `std` under GCC, with 84 `static_assert`s and 47 checks at run time (placement and aligned `new`, braced lists, structured bindings of `pair`, `ranges::swap` through argument-dependent lookup, NaN ordering, the bit functions on values the compiler cannot fold); the shell smoke test's programs part runs it and expects `cxxtest: 47 checks passed`. The full matrix passed (38 cases in 269 s), with `cxxtest` in all six shell cases, Release included. clang-tidy compiles the same file with Clang for both targets, so both compilers see the headers as `std`.
  - *Swift spike:* a module map over a header that includes every Part 1 header and uses `pair`, `initializer_list`, `<=>`, `bit_width`, `numeric_limits`, a constrained template and `strlen` compiled in Embedded and in full Swift with `-Xcc -nostdinc++ -Xcc -isystem src/libcxx/include` in place of libc++'s directories, both targets' flags as the build uses them. Swift imported `std.pair` and the ordering types, and the objects referred to nothing but C library functions. The C++ programs themselves still use libc++, since `cxx_interop_std.h` needs `<optional>`, `<span>`, `<string_view>` and `<vector>`; they move over with Parts 2 and 3. No `std` module map was needed: without one, the headers are textual parts of the modules that include them.
- **Part 2: vocabulary types (done).** `<array>`, `<span>`, `<string_view>`, `<optional>`, `<tuple>`, `<variant>`, `<expected>`, `<memory>` and `<functional>`, in three steps: (1) the types that need no allocation, (2) `<tuple>`, `<variant>`, `<expected>`, (3) `<memory>` and the rest of `<functional>`. Swift's `Cxx` overlay gives `std::optional` its `value` and makes `std::span` from buffers (step 1's spike).
  - *Step 1 done: the types that need no allocation.* New headers `<iterator>`, `<functional>`, `<array>`, `<span>`, `<string_view>`, `<optional>`, over three internal ones: `__memory_base` (`addressof`, `construct_at`, `destroy_at`, `pointer_traits`, `to_address`, and `__detail::ConstructAt`), `__iterator_base` (iterator tags, traits and concepts, `reverse_iterator`, `advance`/`distance`/`next`/`prev`, the free range access functions, the `ranges::begin`-style access objects and the range concepts; `<iterator>` is only this for now) and `__char_traits` (`char_traits` for `char`, `wchar_t`, `char8_t`, `char16_t`, `char32_t`, with `int_type` from `__WINT_TYPE__` for `wchar_t`, so no `<wchar.h>` is needed). `iterator_traits`' primary template names itself (`__primary_template`), which is how the concepts' ITER_CONCEPT tells it from a specialization. `<functional>` has `invoke` and `invoke_r` (over Part 1's `__detail::Invoke`), `reference_wrapper`, `ref`, `cref`, the operator function objects (typed forms return `T` or `bool`, so `bit_not<unsigned char>` does not promote) and the `ranges` comparisons. `array` keeps its elements in a public `__elements` member, an empty struct when the size is 0 (`data()` is then null). `span` of fixed extent stores only its pointer (`[[no_unique_address]] __detail::SpanSize`). `string_view` has the whole C++23 interface but hashing and streams; its `sv` literals turn off the reserved-suffix warning per compiler. `optional` gets conditionally trivial copy, move, assignment and destructor from constrained defaulted members (P0848), keeps its value in an anonymous union, and builds `transform`'s result in place through a private constructor, so a result that cannot be moved works.
    - *Abort hook:* `__featheros_config` declares `std::__detail::LibraryAbort(const char*)`, which the headers call where the standard throws (`optional::value`, the `at` members, `string_view::substr` and `copy`). `src/libc/cxx_runtime.cpp` prints `C++ library: <message>` and aborts, `src/kernel/panic.cpp` panics, and the unit tests define their own.
    - *Surprises:* constant evaluation allows placement `new` only through a function named `std::construct_at`, so the host tests' `featheros_std` copy calls the host's (`__detail::ConstructAt`, which includes the host's `<memory>` only in that build). `ranges::size` on an array was ambiguous between the bound and `end - begin` until the latter excluded arrays. A requires-expression outside a template is a hard error rather than false, so the tests check such things through concepts or `is_invocable_v`.
    - *Tests:* `tests/unit/libcxx/iterator_functional_tests.cpp`, `array_span_tests.cpp`, `string_view_tests.cpp`, `optional_tests.cpp` (593 unit tests in all, under ASan and UBSan). `cxxtest` gains 16 `static_assert`s and 16 run-time checks (structured bindings of `array`, `reverse_iterator`, `span` writes through to an array, `as_bytes`, `string_view` searches on a string the compiler cannot see and its unsigned byte order, overlapping `char_traits::move`, `optional`'s lifetimes and chains, `invoke` through `reference_wrapper`), and the shell smoke test expects `cxxtest: 63 checks passed`. The headers compile cleanly under GCC 14 and under Clang for both targets, as `std`.
    - *Swift spike:* a module map over a header using `optional`, `span`, `string_view` and `array` compiled in Embedded and full Swift with `-Xcc -nostdinc++ -Xcc -isystem src/libcxx/include`. Swift conformed `std.optional` to `CxxOptional` (`.value`) and `std.span` to `CxxSpan` (built from an `UnsafeBufferPointer`), and the objects referenced no C++ library symbols. `cxx_interop_std.h` still uses libc++, since it needs `<vector>` (Part 3).
  - *Step 2 done: `<tuple>`, `<variant>`, `<expected>`.* `<utility>` gained the pack helpers both use (`__detail::PackElement` over `__type_pack_element` where it exists, `PackCount`, `PackIndex`), `ConvertsFromAnyCvref` (moved from `<optional>`, since `expected` shares it), and `pair`'s piecewise constructor, which Part 1 left out: `<utility>` declares `tuple` and its lvalue `get`, so the constructor's qualified `get` call finds them at its definition.
    - *`tuple`:* each element is a base `__detail::TupleLeaf<Index, T>` of the public member `__elements`, with the value as a `[[no_unique_address]]` member, so `tuple<Empty, int>` is the size of an `int`. The `get` overloads come before the class, because the class's constructors call `_FSTD::get` and a qualified name is looked up where the template is defined. The C++23 constructors and assignments from `tuple`, `pair` and other tuple-like types (`array`, until `ranges::subrange` exists) are there, conditionally explicit; the generic tuple-like overload excludes `tuple` and `pair`, which have their own, rather than relying on partial ordering. `tuple_cat` takes any tuple-like arguments and finds element k of its result as element `Inner[k]` of argument `Outer[k]`, two arrays computed at compile time. Comparisons use `synth_three_way`, so elements with only `<` give a weak ordering. `common_type` and `basic_common_reference` cover tuples of equal size.
    - *`variant`:* the alternatives live in a nested union (`__detail::VariantUnion`), and a new alternative is built by constructing the whole union with `in_place_index` (`emplace` destroys the old one first), which keeps constant evaluation content about the active member. The index is an `unsigned char` below 255 alternatives. Index-dependent work (copy, assignment, swap, comparison, `visit`) goes through `__detail::Dispatch`, a table of function pointers built at compile time; `visit` of several variants dispatches on each in turn, binding the chosen alternatives into a lambda, and checks that every combination returns the same type. The converting constructor's FUN is one overload per alternative constrained on `T x[] = {value}`, so narrowing alternatives drop out and duplicates are ambiguous.
    - *`expected`:* as `optional`, a union with conditionally trivial copy, move, assignment and destructor, and private constructors that build `transform`'s and `transform_error`'s results in place. `expected<void, E>` is a partial specialization. Without exceptions nothing can fail between destroying the old contents and building the new, so assignment and `emplace` drop the standard's requirement that one of the types moves without throwing.
    - *Left out:* allocator-extended constructors and `uses_allocator` (need `<memory>`'s `allocator_arg_t`; step 3), `const` assignment of tuples and pairs for proxy references, `pair`'s pair-like constructors, comparisons of a `tuple` with other tuple-like types, `hash` of `variant` and `monostate` (with `hash` in step 3), and `variant::visit` as a member (C++26).
    - *Surprises:* GCC refuses `__type_pack_element` in a function signature ("use library traits instead"), so `PackElement` wraps it in a struct. Host tests cannot check structured bindings of `tuple`, since the compiler asks the host's `std::tuple_size`; `cxxtest` does.
    - *Tests:* `tests/unit/libcxx/tuple_tests.cpp`, `variant_tests.cpp`, `expected_tests.cpp` (constant-evaluated checks of the non-trivial paths with a literal type that has a user-provided destructor, lifetimes counted with a tracked type, move-only and non-movable types). `cxxtest` gains 10 `static_assert`s and 11 run-time checks (structured bindings of `tuple`, `tie` with `ignore`, `tuple_cat` of a tuple, pair and array, the piecewise constructor, `variant` lifetimes, `visit` of two variants, `expected` chains and `expected<void>`), and the shell smoke test expects `cxxtest: 74 checks passed`. The unit tests (619 in all, under ASan and UBSan) and the shell smoke test on x86-64 and AArch64 passed; the headers compile under GCC 14 and under Clang for both targets, as `std`.
  - *Step 3 done: `<memory>` and the rest of `<functional>`.*
    - *`hash`:* a new internal header `__hash_base` has the primary template, which is disabled (deleted constructors and assignment, no call operator) for anything but enums, and the specializations for the integer and character types, the floating-point types, pointers and `nullptr_t`; `__detail::HashEnabled<T>` tests `is_default_constructible_v<hash<T>>`. Integers hash to themselves. Floating-point values hash their bits, with both zeros as 0, through `__builtin_bit_cast` rather than comparison, since `long double` has no arithmetic on AArch64; x87's 80-bit `long double` masks its 48 padding bits, and an 8-byte one (macOS hosts) hashes as `double`. `__detail::HashCharacters` is FNV-1a over each character's bytes, low byte first, for `string_view` now and `string` in Part 3. `<optional>`, `<variant>` (`HashCombine` of the index and the alternative's hash) and `<string_view>` specialize it in their own headers.
    - *`<functional>`:* `mem_fn`; `not_fn`, `bind_front` and `bind_back` over one `__detail::ForwardingWrapper<Operation, State...>`, which keeps the callable and bound arguments in a `tuple` and has a call operator for each of `&`, `const&`, `&&` and `const&&`, each deleted where its call is ill-formed so an rvalue wrapper never falls back to the `const&` one. `function` keeps callables of up to three pointers that move without throwing in a buffer inside it, others on the heap, behind a table of five function pointers (`call`, `copy`, `move`, `destroy` and a type tag) per callable type; `__detail::TypeTag<T>` (an `inline constexpr` variable whose address names the type, in `__memory_base`) stands in for `type_info`, so `target` works without RTTI and `get_deleter` uses the same. The storage is `mutable`, as the standard calls a non-`const` callable through a `const` `function`. Deduction guides cover function pointers and every qualifier of `operator()`, `noexcept` and static ones included.
