    - *`<memory>`:* `allocator` calls the aligned `operator new` for over-aligned types and, in constant evaluation, `::operator new` (allowed only inside `std::allocator<T>::allocate`; the host build calls the host's `std::allocator`). `allocator_traits` finds each member type through small `constexpr` functions returning `type_identity`. `allocator_arg_t` and `uses_allocator` live in `__memory_base` for `<tuple>`; uses-allocator construction is `__detail::UsesAllocatorConstruction<T>::Arguments` in `<tuple>` (with `pair`'s piecewise cases), which `<memory>`'s `uses_allocator_construction_args` wraps. `unique_ptr` keeps the deleter `[[no_unique_address]]`, so `sizeof(unique_ptr<int>) == sizeof(int*)`, and is `constexpr` (C++23). `shared_ptr` is a pointer and a `__detail::SharedCount*`: a control block with atomic owner and weak counts (`__atomic` builtins, ahead of `<atomic>` in Part 4) and virtual `DestroyObject`, `DestroyBlock` and `FindDeleter`; `SharedPointerCount<Pointer, Deleter>` holds a pointer and deleter, `SharedObjectCount<T>` holds `make_shared`'s object in a union. The control block's members are inline in the header, as libstdc++'s are, so there is still no `featheros_cxx` library. Converting a `weak_ptr` to a base goes through `lock()`, since converting a dangling pointer to a virtual base would read the dead object. `enable_shared_from_this` is found by deducing `const enable_shared_from_this<X>*` from the pointer, which fails for an ambiguous or private base.
    - *`<tuple>`:* the allocator-extended constructors that step 2 left out. One template covers copies, moves and every tuple-like source; each element is built from `UsesAllocatorConstruction`'s argument tuple in place, through a private `TupleLeaf` constructor that forwards each argument by its own type (only `<utility>`'s lvalue `get` is declared there). `uses_allocator<tuple<...>, Alloc>` is true.
    - *Left out:* `std::bind` and its placeholders, `move_only_function`, the searchers, `function::target_type` and `dynamic_pointer_cast` (both need RTTI), `shared_ptr`'s allocator-taking constructors and `allocate_shared`, `make_shared` of arrays, `atomic<shared_ptr>`, `out_ptr` and `inout_ptr`, uses-allocator construction of a `pair` from a type that only converts to one. The uninitialized-memory algorithms come with `<algorithm>` in Part 3.
    - *Surprises:* constant evaluation of `featheros_std::allocator` in the host tests needs the host's `std::allocator`, as `construct_at` did in step 1. A non-template member's discarded `if constexpr` branch is still checked, so `hash<long double>` picks its form with `__SIZEOF_LONG_DOUBLE__`. The host's 8-byte `long double` hid a bug in the x87 form, where the sign is bit 15 of the upper word and `high << 1` kept it, so -0.0 hashed apart from 0.0 until `cxxtest` caught it on x86-64. clang-tidy's `performance-unnecessary-copy-initialization` turns a test's second `shared_ptr` into a reference unless the copy is used as one.
    - *Tests:* `tests/unit/libcxx/memory_tests.cpp` (allocator in constant evaluation and over-aligned, `allocator_traits`' defaults, uses-allocator construction, `unique_ptr` with stateful, reference and handle deleters and arrays, `shared_ptr` lifetimes, deleters, aliasing and arrays, `weak_ptr` conversion after expiry, `enable_shared_from_this`, comparisons and hashes), `functional_tests.cpp` (`function`'s storage, copies, swaps, `target`, deduction guides; the wrappers' four call operators and their deleted ones; `mem_fn`; `hash`), and allocator-extended constructors in `tuple_tests.cpp`: 642 unit tests in all, under ASan and UBSan. `cxxtest` gains 6 `static_assert`s (`unique_ptr` and `allocator` in constant evaluation, the wrappers, `function`'s deduction guide, disabled `hash`, `uses_allocator`) and 21 run-time checks (`unique_ptr` through a virtual destructor and of an array, `shared_ptr` counts and `weak_ptr` expiry, `enable_shared_from_this`, `get_deleter` of a lambda, aliasing, `static_pointer_cast`, an over-aligned allocation, a tuple built with an allocator, `function` inline and on the heap, the wrappers, `mem_fn`, hashes of string views, zeros and `optional`), and the shell smoke test expects `cxxtest: 95 checks passed`. The headers compile as `std` under GCC 14 and under Clang for both targets with system-header warnings on. The shell smoke test passed on x86-64 and AArch64, and the full matrix passed (38 cases in 444 s), with `cxxtest` in all six shell cases, Release included.
- **Part 3: containers and algorithms (done).** `<vector>`, `<string>`, `<algorithm>`, `<numeric>`, `<iterator>`, `<charconv>`, then `<deque>`, `<map>`, `<set>`, `<unordered_map>`, `<unordered_set>`. `cppbench` drops its `Vector` (with a benchmark run to show the cost), and `cxxinterop` checks `std::string` and Swift's `CxxStdlib` overlay (`String(std.string)`) through a module map of our own. Planned in four steps: (1) the classic `<algorithm>` (not the `ranges::` ones), `<numeric>`, the rest of `<iterator>` (insert iterators, `move_iterator`, the sentinels) and `<memory>`'s uninitialized-memory algorithms, none of which allocate but `stable_sort`, `stable_partition` and `inplace_merge`; (2) `<vector>`, with `cppbench` moving to it; (3) `<string>` and `<charconv>`, with `featheros_cxx` for what is not a template, and the Swift C++ programs moving to our headers; (4) `<deque>`, `<map>`, `<set>`, `<unordered_map>`, `<unordered_set>`.
  - *Step 1 done: algorithms, numerics and iterator adaptors.*
    - *`<algorithm>`:* every classic algorithm of C++23 but the parallel overloads: the non-modifying ones (`all_of` to `is_permutation`, `search` with a searcher), the modifying ones (`copy` to `sample`, `shift_left` and `shift_right`), partitions, sorting, binary search, merging, set operations, heaps, `min`/`max`/`minmax`/`clamp`, `lexicographical_compare_three_way` and the permutations. They dispatch on the classic iterator categories (`__detail::ClassicForward`, `ClassicBidirectional`, `ClassicRandomAccess`). `sort` is an introsort (median-of-three Hoare partition with no bounds checks, heapsort past a depth of 2 log n, one insertion sort over runs of up to 16 at the end) and `nth_element` the matching introselect. `stable_sort` is a merge sort over insertion-sorted runs, `inplace_merge` and `stable_partition` merge and partition through a `__detail::TemporaryBuffer` from `operator new(nothrow)` and fall back to rotations in place (`MergeInPlace`, `StablePartitionInPlace`) when there is none; those three are not `constexpr`, as in C++23. `copy`, `copy_backward`, `move` and `move_backward` become `memmove` between pointers to one trivially copyable type outside constant evaluation. `shuffle` and `sample` draw through `__detail::UniformBelow`, which rejects the remainder and combines draws when the generator's range is narrower than the bound, so no `<random>` is needed; `sample` selects in order from forward iterators and keeps a reservoir otherwise.
    - *`<numeric>`:* `iota`, `accumulate` and the other folds (moving the running value), `reduce`, `transform_reduce`, the four scans, `adjacent_difference`, `partial_sum`, `gcd` and `lcm` over unsigned magnitudes (so `INT_MIN` works), and `midpoint` for integers (rounding towards the first argument, no overflow), floating-point values and pointers.
    - *`<iterator>`:* `back_insert_iterator`, `front_insert_iterator` and `insert_iterator` with their makers, `move_iterator` (C++20's, with `iterator_concept` and `iterator_category` only where the base has one) and `move_sentinel`, `default_sentinel_t` and `unreachable_sentinel_t`.
    - *`<memory>`:* the uninitialized-memory algorithms (`uninitialized_copy` to `uninitialized_default_construct_n`) and `destroy`/`destroy_n`, `constexpr` but for the two that default-initialize. Without exceptions they never roll back.
    - *Left out:* the `ranges::` algorithms and the indirect callable concepts, `counted_iterator`, `common_iterator`, `ranges::iter_swap`, the stream iterators, execution policies and saturating arithmetic.
    - *Surprises:* the host tests run the algorithms over the host's containers, whose iterators name the host's tags, so classic dispatch put a `std::vector` iterator on the input-iterator path, where `prev` does nothing. In the host build `__detail::OwnTag` now counts the host's tags as ours in `iterator_traits` and ITER_CONCEPT; the opposite direction (the host's `vector` taking our `move_iterator`) still fails, so those tests copy with our algorithms. Apple's Clang 17 rejects step 2's `visit` in a `static_assert` ("read of element of array without known bound"); Homebrew's Clang 23, which the build and clang-tidy use, and GCC 14 accept it.
    - *Tests:* `tests/unit/libcxx/algorithm_tests.cpp` (each family; `sort`, `nth_element`, `partial_sort`, `stable_sort`, `stable_partition`, the merges, the set operations and the permutations against the host's library on random data up to 5000 elements, sorted, reversed and equal input, stability with tagged keys, the in-place fallbacks called directly, forward-only and input-only iterators, move-only elements, a narrow generator), `numeric_tests.cpp`, the adaptors in `iterator_functional_tests.cpp` and the uninitialized algorithms in `memory_tests.cpp`: 661 unit tests in all, under ASan and UBSan. `cxxtest` gains 5 `static_assert`s (`sort` and `next_permutation` in constant evaluation, `binary_search`, `accumulate`, `gcd`/`lcm`/`midpoint`, `move_iterator`) and 13 run-time checks (`sort`, `nth_element`, `stable_sort`'s stability, heaps, `rotate`/`reverse`, an overlapping `copy`, `merge`/`set_intersection`, `unique`/`remove`, the numeric algorithms, `shuffle` with an LCG, `back_inserter`, `minmax_element`/`clamp`, `construct_at`/`destroy`), and the shell smoke test expects `cxxtest: 108 checks passed`. `cxxtest` compiles with `-Wsystem-headers` under GCC 14 and Clang 23 for both targets. The unit tests and the shell smoke test on x86-64 and AArch64 passed; the full matrix waits for the end of Part 3.
  - *Step 2 done: `<vector>`.*
    - *`vector`:* the whole C++23 interface, `constexpr` throughout: the iterator-pair, count, `initializer_list` and `from_range` constructors with their deduction guides, `assign` and `assign_range`, `insert` in its five forms and `insert_range`, `append_range`, `emplace`, `emplace_back` (returning a reference), `erase`, `resize`, `reserve`, `shrink_to_fit`, `==` and `<=>` (synth-three-way), `swap`, and the free `erase` and `erase_if`. Its iterators are `__detail::PointerIterator<T, Owner>` from a new internal header `__pointer_iterator` (contiguous, a class of their own per container, the mutable one converting to the const one), which `<string>` will reuse. Growth doubles the capacity (at least to what is needed) through `allocate_at_least`. Every path that reallocates (the private `ReallocateInsert` and `Relocate`) builds the new elements in the new storage before the old ones move, so `v.push_back(v[0])` and `v.resize(n, v[0])` are safe; `emplace` and `insert(pos, n, value)` without reallocation take a copy first. Elements move to new storage by `move_if_noexcept`, or by `memcpy` when the type is trivially copyable and the allocator has no `construct` or `destroy` of its own (`CopiesBytes`), and copies from a contiguous range of the same type are a `memcpy` as well. The allocator is used through `allocator_traits` throughout: `select_on_container_copy_construction`, the three `propagate_on_container_*` traits and `is_always_equal` (move assignment between unequal allocators moves element by element). `T` may be incomplete where `vector<T>` is named (a struct holding a vector of itself), so nothing at class scope asks about `T`.
    - *`vector<bool>`:* one bit per element in words of `size_t`, with `__detail::BitReference` (C++23's `const` assignment, so `vector<bool>::iterator` is an `output_iterator`; `flip`, and `swap` overloads that `iter_swap` finds) and `__detail::BitIterator<IsConst>` (random access). `flip()`, the static `swap(reference, reference)` and `hash<vector<bool>>` (over whole words, the last one masked) are there. Words are constructed when allocated, so constant evaluation can read them; bits past `size()` hold anything.
    - *Shared pieces:* `__iterator_base` gained `from_range_t`/`from_range`, `__detail::ClassicForward` (moved from `<algorithm>`), `__detail::ClassicInput` (what keeps `vector(I, I)` apart from `vector(n, value)`), `__detail::ContainerCompatibleRange` and `__detail::RangeLength`.
    - *`cppbench`:* its `Vector` template is gone; the parser and the document use `std::vector` (`push_back`, `insert` at the end for runs of bytes, `==` for member keys). A Release benchmark before and after (3 rounds of 1 s, same QEMU and host): `json`, the only workload that uses the vectors, went from 7142 to 8196 µs on x86-64 (+15 %) and from 6329 to 7518 µs on AArch64 (+19 %), making Embedded Swift's ratio 1.54 and 1.55 instead of 1.86 and 1.81; the other workloads stayed within the usual noise. `cppbench`'s code shrank from 108 446 to 103 598 bytes on x86-64 and from 98 376 to 94 440 on AArch64. The likely cause, not measured, is the growth policy: the old `Vector` started at 4 elements, `std::vector` (like libc++'s and libstdc++'s) at 1, so the parser's many small vectors reallocate twice more.
    - *Left out:* allocators whose `pointer` is not a plain pointer (a `static_assert`), `pmr::vector`, formatting `vector<bool>`. A size past `max_size()` and `at()` out of range call the abort hook. `emplace` into the middle builds its temporary directly rather than through the allocator.
    - *Surprises:* the host's libc++ 23 `vector<bool>` shifts a word by its full width in some inserts, which UBSan stops, so the unit tests model `vector<bool>` with a host `vector<char>`. A constrained non-template converting constructor `BitIterator(const BitIterator<false>&) requires IsConst` would have been `BitIterator<false>`'s copy constructor with an unsatisfied constraint, leaving it uncopyable; it is a template.
    - *Tests:* `tests/unit/libcxx/vector_tests.cpp` (13 cases: construction and growth, iterator concepts, a recursive struct, constant evaluation of `vector` and `vector<bool>`, 4000 random operations against the host's `vector`, arguments that alias elements, leak counting, move-only elements and `move_if_noexcept`, a counting stateful allocator through copy, move and swap, comparison and `erase`/`erase_if`, the range members with input-only ranges, 3000 random operations on `vector<bool>`, its hash): 674 unit tests in all, under ASan and UBSan. `cxxtest` gains 2 `static_assert`s (`vector` and `vector<bool>` in constant evaluation, the iterator concepts and deduction guide) and 10 run-time checks (growth, insert and erase, `push_back` of an element while growing, a move-only type and its destruction, `unique_ptr`, an incomplete type, `erase` and `<=>`, `vector<bool>`, `from_range` and `append_range`), and the shell smoke test expects `cxxtest: 118 checks passed`. `cxxtest` and `cppbench` compile with `-Wsystem-headers` under GCC 14 and Clang 23 for both targets. The unit tests and the shell smoke test on x86-64 and AArch64 passed (`tools/check.sh --modes SHELL --arch both`); the full matrix waits for the end of Part 3.
  - *Step 3 done: `<string>`, `<charconv>` and `featheros_cxx`; the Swift C++ programs on our headers.*
    - *`<string>`:* `basic_string` with the whole C++23 interface but streams, `constexpr` throughout, and the five string types. The object is four pointers: a `Storage` union of a local array (16 bytes, so 15 `char`s and the terminator) and a heap pointer, the size and the capacity, plus the allocator `[[no_unique_address]]`; a capacity equal to the local capacity marks the local form, since heap blocks are only allocated for more. No member points into the object (libstdc++'s does), so strings move by copying bytes, Swift may treat them as it likes, and constant evaluation can use the local form: the union is switched by constructing it whole (`__detail::ConstructAt(&m_storage, ...)`), and heap characters are constructed when allocated there. One private `Splice(position, removed, count, may_alias, write)` is behind `insert`, `replace`, `append` and `assign`: in place when the result fits and the new characters cannot come from the string, otherwise `Rebuild` writes the result into new storage (or a stack buffer when it fits locally) before the old goes, so `s.insert(0, s)` and `s.replace(1, 2, s.c_str() + 3, 5)` work. Iterator ranges that are not contiguous `CharT` are copied into a temporary first, since they may point into the string. Growth doubles. Searches and comparisons forward to `basic_string_view`. `hash` of the five types uses `__detail::HashCharacters`, so a string hashes as its view.
    - *`featheros_cxx`:* a static library (`src/libcxx/src/`, CMake in `src/libcxx/src/CMakeLists.txt`, added after the C library) linked into every program after its code and before `featheros_c`, and into full-Swift programs by `ld.lld`. `<string>` declares `extern template class basic_string<char>` and `string.cpp` instantiates it, so programs call `std::string`'s members there (`cxxtest`'s object refers to 21 of them and compiles only the member templates); it also has `stoi` to `stold` (over `strto*`, aborting on no number or `ERANGE`) and `to_string` (integers through `to_chars`, floating-point values through `snprintf`'s `%f`, sized first). The sources include the headers as `<string>` so clang-tidy sees them as system headers (a relative include had `--fix` rewrite `<functional>`'s macros), and carry `NOLINTBEGIN(bugprone-std-namespace-modification)`.
    - *`<charconv>`:* the integer `to_chars` and `from_chars` are `constexpr` overloads for each integer type over `__detail::IntegerToChars` and `IntegerFromChars`. The floating-point ones (`charconv.cpp`) use the C library's engines: `FloatFormatDetail::ScientificDigits`/`FixedDigits` for exact digits and `ParseFloat` for correctly rounded reading. Shortest output bisects the digit count (if n digits can read back, n + 1 can), and for each count tries the correctly rounded digits and, if they miss, their neighbour on the other side of the value (`StepLastDigit`), which covers the asymmetric interval at a power of two. A whole number in fixed form prints its exact integer, or that integer's neighbour, rather than the shortest digits padded with zeros, as libc++ (MSVC's code) and libstdc++ do: `to_chars(1e21, fixed)` is `1000000000000000000000`, although `999999999999999999999` would read back and is shorter. General without a precision uses `%g`'s default P = 6 (fixed for exponents -4 to 5), as libstdc++ does. Precision forms go through `FormatFloat` (hex drops its `0x`). `from_chars` scans the grammar itself (no `+`, no `0x`; an exponent required for `scientific`, refused for `fixed`; `inf`, `infinity`, `nan(...)`), keeps at most 800 significant digits (40 hex) and a sticky 1, and hands `ParseFloat` a short `0.ddd e N` text, so input of any length needs no allocation; an out-of-range result returns `result_out_of_range` and leaves the value alone, as the standard says (libc++ stores infinity). `errc` is in `__errc`, with Linux's numbers.
    - *`<ratio>` and `<chrono>`'s durations (from Part 4):* Swift's `CxxStdlib` overlay imports the toolchain's `CxxStdlibShim` module, whose header includes `<chrono>` for its duration helpers, so durations came now: `ratio` with exact 128-bit arithmetic and comparisons (a result outside `intmax_t` is a `static_assert`), the SI typedefs from `atto` to `exa`, and `duration` with `common_type`, the arithmetic and comparisons, `duration_cast`, `floor`, `ceil`, `round` (ties to even), `abs`, the typedefs and the integer literals. The floating-point literals are left out: they take a `long double`, which AArch64 cannot convert at run time.
    - *Swift:* the C++ programs (`cxxinterop`, `http`, `fullcxxinterop`, `fullhttp`) and the overlays read our headers (`FEATHEROS_SWIFT_CXX_INCLUDES`: `-nostdinc++`, `src/libcxx/include` as a system directory, and `src/libcxx/swift`), whose `module.modulemap` makes them the Clang module `std` (Swift's `CxxStdlib`). `swift_cxx/CMakeLists.txt` builds upstream's overlay (`std.swift`, `Chrono.swift`, `String.swift`) unchanged as an Embedded module, and `swift_runtime/CMakeLists.txt` as a full-Swift module and archive (`featheros_swift_full_cxx_stdlib`), loading the toolchain's `embedded/libcxxshim.modulemap` by name, since the toolchain names `CxxStdlibShim` only for Embedded Swift and its own platforms. `cxx_interop_std.h` is gone: `Half`, `Squares`, `Sum` and `CountVowels` are declared in `cxx_interop.h` and built by GCC in `cxx_interop.cpp`, with `Greeting` (returns a `std::string`) and `CountWords` (takes one), so standard library types cross between GCC's code and Swift's Clang. Only the concurrency runtime and the full runtime still read libc++'s headers (Part 6).
    - *Surprises:* Swift imports no C++ default arguments, so the overlay's `std.string(buffer, count)` found only the allocator-taking constructor; `basic_string` now declares the forms without an allocator separately, as libc++ does. The toolchain's `CxxStdlibShim` clashed with a shim module of our own (a redefinition), which is why the overlay uses the toolchain's. In host tests `construct_at` must be the host's (`__detail::ConstructAt`), as before, and a local named `in_place` shadowed `std::in_place`. Comparing `long double`s on AArch64 needs compiler-runtime calls, so `cxxtest` compares their text.
    - *Left out:* iostreams (`<<`, `>>`, `getline`), `pmr` strings, `to_wstring`, allocators whose `pointer` is not a plain pointer, `<system_error>` beyond `errc`, and `<chrono>`'s clocks, time points, calendar and floating-point literals (Part 4).
    - *Tests:* `tests/unit/libcxx/string_tests.cpp` (8 cases: construction around the local boundary, arguments that alias the string, 6000 random operations against the host's `std::string`, the replace/erase/resize forms with input-only ranges, searches, comparisons and every `operator+`, allocator propagation and leak counting, wide strings, numeric conversions), `charconv_tests.cpp` (integers in every base against the host's `<charconv>`, errors, floating-point output for random `double` and `float` bits, powers of ten and two and their neighbours in every format and several precisions against the host's libc++ 23, hex read back, input against the host's `from_chars` including long ties and out-of-range values) and `chrono_tests.cpp` (`ratio` and durations, mostly `static_assert`s), with `featheros_cxx`'s sources compiled into the test binary: 688 unit tests in all, under ASan and UBSan. `cxxtest` gains 3 `static_assert`s (`string` and integer `<charconv>` in constant evaluation, `string`'s size, iterators and deduction guide, durations and `ratio_add`) and 14 run-time checks (growth, aliasing arguments, concatenation and comparison, searches, swap and move, hash, `to_string`/`sto*`, `u16string`/`u32string`, `resize_and_overwrite`/`erase_if`, integer `<charconv>`, shortest and precision `double` output, `double` input, `long double` text), and the shell smoke test expects `cxxtest: 132 checks passed`. `cxxinterop` and `fullcxxinterop` print two new lines: `string hello, swift, 5 words, equal true, same hash true` (a GCC-built `std::string` through `String(std.string)`, a string literal as `std.string`, `+=`, `==` and `hashValue`) and `chrono 90 seconds, true`. `cxxtest` compiles under GCC 14 and Clang 23 for both targets. The unit tests and the shell smoke test on x86-64 and AArch64 passed (`tools/check.sh --modes SHELL --arch both`); the full matrix waits for the end of Part 3.
  - *Step 4 done: `<deque>`, `<map>`, `<set>`, `<unordered_map>`, `<unordered_set>`.*
    - *Shared pieces:* `__node_handle` has `__detail::NodeHandle<Node, Allocator, IsMap>` (the `node_type` of all eight node containers: `key()`/`mapped()` or `value()`, an `optional<Allocator>`, swap and move by the allocator's traits), `InsertReturnType`, `NodeOperations` (a node is allocated and constructed bare, its `value` in an anonymous union built through the allocator), `SetKey`/`MapKey`, `PairWithKey` (an `emplace` argument whose key can be looked up before a node is built) and the deduction guides' `IterKey`, `RangeKey` and `AllocatorLike`.
    - *`<map>` and `<set>`:* `map`, `multimap`, `set` and `multiset` are thin classes over `__detail::Tree<Value, KeyOf, Compare, Allocator>` (`__tree`), a red-black tree with the header node inside the container as `end()` (libstdc++'s layout: the header's parent is the root, its left and right the leftmost and rightmost nodes, and it is red, which tells it from the root in `TreeDecrement`). The rebalancing and stepping are not templates and live in `featheros_cxx` (`src/libcxx/src/tree.cpp`: `TreeIncrement`, `TreeDecrement`, `TreeInsertAndRebalance`, `TreeRebalanceForErase`). Unique inserts look for the key before building a node whenever it is at hand (`insert`, `try_emplace`, `emplace(key, mapped)`, `emplace(pair)`); hinted inserts are constant time when the key belongs next to the hint, and a multimap keeps equal keys in insertion order (after them without a hint, as close as possible before the hint with one). Copies copy the tree's shape and colours, so they need no rebalancing. Moving the container re-points the root at the new header; iterators to elements stay valid. The whole C++23 interface: node handles and `merge` (between map and multimap, any comparators), `try_emplace`, `insert_or_assign`, transparent `find`, `count`, `contains`, `lower_bound`, `upper_bound`, `equal_range`, and C++23's transparent `erase` and `extract`, `from_range` and `insert_range`, `<=>`, `erase_if` and the deduction guides. `set`'s iterators are one constant type; `map`'s `value_compare` is the nested class. The iterators' `==` is a namespace-scope template, as for `vector`, which Swift's importer finds.
    - *`<unordered_map>` and `<unordered_set>`:* the four over `__detail::HashTable` (`__hash_table`), with their shared members in one macro there (`FEATHEROS_HASH_CONTAINER_COMMON`). All nodes are on one singly linked list with each bucket's nodes adjacent; a bucket holds the node before its first one (the table's `m_before_begin` for the list's first bucket), so unlinking needs no second pointer, and iterators are node pointers with `end()` null, which keeps them valid through rehashes and moves. Each node caches its hash. Bucket counts are powers of two and `HashBucket` takes the top bits of the hash times 2^64/φ, since `hash<int>` is the identity; libc++ and libstdc++ use prime counts instead. `rehash` may shrink, `max_load_factor(z)` grows the table when needed, and growth at least doubles. A multi container puts a new key before the first equal one (after the hint when the hint is equal), and rehashing relinks the list in order, so groups of equal keys stay together and in order, as the standard requires. `==` compares groups with `is_permutation`. A default-constructed container allocates nothing, so its `bucket_count()` is 0, as libc++'s is.
    - *`<deque>`:* blocks of `DequeBlockSize<T>()` elements, a power of two (512 bytes' worth, at least 16), which never move, and a map of block pointers that grows by recentring or doubling. An iterator is the map and a position counted from its first slot (two words, arithmetic without branches), so popping or erasing at either end leaves other iterators valid, as the standard asks. Front and back spares are trimmed to one block, and before allocating at one end a spare block at the other is moved over, so a queue's blocks circulate (`deque_tests.cpp` checks the allocation count stays fixed over 300 000 operations); `clear()` keeps one block in the middle of the map, `shrink_to_fit()` of an empty deque frees everything. One element inserted in the middle is built first, then the shorter side shifts by one; several are built at the shorter end and rotated into place.
    - *Left out:* the `pmr` containers, C++26's heterogeneous `insert`, `try_emplace`, `operator[]` and `at`, allocators whose pointer is not a plain pointer for `deque`. `at` of a missing key or index and sizes past `max_size()` call the abort hook. `<list>`, `<forward_list>`, `<stack>`, `<queue>` and `<bitset>` were not part of this step (Swift's concurrency runtime needs `queue` and `bitset`, Part 6).
    - *Surprises:* the host's libc++ 23 `multimap::find` returns any of the equal keys, not the first, which the standard allows, so the random tests erase through `lower_bound`. The other failures while writing the tests were in the tests themselves.
    - *Tests:* `tests/unit/libcxx/map_set_tests.cpp` (8 cases: 30 000 random operations on `map` and 20 000 on `multimap` with hints anywhere against the host's, checked through the iterators' `base()` nodes for the red-black rules, parent links and the header's leftmost and rightmost; 50 000 sorted, reversed, repeated and hinted inserts; `set`/`multiset` against the host's; transparent lookups; `try_emplace` leaving a move-only argument alone; node handles, refused inserts and `merge` across comparators; a counting stateful allocator through copy, move and swap with leak and lifetime counts; comparisons, `erase_if`, input-only ranges and the deduction guides), `unordered_tests.cpp` (8 cases: 30 000 random operations against the host's `map` with keys that are multiples of 1024, bucket invariants, `unordered_multimap`'s groups through a rehash, every key in one bucket, the bucket interface and hash policy, element and iterator stability through `rehash` and a move, a transparent hash, node handles, `merge` and `==` of permutations, the allocator, ranges and deduction guides) and `deque_tests.cpp` (7 cases: random operations against the host's `deque` for `int`, `char`, a 64-byte type and a counted type, iterator concepts and reference and iterator stability, the queue's bounded allocations, arguments that alias elements, the allocator's traits, move-only elements, comparisons, ranges and deduction guides), with `tree.cpp` compiled into the test binary: 712 CTest cases in all, under ASan and UBSan. `cxxtest` gains 2 `static_assert`s (the iterator concepts of all five, deduction guides and `set`'s single iterator type) and 14 run-time checks (`map` order and counts, `try_emplace`/`insert_or_assign`/`at`, `erase` and bounds, `multimap` order, a transparent `set`, node handles and `merge`, `unordered_map` buckets and load factor, `erase`/`erase_if`, a string-keyed `unordered_map`, `unordered_multiset` `==`, `deque` at both ends, in the middle and as a queue, and destruction counts), and the shell smoke test expects `cxxtest: 146 checks passed`. The Swift module map lists the five headers. `cxxtest` compiles with `-Wsystem-headers` under GCC 14, Clang 23 and the Swift toolchain's Clang for both targets. The unit tests and the shell smoke test on x86-64 and AArch64 passed (`tools/check.sh --modes SHELL --arch both`); the first AArch64 run stalled in `libctest`'s session checks, before `cxxtest`, with no panic, and the re-run passed (session kept in `build/aarch64/smoke-output-libctest-stall.txt`). The full matrix, run to close Part 3, first failed to link `fullcxxinterop` in AArch64 Release, a step 3 problem no earlier run had reached: the full-Swift `CxxStdlib` object imports `Cxx`, and in Release's bitcode its request to autolink `swiftCxx` became an ELF dependent library that `ld.lld` could not find; `swift_runtime/CMakeLists.txt` now builds the overlay with `-disable-autolink-library swiftCxx`, as the programs already were. Then `tools/check.sh --matrix` passed (38 cases, both targets, Debug and Release).
- **Part 4: threads and time (done).** `<atomic>` over the compiler's builtins (with waiting over the futex), `<thread>`, `<stop_token>`, `<mutex>`, `<shared_mutex>` and `<condition_variable>` over the C library's pthreads, `<semaphore>`, `<latch>` and `<barrier>` over atomic waits, and `<chrono>`'s clocks over `clock_gettime`. `<ratio>` and `<chrono>`'s durations came in Part 3 step 3. Done in three steps, as planned: (1) `<atomic>` without waiting (`atomic`, `atomic_ref`, `atomic_flag`, fences; a lock table for what is not lock-free, since there is no libatomic) and `<chrono>`'s clocks and time points, with the kernel's and the C library's atomic builtins moving to `std::atomic` where they fit; (2) `<thread>` with `std::this_thread` and `jthread` over `<stop_token>`, `<mutex>`, `<shared_mutex>` and `<condition_variable>`, over pthreads in `featheros_cxx`; (3) `wait` and `notify` on atomics over the futex, `<semaphore>`, `<latch>` and `<barrier>`.
    - *Step 1 done: `<atomic>` and `<chrono>`'s clocks.* `src/libcxx/include/atomic` has `atomic` (a primary template for any trivially copyable type, partial specializations for integers, floating point and pointers), `atomic_ref` with the same four forms, `atomic_flag`, fences, `memory_order`, the free functions, the aliases and the `ATOMIC_*_LOCK_FREE` macros. The members the forms share are written once as macros (`FEATHEROS_ATOMIC_COMMON`, `_INTEGRAL`, `_FLOATING`, `_POINTER`), with a `MUTATING` argument that is `const` for `atomic_ref`. A type is lock-free when its size is 1, 2, 4, 8 or 16 bytes and `__atomic_always_lock_free` says so (16 only under Clang with `-mcx16`); `atomic` then aligns it to its size, and the operations in `std::__detail` move it as an unsigned integer of that size (`AtomicWord<T>`, a `may_alias` typedef). Any other type takes a spin lock chosen by its address from `__detail::AtomicLocks` (64 bytes in the header), because there is no libatomic. Padding is cleared before a value is stored (`__builtin_clear_padding`), and `AtomicCompareExchange` retries a failure caused by padding alone, so an `atomic_ref` to an object with uncleared padding compares values. Floating-point `fetch_add`/`fetch_sub` are compare-exchange loops; pointer arithmetic is scaled by the element size, since the builtins add bytes. `<chrono>` gained `time_point` (arithmetic, comparisons, `common_type`, `time_point_cast`, `floor`, `ceil`, `round`), `system_clock` with `to_time_t`/`from_time_t` (only where `<time.h>` exists, so not in the kernel), `sys_time`, `sys_seconds`, `sys_days`, `steady_clock`, `high_resolution_clock` (the steady clock) and `is_clock`; both clocks count nanoseconds, and their `now()` is in `featheros_cxx` (`src/libcxx/src/chrono.cpp`, over `clock_gettime`). The calendar, time zones and the other C++20 clocks are left out.
    - *Users:* the kernel's `Sync::SpinLock`, `smp.cpp` (online and halted CPU counts, IPI counts, the halt flag, Limine's `goto_address` through `atomic_ref`), the TLB shootdown's per-CPU active roots and flush requests in both architectures' `virtual_memory.cpp`, the status bar flag in `console.cpp`, the keyboard target, IPv4's identification counter, the link state, the framebuffer terminal's output count, the pseudo-terminals' output count and row flag, the virtqueue fence and the SMP kernel test's counters now use `std::atomic`. The C library's thread count and key-destructor hook do too. Kept on the builtins: variables written under a lock but read without it (`shown_console`, `output_watchers`), whose `GUARDED_BY` the thread-safety analysis would then apply to the lock-free reads; the signal state's `pending`, since `State` is copied and read through `const&` (`atomic_ref<const T>` is C++26); and the C library's futex words, which live inside C structures (`pthread_mutex_t`, `pthread_cond_t`, `pthread_once_t`) and the ABI's guard variables. The Swift module map lists `<atomic>`.
    - *Left out:* `wait`/`notify` (step 3), the `volatile` overloads, `fetch_max`/`fetch_min` (C++26), `atomic<shared_ptr>` and `atomic_ref<const T>`.
    - *Surprises:* Clang warns that an atomic operation on a `T*` aligned only to `alignof(T)` is misaligned, and compiles it to a libatomic call; an `aligned` typedef on a dependent type did not change that, so the operations go through same-size integers. `std::pair` is not trivially copyable (its assignment is user-provided), so it cannot be an `atomic_ref`'s type.
    - *Tests:* `tests/unit/libcxx/atomic_tests.cpp` (5 cases: integer, pointer, floating-point, `bool`, 8-byte struct, 3-byte locked struct and padded-struct operations; `atomic_ref` including one to an object with garbage padding; `atomic_flag` and `constinit` objects; the free functions; four host threads contending on `atomic<long>`, `atomic_ref<int>`, `atomic<double>`, a 24-byte locked struct through a compare-exchange loop and an `atomic_flag` spin lock) and two new `chrono_tests.cpp` parts (time point `static_assert`s; the clocks against `time()` and a `nanosleep`), with `chrono.cpp` in the test binary: 718 CTest cases in all, under ASan and UBSan. `cxxtest` gains 2 `static_assert`s and 6 run-time checks (`atomic<int>` operations, wrapping and pointer steps, a type that is not lock-free, four pthreads sharing atomics including a locked 24-byte one and an `atomic_flag` lock, `system_clock` against `time`, `steady_clock` over a 30 ms sleep); the shell smoke test expects `cxxtest: 152 checks passed`. `tools/check.sh --modes "SHELL SMP NETWORK INPUT" --arch both` passed (build, lint, unit tests and the four modes on x86-64 and AArch64).
    - *Step 2 done: `<thread>`, `<stop_token>`, `<mutex>`, `<shared_mutex>`, `<condition_variable>`.* For programs only, over the C library's pthreads; the non-template parts are in `featheros_cxx` (`src/libcxx/src/thread.cpp`, `mutex.cpp`, `condition_variable.cpp`, `stop_token.cpp`), and the internal header `__thread_support` holds what they share: timeouts turned into saturated steady-clock deadlines (`__detail::ClampedNanoseconds`, `SteadyDeadlineAfter`, `SteadyDeadline`), `__detail::Condition` (a pthread condition variable on `CLOCK_MONOTONIC`) and the thread-safety annotation macros. `thread` decay-copies the callable and its arguments into a heap `tuple` that the new thread runs and frees; `thread::id` wraps the `pthread_t` (zero for no thread) and compares and hashes it as a `size_t`. `jthread` holds a `stop_source` and a `thread`. The stop state (`__detail::StopState`) counts references and sources and keeps its callbacks on a doubly linked list under an `atomic_flag` spin lock; `request_stop` unlinks and runs them one at a time with the lock released, so a callback may destroy itself (it then only marks a flag on the requester's stack) or other callbacks, and a callback destroyed in another thread while it runs waits, yielding, until its `m_finished` is set. `mutex` is a `constexpr` pthread mutex with a trivial destructor and `recursive_mutex` a `PTHREAD_MUTEX_RECURSIVE` one. There is no `pthread_mutex_timedlock` or `pthread_rwlock`, so `timed_mutex` and `recursive_timed_mutex` are a flag (or owner and count) under a pthread mutex with a `Condition`, and `shared_mutex` and `shared_timed_mutex` share `__detail::SharedMutexBase`, libstdc++'s two-gate design: a writer sets a bit that keeps new readers out, then waits for the readers inside, and one that times out clears it and wakes the gate. `lock` and `try_lock` erase their arguments into function-pointer triples (`__detail::ErasedLockable`) so `TryLockAll` can walk them by a run-time index; `lock` waits on whichever lock was busy and then tries the others. `call_once` keeps an `atomic<int>` state in `once_flag`; the thread that wins the compare-exchange runs the callable with no lock held, and the others wait on one process-wide pthread mutex and condition variable. `condition_variable` waits on the steady clock; a deadline on any other clock, `system_clock` included, becomes the steady deadline as far away and is compared on its own clock after waking, as libc++ does without `pthread_cond_clockwait`. `condition_variable_any` waits under an internal `shared_ptr<mutex>` that `notify_*` takes first, so no notification falls between unlocking the caller's lock and waiting, and its `stop_token` waits register a `stop_callback` that notifies and check the request under that mutex. `notify_all_at_thread_exit` keeps a `thread_local` list whose destructor unlocks and notifies. The mutexes, `lock_guard` and single-mutex `scoped_lock` carry Clang's thread-safety annotations (as libc++ does under `_LIBCPP_ENABLE_THREAD_SAFETY_ANNOTATIONS`), so programs can name a `std::mutex` in `GUARDED_BY`; their definitions in `featheros_cxx` are `no_thread_safety_analysis`. What would throw `system_error` calls the abort hook. The Swift module map lists the five headers.
    - *Left out:* `operator<<` and `formatter` for `thread::id` (no iostreams; formatting is Part 5), thread attributes such as stack size, the C++26 `stoppable_token` concepts and `inplace_stop_token`, and waiting with `wait`/`notify` on atomics (step 3); a `stop_callback` destroyed while another thread runs it yields in a loop until then, and step 3's atomic waits can replace that.
    - *Surprises:* macOS, where the host tests run, has no `pthread_condattr_setclock`, so `Condition` waits for a relative time there (`pthread_cond_timedwait_relative_np`), only under `__APPLE__`. Catch2's assertions are not thread-safe, so the tests' worker threads record results that the main thread then checks.
    - *Tests:* `tests/unit/libcxx/thread_tests.cpp` (10 cases: stop sources, tokens and callbacks, including order, removal, late registration, a callback that destroys itself, removing one that another thread is running and registering while another thread requests stop; `thread` with move-only and reference arguments, ids and hashes, `detach`, sleeps; `jthread`'s stop and join on destruction and assignment; all four mutexes under contention, recursion, timeouts on `steady_clock` and `system_clock`, `unique_lock`'s forms; `try_lock`'s failed index, `lock` and `scoped_lock` from threads taking three mutexes in opposite orders; `call_once` from 8 threads and nested; `shared_mutex` readers, a waiting writer closing the gate, a timed-out writer reopening it, readers and a writer under contention; `condition_variable` as a queue, timeouts, `notify_all_at_thread_exit`; `condition_variable_any` with a `shared_lock`, a `timed_mutex` and stop requests), with the four sources in the test binary: 728 CTest cases in all, under ASan and UBSan, and the new cases passed 30 repeated runs. `cxxtest` gains 2 `constinit` objects, a `static_assert` and 9 run-time checks (a `thread` with a `unique_ptr` argument, `jthread` and `stop_callback`, `mutex`/`recursive_mutex`/`scoped_lock` under contention, a `timed_mutex` timeout, `call_once` from four threads, `shared_mutex`, `condition_variable` as a queue and its timeout, a stop request waking `condition_variable_any`, `sleep_for`/`sleep_until`); the shell smoke test expects `cxxtest: 161 checks passed`. `tools/check.sh --modes SHELL --arch both` passed (build, lint, unit tests and the shell smoke test on x86-64 and AArch64, the Swift programs building over the module map with the five headers); the full matrix waits for the end of Part 4.
    - *Step 3 done: waiting on atomics, `<semaphore>`, `<latch>`, `<barrier>`.* `atomic` and `atomic_ref` (all four forms, through `FEATHEROS_ATOMIC_COMMON`) gained `wait`, `notify_one` and `notify_all`, `atomic_flag` its `wait` and notifications, with the free functions `atomic_wait`, `atomic_wait_explicit`, `atomic_notify_one`/`_all` and the `atomic_flag_*` ones. `__detail::AtomicWait` compares the loaded value with the old one by their bits after clearing both paddings, as `AtomicCompareExchange` does. `__detail::AtomicWaitUntil(address, poll, deadline)` polls 64 times with a pause, then calls `AtomicWaitSlow` in `featheros_cxx` (`src/libcxx/src/atomic_wait.cpp`) with the poll erased to a function pointer and a deadline in steady-clock nanoseconds (`AtomicWaitForever`). There, 64 cache-line slots chosen by a hash of the address each hold a futex word that counts notifications and a count of waiters: a waiter registers, reads the word, polls, and sleeps in `FeatherOS::LibC::FutexWait` only while the word is unchanged (for a timed wait, at most the milliseconds left, rounded up); `AtomicNotify` increments the word and calls `FutexWake` for all its waiters only when there are any. All four accesses are sequentially consistent, so either the notifier sees the waiter or the waiter sees the new count and with it the stored value. Because a word stands for many addresses, `notify_one` wakes them all, and every type waits the same way, locked ones included; no waiting on the atomic's own address, as libc++ does for 4-byte types on Linux, was needed. `counting_semaphore` is an `atomic<ptrdiff_t>` whose `acquire` waits with `try_acquire` (a compare-exchange loop) as the poll, and whose timed acquires use `__thread_support`'s steady deadlines, checking another clock again after waking. `latch` counts down with release and waits for zero with acquire. `barrier` (`src/libcxx/include/barrier`) is one counter that the phase's last arrival resets after running the completion function, subtracting the threads that called `arrive_and_drop` from the expected count; it then stores the phase number (`arrival_token` holds the phase it was issued in) with release and notifies, and `wait` waits for the number to change. The three are `constexpr`-constructible, so `constinit` works. The C library got no public futex call; `featheros_cxx` calls its internal `FutexWait` and `FutexWake` (`src/libc/lock.h`), and the host tests define those two over one mutex and condition variable (`tests/unit/libcxx/host_futex.cpp`). The Swift module map lists the three headers.
    - *Tests:* `tests/unit/libcxx/wait_tests.cpp` (7 cases: waits that return at once for a changed value, including `-0.0` against `0.0`, a locked 3-byte type, `atomic_ref`, `atomic_flag` and a pointer; waits that block until a notified change for `int`, `char`, the locked type, a padded struct with different padding bytes in the stored and the old value, `atomic_flag`, and a notification without a change; two threads taking 5000 turns through one word and 8 waiters on neighbouring words; semaphores with `release(n)`, timeouts on `steady_clock` and `system_clock`, a deadline in the past, `hours::max()`, four consumers of 4000 items, a binary semaphore as a lock and a `constinit` one; latches with `arrive_and_wait` and a `constinit` one; a barrier of 5 threads over 400 phases whose completion checks every thread's write for the phase, mixing `arrive_and_wait` and `wait(arrive())`, and `arrive_and_drop` shrinking a barrier phase by phase), with `atomic_wait.cpp` in the test binary: 735 CTest cases in all, under ASan and UBSan, and the new cases passed 20 repeated runs. `cxxtest` gains a `constinit` semaphore, a `static_assert` and 5 run-time checks (two threads taking 1000 turns with `wait`/`notify_one` and an `atomic_flag` wait, four consumers on a semaphore and its timeout, latches starting and collecting workers, a barrier's completion once per phase over 100 phases, `arrive_and_drop`); the shell smoke test expects `cxxtest: 166 checks passed`. `tools/check.sh --modes SHELL --arch both` passed (build, lint, unit tests and the shell smoke test on x86-64 and AArch64, the Swift programs building over the module map with the three headers), and `tools/check.sh --matrix` passed to close Part 4 (38 cases in 212 s, both targets, Debug and Release, `cxxtest` in all six shell cases).
- **Part 5: formatting (done).** `<format>` and `<print>` with `std::formatter` for user types, over the C library's float engine (`src/libc/float_format.h`) and `<charconv>`, and `<chrono>`'s formatters over a calendar of its own. Done in three steps, as planned: (1) `<format>` for the standard's scalar and string types and user formatters, checked at compile time (`format_string`, the standard format spec with fill, alignment, sign, `#`, `0`, width and precision from the string or from arguments, `format`, `format_to`, `format_to_n`, `formatted_size`, `vformat`, `vformat_to`, `make_format_args`, `visit_format_arg`, `formattable`), and `<print>` (`print`, `println`, `vprint_unicode`, `vprint_nonunicode`) to `stdout` or a `FILE*`; output goes through one type-erased buffer, so `format_context` has one iterator type and the formatting loop is compiled once into `featheros_cxx`; (2) C++23's ranges, tuples and debug strings: `range_formatter`, `format_kind`, `range_format`, formatters for ranges, maps, sets, `pair`, `tuple` and `vector<bool>::reference`, the `?` type with escaping, and `formatter<thread::id>`; (3) `<chrono>` formatting of durations, `sys_time` and `hh_mm_ss` with the calendar types they need, and programs moving from `printf` to `std::print` where it reads better.
    - *Step 1 done: `<format>` and `<print>`.* `src/libcxx/include/format` holds what is checked or chosen at compile time: `basic_format_parse_context`, `basic_format_arg` (a union over `bool`, `char`, `int`, `unsigned`, `long long`, `unsigned long long`, the three floating-point types, `const char*`, `string_view`, `const void*` and a `handle` that erases a user type's `formatter`) and `basic_format_args` over an array of them, `make_format_args`, `visit_format_arg`, `basic_format_string`, `formattable`, and the formatters. `__detail::ParseFormatSpec` parses the standard spec (`constexpr`) into a `__detail::FormatSpec`, which keeps a width or precision argument's index; each formatter then checks the spec against its types and options (`CheckFormatSpec`), as libc++ does: sign, `#` and `0` only for numbers and integer presentations of `char` and `bool`, a precision only for strings and floating point, `L` not for strings and pointers. `__detail::ScanFormatString` walks a format string with a handler, used at compile time by `FormatChecker` (each argument's formatter parses its field; the parse context carries the argument kinds, so `{:{}}` with a non-integer width argument fails) and at run time by `FormatRunner` in `featheros_cxx`. Output goes through `__detail::FormatBuffer` (a buffer and a drain function), so `format_context` is `basic_format_context<__detail::FormatIterator, char>` whatever the destination: `format` drains into a `string`, `format_to` into any output iterator (or, given `ctx.out()`, straight into the caller's buffer), `format_to_n` keeps the first n characters and counts the rest, `formatted_size` only counts. The run-time half, compiled once (`src/libcxx/src/format.cpp`): the formatting loop `__detail::VFormat`, the dynamic width and precision, padding with a UTF-8 fill, zero padding after the sign and base prefix, integers through `to_chars` (a `c` presentation checks the value fits `char`, which is signed on x86-64 and unsigned on AArch64), and floating point: the shortest `to_chars` text without type or precision (a `#` then adds the point), otherwise the C library's `FormatFloat` with the type's conversion and the `#` flag, the sign taken from the decoded bits so that `long double` needs no arithmetic on AArch64. String widths follow C++20's list of wide ranges, with combining marks after another code point counting zero, instead of Unicode's grapheme and width tables. `<print>` (`src/libcxx/src/print.cpp`) formats straight into the `FILE` through a 512-byte buffer under `flockfile`; `println` adds the newline inside the same lock. Format errors call `LibraryAbort` through the `constexpr` `__detail::FormatError`, so in a checked format string they stop compilation.
    - *Left out:* `format_error` (no `<stdexcept>`), wide characters, `runtime_format` and the member `visit` (C++26), `__int128`, and steps 2 and 3's ranges, tuples, `?`, `thread::id` and `<chrono>`.
    - *Surprises:* libc++ prints `{:#g}` of 0.1 as `0.10000`, one zero short of `printf`'s `%#g` (`0.100000`, which libstdc++ and we print), and writes subnormals in hex unnormalized (`0.0000000000001p-1022`, where our `to_chars` and `{:a}` give `1p-1074`); the host tests skip both comparisons and pin our forms. A width of `0` after the `0` flag (`{:00}`) is an error, not width zero.
    - *Tests:* `tests/unit/libcxx/format_tests.cpp` (9 cases, compared with the host's `std::format`: `static_assert`s on `formattable` and on 13 patterns the compile-time check accepts and 21 it rejects (unmatched braces, mixed indexing, missing arguments, specs that do not suit the type, a `bool` or `double` width argument, an invalid UTF-8 fill, an oversized width); escapes and indices; a user formatter that inherits `formatter<int>` and parses a flag of its own; 23 integer patterns over 12 values as `int`, `long long` and `unsigned long long`; `bool`, `char` and pointers; strings with widths, precisions, CJK, combining marks, an emoji fill and invalid UTF-8; 39 floating-point patterns over 21 values as `double` and `float`; 3000 random doubles; `format_to` into a pointer and a `back_inserter`, `format_to_n` with a short and a negative limit, `formatted_size`, a 1004-character result; the argument kinds `visit_format_arg` sees; `print` and `println` to a `tmpfile`), with `format.cpp` and `print.cpp` in the test binary: 744 CTest cases in all, under ASan and UBSan. `cxxtest` gains a user formatter, a `static_assert` and 9 run-time checks (integers, `double` and `float`, `long double` on both targets, strings with wide columns, `bool`, `char` and pointers, indices, escapes and a width argument, the user type, `format_to`/`format_to_n`/`formatted_size`, `print` and `println` into a file under `/tmp`); the shell smoke test expects `cxxtest: 175 checks passed`. The Swift module map lists `<format>` and `<print>`. `tools/check.sh --modes SHELL --arch both` passed (build, lint, unit tests and the shell smoke test on x86-64 and AArch64, the Swift programs building over the module map with the two headers); the full matrix waits for the end of Part 5.
    - *Step 2 done: ranges, tuples, debug strings and `thread::id`.* `<format>` gained `range_format` and `format_kind` (`map` for a range with `key_type` and `mapped_type` whose elements are pairs or two-element tuples, `set` for one with only `key_type`, `disabled` for a range whose elements are itself, otherwise `sequence`), `range_formatter` (`set_separator`, `set_brackets`, `underlying`, and the range spec `[[fill]align][width][n][m|s|?s][:element spec]`), the default formatter for ranges (`__detail::RangeDefaultFormatter`: one partial specialization for `sequence`, `map` and `set`, whose constructor sets the brackets and, for a map, the elements' `": "`, and one for `string` and `debug_string`, which a program selects by specializing `format_kind`), the formatters for `pair` and `tuple` (`__detail::TupleFormatter`, with `m` and `n`), and the `?` type with `set_debug_format()` for `char` and the string formatters. Range and tuple formatters turn their elements' debug format on (a range only without an element spec), so `vector<string>` prints `["a"]`. `ParseFormatSpec` was split into `ParseFormatFillAndAlign` and `ParseFormatWidth`, which `__detail::ParseCompositeSpec` shares for range and tuple specs. A range, pair or tuple is written through `__detail::FormatPadded` in `featheros_cxx`: the template passes a function pointer and a pointer to its state; without a width it runs straight into the caller's buffer, with one into a string that is then padded by its columns as text is (left by default), through a `format_context` over the same arguments, so the elements' `{}` widths still resolve. The escaping (`WriteEscaped` in `src/libcxx/src/format.cpp`) follows [format.string.escaped]: `\t`, `\n`, `\r`, `\\` and the quote of its kind (`"` in a string, `'` in a character) get short escapes, a code point in `EscapedRanges` becomes `\u{hex}`, as does a Grapheme_Extend one (`ExtendingRanges`) at the start or after an escape, and each byte of invalid UTF-8 becomes `\x{hex}`; a width or precision then applies to the escaped text. `ExtendingRanges`, which string widths also use, grew from the Latin combining marks to those of Cyrillic, Hebrew, Arabic, Devanagari, Thai and kana and ZWNJ. `formatter<vector<bool>::reference>` is in `<format>`, a partial specialization over a forward declaration of `__detail::BitReference`, so `<vector>` does not include `<format>`. `formatter<thread::id>` is in `<thread>`, which now includes `<format>` (both are for programs only); it takes fill, alignment and width, and writes the `pthread_t`, the thread's number, through the integer formatter (`0` for no thread). No header was added, so the Swift module map is unchanged.
    - *Left out:* formatters for the container adaptors (there are none yet), `formatter<filesystem::path>` and C++26's `enable_nonlocking_formatter_optimization`; only `char`. Escaping uses two small tables instead of Unicode's General_Category and Grapheme_Extend: Cc, Cf, Cs, Co, Zs, Zl and Zp are escaped, but of the unassigned code points only the noncharacters, U+FFF0..U+FFF8 and the unassigned planes, so U+0378 is written as it is where libc++ writes `\u{378}`; only the combining marks of the scripts above count as Grapheme_Extend.
    - *Surprises:* the C++23 text gives the default range formatter a `range_formatter` of the non-`const` range's element type, which then cannot take a `const vector<bool>&` (its elements are `bool`, not the proxy); the element type comes from the `const` range when that is formattable, as libc++ does. A range's or tuple's fill cannot be `:`, which starts the element spec (`{:::<4}` pads each element of a nested range). With the small tables, all 256 `char`s and every test string escape as libc++ escapes them.
    - *Tests:* `tests/unit/libcxx/format_range_tests.cpp` (6 cases, compared with the host's `std::format`: `static_assert`s on `format_kind` for 9 containers, on `formattable`, and on 15 patterns the compile-time check accepts and 16 it rejects (`s`, `?s` or `m` on the wrong element type, a bare `?` on a range, `n` with `s`, an element spec after `s`, `m` on a three-element tuple, a sign, precision or `0` on a range, `x` on a `thread::id`, a `double` width argument); sequences (vectors, deques, arrays, nested vectors, strings, `const char*`, `char` with `s` and `?s`, `double`, `vector<bool>` and its reference) under up to 12 patterns with fills, widths and element specs, and widths from arguments; maps, multimaps, unordered maps and sets, and vectors of pairs and tuples with `m`; pairs and tuples, nested, empty and of references; 19 debug strings (short escapes, controls, separators, format characters, combining marks at the start and after a letter or an escape, Hebrew, CJK, emoji, truncated and invalid UTF-8, encoded surrogates, private use, noncharacters, tags) as `const char*`, `string` and `string_view` with widths and precisions, and all 256 `char`s; a user formatter over `range_formatter` with its own brackets and separator, `format_kind` specialized to `string` and `debug_string`, a range iterable only when not `const`, a user element formatter; `thread::id`), 750 CTest cases in all, under ASan and UBSan. `cxxtest` gains a user formatter over `range_formatter`, a `static_assert` and 7 run-time checks (sequences, maps and sets, pairs and tuples, debug strings and characters, character ranges and `vector<bool>`, `thread::id`, the user formatter); the shell smoke test expects `cxxtest: 182 checks passed`. `tools/check.sh --modes SHELL --arch both` passed (build, lint, unit tests and the shell smoke test on x86-64 and AArch64, the Swift programs building over the module map); the full matrix waits for the end of Part 5.
    - *Step 3 done: `<chrono>` formatting and the calendar.* `<chrono>` gained the civil calendar's `day`, `month`, `year`, `weekday`, `year_month`, `month_day` and `year_month_day`, all `constexpr`, with their arithmetic, comparisons, `ok()`, `is_leap`, the month and weekday constants, the `/` forms (`2024y / March / 9`, `March / 9 / 2024`, `9d / March / 2024`) and the `d` and `y` literals; `year_month_day` converts to and from `sys_days` through Howard Hinnant's algorithms (`__detail::DaysFromCivil`, `CivilFromDays`), `weekday` from `sys_days`. `hh_mm_ss` has `fractional_width` (the fewest digits, up to 18, that show the period exactly, else 6), `precision`, `is_am`, `is_pm`, `make12` and `make24`. None of this needs a C library, so `<chrono>` stays usable by the kernel. The formatters are in a new internal header, `__chrono_format`, which `<chrono>` includes at its end only where `<time.h>` exists, so it brings in `<format>` for programs only (no header was added to the Swift module map): `formatter` for `duration` (integer and floating-point counts), `sys_time`, `hh_mm_ss`, `day`, `month`, `year`, `weekday` and `year_month_day`, all on `__detail::ChronoFormatter<Kind>`. `__detail::ParseChronoSpec` parses `[[fill]align][width][.precision][L][chrono-specs]` and checks each conversion against the fields the type has (`ChronoFieldsOf`, `ChronoConversionFields`), so in a checked format string `{:%Y}` of a duration, `{:%H}` of a date, a precision on an integer duration or a time point, an unknown conversion, an `E` or `O` modifier on a conversion that takes none, or text before the first conversion fail to compile. `format` breaks the value down into `__detail::ChronoParts` (the sign apart; hours, minutes, seconds and the fraction as magnitudes; the date's fields; a duration's count as a `basic_format_arg` and its period) and calls `__detail::FormatChronoValue` in `featheros_cxx` (`src/libcxx/src/chrono_format.cpp`), which runs every conversion of [time.format] for the "C" locale and UTC (`%E`, `%O` and `L` change nothing, `%z` is `+0000` or `+00:00` with a modifier, `%Z` is `UTC`) and pads through `FormatPadded`, left-aligned by default. A negative duration or `hh_mm_ss` gets its `-` before the first conversion and `%Q` shows the count's magnitude; a duration's `%H` counts the hours within a day and its `%j` the days, as libc++ does. A weekday or month name of an invalid value, a weekday or day or week of the year of an invalid date, and the hour conversions of an `hh_mm_ss` of a day or more call the abort hook. Without chrono-specs, values print as the standard's `operator<<` does (`42s`, `5[2/3]s`, `2024-02-29 13:05:09.007`, a time point of whole days as its date, `-13:05:01.250`, `13 is not a valid month`, `2023-02-29 is not a valid date`), and a precision formats a floating-point count in fixed notation. `hh_mm_ss` of a duration too fine to hold an hour (attoseconds) does not compile, since an hour in its period overflows `intmax_t`, so such durations are broken down through seconds. `<format>` gained `__detail::ParseFormatPrecision`, which the chrono parser shares. Programs moved to `std::print`: `date` prints `{:%F %T %Z}` of a `sys_seconds`, `uptime` `{}:{:%M:%S}` of its seconds, and `threads` its lines through `std::print`/`std::println`, timing with `steady_clock`; their output is unchanged, and each grew by about 90 KB stripped (Debug) with the formatting code, which the other small programs were left without.
    - *Left out:* time zones (`zoned_time`, `local_time`, the time zone database) and the other clocks, so `%Z` is always `UTC`; the rest of the calendar (`last`, `weekday_indexed`, `weekday_last`, `month_day_last`, `month_weekday`, `year_month_day_last`, `year_month_weekday`) and formatters for `year_month` and `month_day`; `chrono::parse` and stream output; locales other than "C"; durations whose count is not an arithmetic type, and on AArch64 `long double` counts beyond the default output (their hours and seconds need `long double` arithmetic).
    - *Surprises:* the host's libc++ (LLVM 23) departs from the standard in several places, which the host tests pin instead of comparing: it aligns chrono values right by default where non-arithmetic types align left (`{:6}` of `5s` is `5s    ` here); it ignores a precision and then drops the unit (`{:.3}` of 1.5 s is `1.5` there, `1.500s` here); it rounds a floating-point fraction of a second where `hh_mm_ss` truncates (1.9 ms as `double` under `%S` is `00.002` there, `00.001` here); it keeps a duration's `%j` in an `int`; and its `%EY`, `%EC` and `%G` pad a negative year to four characters including the sign (`-001` for ISO year -1, `-0001` here as for `%Y`), `%EC` dividing toward zero. An `unsigned char` count prints as a number, where libc++ streams it as a character. The standard's chrono-specs must start with a conversion (`{:x%H}` is an error), which keeps a fill from being read as text.
    - *Tests:* `tests/unit/libcxx/chrono_format_tests.cpp` (5 cases, compared with the host's `std::format` and `<chrono>`: `static_assert`s on `formattable` for 10 types and against 2, on 15 patterns the compile-time check accepts and 20 it rejects, and on the calendar's arithmetic, conversions, `/` forms and `hh_mm_ss` fields in constant evaluation; `year_month_day` and `weekday` from every 997th day between years -32767 and 32767 and back, and month arithmetic; 10 counts as durations of 10 periods and representations (seconds to days, thirds, 1/1024, 2/3, `unsigned`) under 16 patterns, every named period, floating-point counts; the pinned divergences, fills and widths from arguments; 400 random `sys_seconds`, `sys_time<milliseconds>` and `sys_days` from year 6 to 9999 under 12 patterns that use every conversion and modifier, ISO weeks around 33 year ends, nanosecond, minute, hour and third-of-a-second time points; days, months, years, weekdays and dates, valid and not, and 11 `hh_mm_ss` values), with `chrono_format.cpp` in the test binary: 755 CTest cases in all, under ASan and UBSan. `cxxtest` gains a `static_assert` and 8 run-time checks (durations, floating-point durations and odd periods, time points, calendar arithmetic, calendar types, `hh_mm_ss`, fills and widths, `system_clock::now()` against `strftime`); the shell smoke test expects `cxxtest: 190 checks passed`. `tools/check.sh --modes SHELL --arch both` passed (build, lint, unit tests and the shell smoke test on x86-64 and AArch64, with `date`, `uptime` and `threads`, and the Swift programs building over the module map, whose `<chrono>` now brings in `<format>`). The full matrix passed (38 cases on both architectures).
- **Part 6: the Swift runtimes over our headers (done).** The spike worked for both runtimes, not only the concurrency one, so `deps/llvm-project` is no longer fetched (`build.sh` lost `LIBCXX_TAG` and the clone; `fullclean` still removes an old one). `src/user/swift_concurrency/CMakeLists.txt` and `src/user/swift_runtime/CMakeLists.txt` compile upstream's C++ with `-std=c++20` (upstream uses C++17, our headers need C++20) and `-isystem src/libcxx/include`; `swift_concurrency/libcxx/__config_site` and `swift_runtime/platform.cpp` (libc++'s `basic_string<char>` instantiation, `__next_prime`, `__hash_memory`, `to_string`, `hardware_concurrency`) are gone, since `featheros_cxx`, already in each program's link, has what the runtimes call, and the C library no longer defines `std::__libcpp_verbose_abort`. Compiling the runtimes against the headers (a scratch script swapping the include path in each object's command) showed what was missing:
    - *New headers:* `<bitset>` (64-bit words, `constexpr`, `to_string`, parsing, `reference`, `hash`; `count` through `popcount`, since GCC's builtin calls libgcc's `__popcountdi2` on x86-64), `<queue>` (`queue`, `priority_queue`) and `<stack>` with the C++23 interface over the shared `__container_adaptor` (allocator concept, `AdaptorAppendRange`, `AdaptorFromRange`), `<cmath>` (wrappers over the C library's `math.h`, `float` and x86-64 `long double` overloads, promotion of integer and mixed arguments, three-argument `hypot`, `lerp`, the classification macros as `constexpr` functions, also put in the global namespace) and `<exception>` (`exception`, `bad_exception`, the terminate handler in `featheros_cxx`'s `exception.cpp`). `<type_traits>` gained `aligned_storage`, `aligned_union` and `is_pod`, which C++23 deprecates but the runtimes use. The Swift module map lists the five headers.
    - *Changed:* `<atomic>` clears padding only where `__builtin_clear_padding` exists (GCC); under Clang, as in libc++, a compare-exchange is sure to match only an expected value loaded from the atomic. `queue`'s and `stack`'s `<=>` is deduced, because a task group's `queue<ReadyQueueItem, deque<...>>` must build although its elements cannot be compared. `swift.patch` gained two hunks: `#include <stdio.h>` in `TaskAlloc.cpp`, which got `puts` through libc++'s headers, and the removal of `MetadataLookup.cpp`'s unused `#include <list>`. `Exception.cpp` includes `<exception>` but uses it only with exceptions on.
    - *Left out:* `<list>` and `<forward_list>` (the runtime only included `<list>`), `exception_ptr` and `nested_exception`, the `<cmath>` functions the C library lacks and the special math functions, `bitset`'s stream operators.
    - *Tests:* `tests/unit/libcxx/bitset_adaptor_tests.cpp` (bitsets of 0 to 200 bits under random shifts, logic and flips against the host's `std::bitset`, constant evaluation, parsing, conversions and hashes; `queue` and `stack` against the host's under pushes and pops, from ranges and iterators, comparisons, `swap`, deduction guides and an adaptor of incomparable elements; `priority_queue` against the host's over 500 random operations, with `greater<>`, `from_range` and `push_range`), `cmath_exception_tests.cpp` (overload types and promotion; 12 values, zeros, subnormals, infinities and NaN through every wrapper and classification function against the host's `<cmath>`; `hypot` and `lerp`'s exactness and monotony; `exception` and the terminate handler), and `static_assert`s on `is_pod`, `aligned_storage` and `aligned_union` in `type_traits_tests.cpp`: 763 CTest cases in all, under ASan and UBSan. `cxxtest` gains a `static_assert` and 5 run-time checks (`bitset`, `queue` and `stack`, `priority_queue`, `cmath`, `exception`); the shell smoke test expects `cxxtest: 195 checks passed`. `tools/check.sh --modes SHELL --arch both` passed with `deps/llvm-project` moved away, and the full matrix passed (38 cases on both architectures), including every Swift program in Debug and Release.

**Complete when:**
- C++ programs, the kernel where it fits, and Swift's C++ programs use the same headers on both targets. Done; the Swift runtimes too (Part 6).
- Each part's headers have host tests, and a QEMU program checks them as `std` on both targets. Done: `tests/unit/libcxx/` (763 CTest cases in all) and `cxxtest` (195 checks) in the shell smoke test.
- `docs/development.md` lists what the library has and leaves out, as `docs/posix.md` does for the C library. Done ("C++ Standard Library").

### 27. Full Swift With Runtime Type Information (Complete)

**Summary:** FeatherOS programs can now be written in full Swift as well as Embedded Swift. `src/user/swift_runtime/` builds upstream's standard library, runtime, `_Concurrency`, `Cxx` overlay and regular-expression modules from source for both targets, the program loader handles programs of any size, and Release programs are sealed at an `ld.lld` link. Full-Swift programs have what Embedded Swift leaves out: protocol casts, `Mirror`, key paths, `weak` references, upstream's `Regex` and `Codable`. `FeatherSupport` builds in both modes, so programs move between them unchanged. A full program costs 2.4 to 3.1 MB in Release against Embedded Swift's 0.15 to 0.4 MB, at about the same speed; `docs/development.md` ("Embedded or full Swift") says when to choose which.

Embedded Swift (M21, M25) is a compiler mode that leaves out runtime type information: the records of protocol conformances, field layouts and type descriptors, and the runtime that reads them for protocol casts, generic and tuple types built while a program runs, `Mirror` and key paths. A library cannot add them back, since the compiler never writes the records, and a patched swiftc is not worth maintaining. This milestone builds Swift's full standard library and runtime for FeatherOS instead, so programs compiled without `-enable-experimental-feature Embedded` run with everything M25 left out of scope.

**Probe results that shape the plan:**
- The stock swiftc 6.4.2 compiles full Swift for `x86_64-unknown-none-elf` and `aarch64-none-none-elf` and writes the record sections (`swift5_protocol_conformances`, `swift5_fieldmd`, `swift5_type_metadata`, `swift5_typeref` and the rest), so the compiler needs no change.
- Upstream already builds the full library for systems without an OS: the "minimal" stdlib preset (`mixin_stdlib_minimal` in `utils/build-presets.ini`, used for freestanding Mach-O) turns off `dladdr`, `dlsym`, the environment, locale, stdin and type printing, uses pthreads, a static library only, and finds the record sections through linker-defined bounds (`swift-runtime-static-image-inspection`) instead of a dynamic loader. Its settings are the template here, on ELF instead of Mach-O.
- Embedded and full Swift modules cannot be linked into one program, so the two kinds of program live side by side; Embedded programs stay as they are.

**Design:**
- Full-Swift programs go in a list of their own next to `FEATHEROS_SWIFT_PROGRAMS`, against a `Swift` module, runtime and `FeatherSupport` built in full mode.
- The runtime (`stdlib/public/runtime`, `lib/Demangling`, the stubs) is built from the `deps/swift` sources by the toolchain's `clang++`, as `_Concurrency` is, with the preset's settings as compile definitions.
- The user linker script keeps every `swift5_*` section through `--gc-sections` and gives the runtime their bounds.

**Out of scope:** Foundation (swift-corelibs-foundation needs far more of an OS), shared libraries and a dynamic linker, compiling Swift on FeatherOS.

- **Part 1: the program loader (done).** `Spawn` read the whole program file into one kernel heap buffer, which capped programs at 1 MiB, and a process had 1024 pages (4 MiB) for code, data, heap and stacks. Now `Files::ProgramFile` holds the file open as a reader (registered like an open descriptor, so it cannot be written, replaced or removed during the load; `Spawn` of a file open for writing fails with `SyscallErrorBusy`) and reads at any offset. The ELF loader reads through an `ELF::Source`: the header and program headers one at a time, then each segment and the relocation and dynamic tables through a 64 KiB kernel buffer per load, so only a program's segments count, against its page limit, and a file's size does not matter. `Image` keeps up to eight parsed load segments instead of a pointer into the file; the kernel's own test program reads from the initramfs through `ELF::MemoryFile`. Segment bytes and relocations are written through the new pages' physical addresses rather than their user addresses, since a read from the disk or under the filesystem lock may block, and the task then resumes with its own address space active, not the new program's (the first try did, and faulted on a pipeline). Each process now has 16384 pages (64 MiB); Part 2 measures whether full-Swift programs need more. `MaximumProgramSize` is gone, and the kernel copies and zeroes segments with `memcpy`/`memset`. Host tests cover loading through a 64-byte buffer, the segment cap, a source that fails or shrinks, and `ProgramFile`'s reads and its hold on the file; `hardening` (69 checks) spawns a program whose 1 GiB segment exceeds the page limit (`SyscallErrorNoMemory`), one open for writing, and a copy of `sleep` padded past 1 MiB, which must run; `libctest`'s failing `malloc` asks for 128 MiB, and `memtest exhaust` reaches about 16,350 heap pages, on both targets.
- **Part 2: probes (done).** Run outside the build, from a sparse clone of the same `swift-6.4.2-RELEASE` sources with `lib/Demangling`, `stdlib/public/{stubs,LLVMSupport,SwiftOnoneSupport}` and `Runtimes/Core` added (upstream's newer CMake build, whose lists name each library's files). Results:
  - *The runtime needs no compiled libc++.* Its C++ (the 50 files of `stdlib/public/runtime`, `SwiftObject.mm` and `ErrorObject.mm` compiled as C++, `SwiftRT-ELF-WASM.cpp`, the stubs, `LLVMSupport`, `lib/Demangling`, `lib/Threading/Pthreads.cpp`) compiles with `_Concurrency`'s flags in full mode (reflection, type printing, stdin and the environment on; `dladdr`, `dlsym`, backtracing and filesystem paths off), given a `cxxabi.h` declaration (included, `__cxa_demangle` never called) and `swift.patch`'s `Pthreads.cpp` hunk. It uses 15 symbols from libc++'s compiled library: `basic_string<char>`'s extern-template members (the demangler's printer and `MetadataLookup.cpp`), `__next_prime` and `__hash_memory` (an `unordered_map` in `Metadata.cpp`), `to_string` for three integer types and `thread::hardware_concurrency`. A 25-line file resolves them all: it instantiates `basic_string<char>` from libc++'s headers with `_LIBCPP_STRING_V1_EXTERN_TEMPLATE_LIST`, as libc++'s `string.cpp` does, and defines the other five. So the runtime does not wait for M26.
  - *Section registration needs nothing new.* Upstream's static image inspection is Mach-O only. On ELF, `SwiftRT-ELF-WASM.cpp`'s constructor registers the `swift5_*` sections through the linker's `__start_`/`__stop_` symbols, which works in our static PIE with no loader and the current linker script (GNU ld and lld both keep the sections and place them after `.rodata`).
  - *What the C library lacks.* The runtime's other 55 calls (stdio, the `malloc` family, `posix_memalign`, pthread mutexes, condition variables and keys, `strtold`, `getline`, `sysconf`, `getenv`, `__cxa_guard_*`) are all there. The `Swift` module's object needs `remainder`/`remainderf` on both targets and, on x86-64 only, `rint`/`rintf`/`nearbyint`/`nearbyintf` and 17 `long double` functions for `Float80` (`ceill`, `floorl`, `truncl`, `roundl`, `rintl`, `nearbyintl`, `fmodl`, `remainderl`, `fmal`, `sqrtl`, `sinl`, `cosl`, `expl`, `exp2l`, `logl`, `log2l`, `log10l`), plus libgcc's 128-bit division. `malloc_usable_size` is needed too: `ManagedBuffer.capacity` calls `_swift_stdlib_malloc_size` unconditionally, and `LibcShims.h` returns 0 outside the systems it lists, so it needs a `__featheros__` case over `HeapAllocator::UsableSize`.
  - *Patches.* The full `Swift` module (upstream's whole list, 254 files, about 70 s at `-O`) builds for both targets once `CTypes.swift` defines `CLongDouble = Float80` for `os(none)` on x86-64; without it, no `long double` shim imports and `Float80` fails to compile. `Random.cpp` must take its `arc4random_buf` branch, since it otherwise opens `/dev/urandom` for the hashing seed and every program stops with `Fatal error: 2 in 'swift_stdlib_random'`. `-Onone` programs import `SwiftOnoneSupport`, built from its one file.
  - *It runs.* A `hello` and a program with protocol casts, `as? any P`, `Mirror`, a writable key path, `type(of:)`, `String(describing:)`, `dump` and `weak` print the right output on both targets in QEMU. One AArch64 link failed: GNU ld left an `R_AARCH64_NONE` relocation, which the loader rejects because it accepts only `RELATIVE`, and `Spawn` reports the failure as "Out of memory". lld does not emit one.
  - *Sizes,* stripped, x86-64 / AArch64: `-Onone` 5.06 / 4.70 MB, `-O` 4.94 / 4.59 MB, `-O` with `-experimental-hermetic-seal-at-link` and `--gc-sections` 2.39 / 2.18 MB (Embedded `hello` at `-Onone`: 0.88 / 0.84 MB). Without the seal, the `swift5_*` records reference every type's metadata and witness tables, so `--gc-sections` saves 2%, with `-function-sections` too. The seal compiles the module and program to bitcode (`-lto=llvm-full`), which GNU ld cannot read, so it links with the toolchain's `ld.lld`.
